## Grade 3 — Teacher's Guide My English Book - 3 (teacher's guide)

### Identity block
- Title as printed: Cover: "TEACHER'S GUIDE / MY ENGLISH BOOK / Grade 3" (purple cover, scanned from a worn physical copy; library sticker "427 CDC-e 2008 c.6" pasted on it). Title page: "TEACHER'S GUIDE / My English Book - 3", Government of Nepal, Ministry of Education, Curriculum Development Centre, Sanothimi, Bhaktapur — with the scanned copy's handwritten marks "420 / CDC-8 / 2008" (a previous owner's/library handwriting, not print). The record's "Teacher's Guide My English Book Grade 3" is accurate.
- Publisher: Government of Nepal, Ministry of Education, Curriculum Development Centre, Sanothimi, Bhaktapur (imprint page: "© CDC"; comments to Editing & Publishing Section CDC, Email: cdc@ntc.net.np). Printed at: **Bagmati Offset Press, Minbhawan, Kathmandu** (Tel.: 4783420).
- Year/edition/ISBN as printed: **"First edition: 2008"** (imprint page). "Price:" is printed with NO amount filled in. **No ISBN is printed anywhere in the book** — unlike the companion student's book, which carries one.
- Developers/authors as printed: The Preface names the writers: **Mr. Bishnu Prasad Parajuli, Mr. Madhu Upadhaya, Mr. Bharat Babu Khanal and Mr Arjun Lamichhane**; the Executive Director Mr. Hari Bole Khanal and Deputy Director Mr. Drona Dahal "have also significant contribution"; typing and layout by Mr. Anil Kumar Thapa. (Note: Bishnu Prasad Parajuli is also a co-author of the student's book; the other guide writers differ from the student's-book team.)
- Curriculum/series notes: Written for the "new primary curriculum-2059" (Preface), under which English began at grade one nationwide from school year 2060 BS; the approach is "functional or communicative", oral-skills first. Front matter: cover, title page, imprint, Preface, a "Class room language" page (15 teacher phrases and 15 student phrases: "May I come in, please?", "I make my apologies for coming late.", "Can I sharpen my pencil, please?" etc.), Contents. Each unit section follows one fixed pattern: 1. Introduction (with a Nepali summary line), 2. Unit objectives, 3. Materials, 4. estimated-periods table mapping the student's-book exercises to periods, 5. Activities — a procedure for every numbered exercise in the student's book, with printed keys/sample answers — and a boxed "Student evaluation" rubric paragraph to close. A Nepali class song for Grade 3 (कक्षा ३ को कक्षागत गीत, lyricist/composer credited in Nepali) with the CDC Nepali colophon is the last page.
- Extent: 71 PDF pages = 6 front-matter pages + printed pp. 1–64 + 1 unfoliated final page (the Nepali song). Printed↔PDF offset: **printed = PDF − 6**, established from the printed folios in the footer ("Teacher's Guide My- English Book - III"): Unit-1 opens on printed p. 1 = PDF 7, Unit-10 opens on printed p. 60 = PDF 66, and the last folio 64 falls on PDF 70. The guide's own Contents page agrees (units at printed 1, 9, 16, 23, 28, 34, 43, 49, 53, 60).

### Unit-by-unit summary
- **Front matter (unnumbered):** Imprint (First edition: 2008; Bagmati Offset Press; no ISBN; price blank). Preface: purpose and five aims of the guide; the unit/period system explained; assessment guidance promised at each unit's end; Nepali used in places "to make it easier for the teachers". Class room language lists. Contents.
- **Unit 1 — Lilu's Diary (printed pp. 1–8; 18 periods):** Procedures and keys for all 13 student exercises: the clock chant; the four watches keyed (six o'clock, six thirty, nine o'clock, quarter to ten); Lilu picture questions keyed; an "Additional Exercise" of sentence completions; the Read procedure with Discuss answers keyed (Lilu lives in a small village; gets up at six; goes to school at quarter to ten); pair-work sample answers; two "write in words" keys (07:00 Seven o'clock … 09:45 Quarter to ten; 09:50 "It is ten to nine"); completions keyed morning/afternoon/evening/night; clock-hand questions keyed ("It is quarter to four"); the crossword printed filled, Across ELEVEN/TEN/FOUR/SEVEN/TWO/TWELVE, Down NINE/THREE/FIVE/SIX/EIGHT/ONE; "Hickery, dickery, dock"; the time game; riddle answered: **'Watch'**.
- **Unit 2 — What do you like? (printed pp. 9–15; 18 periods):** Keys throughout: choice-question drills; the two-wives story answers (the young wife liked black hair; the old wife pulled out black hair; at the end the man hadn't any hair); pair-work samples; §3 explains the student's words as containing "diphthongs /ai/ and /eə/"; matching keyed as full sentences; capitals keyed (Sit down, Pick up, Point to, Come in, Put down, Stand up); alphabetical order taught in three steps with the key "can, cap, car, catch, come, copy, could, crow"; picture-choice answers (cow, rat, TV, pilot, ball, girl, snake); the number word-search grid printed; "White sheep" chant; copy-work guidance (four-lined copy, letters h, p, g, l, c); miming game; riddle answered: **Timetable**.
- **Unit 3 — Where is my Pencil? (printed pp. 16–22; 16 periods):** Preposition teaching procedure with an explicit usage note (over = "straight on top", above = "higher than"), role play (Miss Rai questions Lilu, Pemba, Anu, Ali); Pemba reading keyed (she looked in her bag, table, bed and drawer; she didn't go to school because Saturday was a holiday; she found the pencil in her mouth); picture completions keyed (bird on the roof … cat under the table); personal-information question set modelled ("I live in Nuwakot", "I study in Janta Primary School"); the listening exercise keyed ON/IN/ON/IN/IN — the guide reprints the listening text and notes "The listening text is given on page 80 of the text book"; punctuation keyed ("He doesn't know how to fly a kite."); word-box crossword printed filled (TODAY/MORNING/YESTERDAY/SATURDAY/ALREADY); numbers-in-words keyed in full, 514 = "Five hundred and fourteen" to 799 = "Seven hundred and ninety-nine"; riddle answered: **Kite**.
- **Unit 4 — Why are you late? (printed pp. 23–27; 14 periods):** The Introduction recaps Units 1–3 before starting. Procedures: apology dialogue with variants ("I missed the bus, my food was late"); comprehension discussion; the Prashant/Prerana dialogue — the guide itself describes its structure ("May I +V1 … please?", "I am sorry …", "That's all right"); direction language for the oral conversation ("Turn left. Go straight. … You will find …"); dialogue completion keyed (Can I help you? … Thank you — You're welcome); direction-writing "possible answers" for the swimming pool, crossroad/school and post office questions; "Whose …?" chant procedure with possessive pronouns; /e/ and /r/ copying guidance ("'d' and 't' go up but 'y' goes down"); role play; crossword keyed by picture names (Pencil; Table; Chair; Bag; Pen; Eraser); riddle answered: **Table**.
- **Unit 5 — Can You? (printed pp. 28–33; 16 periods):** The guide states the target structures outright: "'Can you + V1 …?' … The supporting answer is: I am (not) + adj + enough + to infinitive", and models why-questions ("Why can't Prashant touch the ceiling? Because he is not tall enough"); true/false checking procedure (answers left to the class exchange, not printed); nationality matching keyed — with the guide contradicting itself (see Slips): the explanation line gives "Nepal= Nepali, Palpa=Palpali" but the printed answer line reads "China=Chinese, Nepal=Nepalese, France=French, Japan=Japanese"; the animal true-sentences exercise procedure; pair work; riddle answered: **Duck**; the Chintu guessing game (donkey picture pasted on a student's back); permission structure printed as "Can + V1 + infinitive + ? — Can I sit here?"; the Pushpa listening sentences reprinted with a tick/cross key (partly wrong — see Slips).
- **Unit 6 — A Picnic (printed pp. 34–42; 19 periods):** Note the guide's heading spells A PICNIC correctly against the student's book's "A PICINIC". Chart procedure (bar-diagram of maximum/minimum temperatures; "higher the degree, hotter the temperature"); picnic A answers keyed (a)–(i) (last Monday; reached at nine; Ali felt hot; the girls woke up when the food was ready) and the anagram answers continued as (j)–(n) (Cold, Happy, Swims, Cooked, Hungry); the sentence-table structure named — "Sub+V2+(obj)+time" — with all eight sentences keyed; yes/no procedure; a model likes/dislikes dialogue (the guide renames the pair Rina/Binu); month answers (June, July, February, January, March — see Slips on February); fruit grouping keyed in a table (Cold: Orange, Apple, Grapes, Tangerine; Hot: Pineapple, Mango, Guava, Jackfruit; Both: Banana, Papaya); magic-square key printed — 6 1 8 / 7 5 3 / 2 9 4, "The sum will be the same i.e. 15"; book-making procedure (paper, thread, big needle); thunder chant; the crow story reprinted WITH a "Possible ending" (stones dropped in the pot until the water rose; he drank and flew away) — the ending the student's book truncates; months copy-work; riddle answered: **river**.
- **Unit 7 — Hello! (printed pp. 43–48; 16 periods):** Telephone answers keyed (Pemba is calling Lilu; Lilu said Hello; they did nothing yesterday — see Slips on "He wanted to know"); the Ravi dialogue printed completed; Ask-your-Friend possible answers (with jumbled numbering); Bina/Pangre story: board questions set, and A keyed (didn't go, jumped, pulled, didn't look after, pulled); pair work; the word-chain table printed completed (Ball→leaf→fan→now→write …); picture answers (mango, running, pigeon, crying, left hand); colour key (Milk–white, Sky–blue, Blood–red, Ripe banana–yellow, Crow–black, Grass–green); same/different procedure; occupation answers (driver, pilot, cook, farmer, barber); crossword keyed 1. TORTOISE 2. MOUSE 3. DOG 4. CAT 5. SNAKE; riddle answered: **An elephant**.
- **Unit 8 — Birthday (printed pp. 49–52; 16 periods):** Letter work: board questions and A answers (Anu; a book named Muna; August 12th; Anu's father; "He might go"); dialogue acting, then partners ask each other "when her/his birthday is"; fill-ins keyed (When; 21st May; today; Yes, it is; Happy birthday to you!; Thank you); a full sample invitation letter (Sangeeta to Beenita, Thimi, Bhaktapur — dated "24 January, 2009"); cold-room dialogue model ("Could you close the door, please? — Yes of course."); map work organised as five group questions from Anu's house/the library/the stadium; direction questions ("Excuse me, how can I get to the school?"); classroom-list exercise with adjectives small, big, long, short, hard, soft; capitalisation of days/months; crossword keyed Down MOUTH/EAR/HEAD, Across NOSE/EYE/HAIR; the heart/earth riddle answered with its explanation ("Just move the initial letter h to the end").
- **Unit 9 — A Village Girl (printed pp. 53–59; 19 periods):** The guide's one explicit tense lesson: teach "the verb forms of the Simple Past (V2) and the Simple Future (Will/Shall + V1)". Procedures: dialogue drilling; Gauri answers keyed (14 years old; to sell the milk; the pot fell when she turned her head; at last she felt sorry); extra questions incl. the slip "Was she success in her plan?"; matching keyed; future rewrites keyed (b)–(h); table sentences; "Will + V" change-form key printed ("ate=will eat, danced=will dance, played=will play, washed=will wash, looked=will look" — headed "Answeres:"); unscramble key (I am nine years old … I love my dog); parallel writing model ("I am a boy, and my name is Bikash"); small-words key (birthday→bad, hat, bird, tray; teacher→car, rat, ear, eat; carpenter→tat, pen, can, net; football→fat, tall, fall, bat; computer→come, put, pot, met); a scripted where/what-doing Q&A for ex. 10, with picture answers (at the tap washing clothes; on the playground playing football; at the bus stop; in the classroom reading; in the field planting paddy); copy-work; crossword printed filled (EGGS, HOLIDAY, SISTER, MOTHER, MILK); riddle answered: **shoes**.
- **Unit 10 — A Sports Day (printed pp. 60–64; 15 periods):** Headed "A SPORTS DAY" (the guide Contents had listed it as "A sport day"). Sports-text answers keyed: event, three teams, first competition the 100 m race, Anu (blue) first, red team the sack race — and for the last race: "Ruchi's team … came last in the **marble** (to be corrected in the text book) and spoon race" — the guide itself flags the student's book's marble/egg contradiction (see Cross-check); B matching keyed (Ruchi–Yellow, Anu–Blue, Anand–Red); C keyed (✗, ✓, ✓, ✓, ✗); likes structure named — "Do/Does +Sub.+ like + V '-ing'.?"; likes table; the number puzzle procedure (choral model reading; no worked key printed); past-events dialogue with the guide's boxed **Note: "The 2nd, 3rd and 4th sets of pictures are not in order. The pictures of the boy and the girl need to be swapped."** and answers (Prashanta is talking to Nima; the wedding was Nima's uncle's); miming game; the man/woman listening reprinted with key (✗, ✓, ✗, ✗, ✓) and the rubric "Read the sentences given on page 80"; colouring; running-rhyme chant; riddle answered: **Globe**; a closing full-page "Students' Evaluation" text on continuous assessment; then the unfoliated Nepali class song ends the book.

### Grammar spine (as the guide frames it)
The guide does little explicit grammar naming in Units 1–4 (structures are embedded in procedures): time expressions and clock-hand language (U1); "like" choice questions and likes/dislikes statements (U2); prepositions of place with the over/above distinction spelled out in prose (U3); apology/request formulas with "May I + V1 … please?" diagrammed, direction imperatives, and "Whose …?" + possessive pronouns mine/hers/yours/his (U4). From Unit 5 it names structures directly: "Can you + V1 …?" with "I am (not) + adj + enough + to infinitive" (U5) and the garbled permission formula "Can + V1 + infinitive + ?"; "Sub+V2+(obj)+time" for past narration and positive/comparative adjectives (U6); past-tense question forms throughout the telephone/story procedures (U7); question words and direction questions (U8); the book's only full tense treatment — "Simple Past (V2)" vs "Simple Future (Will/Shall + V1)" (U9); and "Do/Does + Sub. + like + V-ing?" (U10). Assessment doctrine, repeated in every unit's closing box: assessment is continuous, covers all four skills, and is based on the unit objectives.

### Honest negatives
- No word list, glossary or vocabulary index (the student's book has one; the guide simply keys words as they arise).
- No ISBN, and the printed "Price:" line is left blank on this edition.
- Not every student's-book exercise gets a printed key: several are procedure-only (e.g. Unit 5's true/false animal sentences are peer-checked with no key; Unit 10's number puzzle gets choral-drill instructions but no worked solution; Unit 4's directions answers cover only some letters — post office and police station are not keyed).
- No listening transcripts section of its own: the two listening texts are reprinted inside their unit sections (both correctly referenced to p. 80 of the student's book).
- No answers are supplied for the student's book's own unanswered items beyond what is listed above — notably the guide gives no resolution for the number trick beyond classroom procedure.

### Slips and errors as printed
- Preface (unnumbered): the guide is designed for activities "prescribed in **My English Book Fore**" — "Fore" (for "Four") in a Grade 3 guide: the preface text was evidently carried over from the Grade Four guide and its book name garbled.
- Contents (unnumbered) lists Unit 10 as "**A sport day**" where the student's book and the guide's own unit heading (p. 60) have A SPORTS DAY; Contents also drops the question mark from "What do you like".
- Unit 2, p. 11: pair-work sample prints "**I like white heir.**" (heir for hair).
- Unit 3, p. 16: Materials list "Picture cards, word cards, **relia**, etc." — "relia" for "realia" (spelt correctly as "relia" again in Unit 9's materials discussion, p. 54 area; the word is misspelt the same way both times).
- Unit 3, p. 18: the picture-completion answers are lettered (e)–(j) although the student's book items are (a)–(f) — the lettering continues from section A instead of restarting (the same continuation lettering recurs in Unit 6's anagram answers, (j)–(n), p. 36).
- Unit 3, p. 17: the over/above rule as printed — "when the object is straight on top something, use over and when it is higher than something, use above. The sky is above our head. The sun comes over our head at noon." — is at best muddled as a usage rule.
- Unit 4: the activity procedures are numbered 1, 2, then 4 (no §3 heading appears), although the periods table numbers Read and act as exercise 3 (printed pp. 23–24).
- Unit 4, p. 26: the "Whose …?" chant is described as "Each question starts with **Which** + noun phrase + …?" — the student's chant and the guide's own next sentence use Whose/possessive pronouns; "Which" is the guide's error.
- Unit 5, p. 29: model answer "**I am not tell enough to touch it.**" (tell for tall); the same section's questions name the girl both "Prerna" and "Prerana" (the student's book has Prerana).
- Unit 5, p. 31: the nationality key line "Nepal=**Nepalese**" contradicts the guide's own explanation two paragraphs earlier ("Nepal= Nepali, Palpa=Palpali") and the student's book's "Nepal – Nepali" pattern.
- Unit 5, p. 33: in the Pushpa listening key, item 7 "She can dress herself." is marked ✗ and item 8 "But she cannot wash her clothes." is marked ✓ — the reverse of the listening text both books print (dressing herself is something Puspa CAN do); the rubric also spells her "Pushpa" while the sentences print "Puspa".
- Unit 6, p. 38: the month-completion key gives (c) **February** for "We wear cotton clothes in —" (February/August), where the hot-month answer August is the only one consistent with the exercise's own weather logic (probable key error).
- Unit 6, p. 39: "make a **magic squire**" (squire for square).
- Unit 7, p. 44: telephone answer iii calls Pemba "He" ("He wanted to know what Lilu did yesterday") — Pemba is a girl throughout both books; §3's possible answers (p. 45) are numbered i, vi, vii, iv, v in that scrambled order.
- Unit 8, p. 51: the sample invitation letter is dated "24 January, **2009**" inside a first edition of 2008 (a sample written for the following school year).
- Unit 9, p. 55: extra question "**Was she success in her plan?**" (success for successful); p. 56: the change-form key is headed "**Answeres:**".
- Unit 9, p. 57: small-words key offers "**tat**" as a word inside "carpenter".
- Unit 9, p. 58: the scripted drill asks "Where is Kamal? / What is she doing?" and answers with "He is in class" — name and pronouns mismatched within one example.
- Unit 10, p. 60: the Introduction calls this unit "a continuation to the previous lesson, Lesson-9" — lesson/unit terminology mixed (elsewhere always "Unit").
- Unit 10, p. 63: key spells the boy "**Prashanta**" (student's book: Prashant).

### Copy completeness and cross-check
- Copy completeness: the scanned copy is complete and in order: cover, title page, imprint, Preface, Class room language, Contents, printed pp. 1–64 running without gaps or repeats (folios checked at every unit opening and close: 1, 8, 9, 15, 16, 22, 23, 27, 28, 33, 34, 42, 43, 48, 49, 52, 53, 59, 60, 64), and the unfoliated Nepali song page last. Scan show-through from the thin paper is heavy on some leaves but no page is illegible. This copy's handwriting (title-page "420 CDC-8 2008", imprint "5048 c3", price-area marks) is a previous owner's, not print. No back cover is included in the file.
- Contents-vs-actual: the guide's Contents unit openings all match the folios exactly (1, 9, 16, 23, 28, 34, 43, 49, 53, 60). Its per-unit period tables match the student's book's exercise numbering in every unit (13, 13, 13, 12, 13, 13, 13, 12, 13, 11 exercises), with totals 18+18+16+14+16+19+16+16+19+15 = 167 periods.
- Cross-check vs the companion student's book (g03-student): **agreement — this is the genuine companion guide** (CDC, the same curriculum-2059 series), and the one matched pair in this band. Unit order and titles agree (modulo the student's "A PICINIC" heading vs the guide's correct "A PICNIC", and the guide Contents' "A sport day"). Every student's-book exercise is taken up in student-book order, the listening texts reprinted in the guide match the student's book's Listening Texts exactly, and both of the guide's page references to the student's book ("page 80 of the text book", Unit 3 p. 19 and Unit 10 p. 64) land on the held copy's Listening Texts page. All ten riddles are keyed (Watch, Timetable, Kite, Table, Duck, river, elephant, heart→earth explained, shoes, Globe). Two student's-book faults are flagged by the guide itself: the sports text's marble/egg race — keyed "marble **(to be corrected in the text book)**" (p. 61) — and Unit 10's picture order — "The pictures of the boy and the girl need to be swapped" (p. 63). The guide's own errors against the student's book are the inverted Pushpa ticks (items 7–8, p. 33), "Nepal=Nepalese" (p. 31), and name wobbles (Prerna/Prerana, Pushpa/Puspa, Prashanta, Pemba as "He"). The guide also completes what the student's book leaves open: it prints the crow story's ending and resolves the Unit 6 matching/anagram and Unit 2 matching keys in full sentences.

## Grade 4 — MY ENGLISH BOOK Grade IV (student's book)

### Identity block
- Title as printed: cover "MY ENGLISH BOOK / Grade IV"; running head on every content page "MY ENGLISH BOOK: Grade IV". There is no separate title page — the cover is followed directly by the imprint page. The record calls it "My English Book Grade 4"; the book itself uses the roman numeral "Grade IV" everywhere. Interior confirms Grade Four throughout (Preface: "The present English textbook for Grade Four"; the Unit 1 story hero "studies in Grade four"; Listening Text One: Anil "studies in grade four"). **Swap-check verdict: this file is genuinely Grade 4 — no swap with Grade 5.**
- Publisher: Government of Nepal, Ministry of Education, Curriculum Development Centre, Sanothimi, Bhaktapur, Nepal (cover and imprint).
- Year / edition / ISBN as printed: ISBN: 978-999-33-714-1-6 (imprint). "First edition: 2003 / Reprint: 2011" (imprint). The imprint also records approval for private-sector printing and distribution in 32 districts of the Eastern and Western Development Regions for the academic year 2068 BS under the 'School Textbook Printing and Distribution Directives - 2067 BS'. Printer's name and price are left blank ("Printer:" with no name; "Price: Rs. ..............").
- Developers/authors as printed: written by Pramod Kumar Shah and Dev Narayan Mukhiya; edited by Arun Kiran Pradhan and Bishnu Prasad Parajuli; layout design by Navindra Rajbhandari and Himalaya Gautam; illustrations by Abindra Man Shrestha (all in the Preface). No authors are named on the cover or imprint.
- Curriculum/series notes: the Grade 4 volume of the CDC primary English series introduced from Grade One in the academic year 2003 (Preface). The Preface states the book focuses "particularly on listening and speaking skills for this grade". Every unit follows the same internal pattern: Look, listen and say → chant/poem → Listen and do (keyed to the Listening Texts at the back) → Read and answer (a story or text) → vocabulary/spelling/grammar "Fun with…" sections → pair-work → writing. There is no teacher's guide for this grade in the record, and none is held.
- Extent: 92 PDF pages = cover (PDF 1) + imprint (PDF 2) + Preface (PDF 3, unnumbered) + Contents (PDF 4, unnumbered) + printed pp.1–88 (PDF 5–92).
- Printed↔PDF offset: printed = PDF − 4. Established from the Contents list (Unit 1 at printed p.1 = PDF 5) and the printed folios, which match this offset on every page from p.1 (PDF 5) to p.88 (PDF 92).

### Unit-by-unit summary
- Unit 1 Be careful! (pp.1–10): warnings and exclamations — "What a big snake! / Be careful! It's dangerous", "Don't go near"; a safety chant ("Look before you jump…"); a listening text about Anisha and Anil; the story of Raj, a careless boy playing video games on a broken bridge who is saved by a young man; present→past verb table (shout/shouted, catch, break, sleep, drive, buy); pair-work "Don't get on the bus — it's moving"; grammar: a/an with consonant/vowel sounds inside "What a…!" exclamations; punctuation with full stops, exclamation marks and capitals; writing a paragraph about yourself; a clock riddle.
- Unit 2 Me and my habits (pp.11–19): telling the time (o'clock, half past, quarter to/past) in "What time do you get up?" exchanges; writing times in words; the reading text about Suresh Gurung, 11, a football player whose day runs from a 6 o'clock start to school at ten; a morning-routine dialogue (Sudip and Deepa); a listening text about Janak's day; a "What can you do in a day?" poem; contractions (I'm, I've got, don't, she's, doesn't); the "What's the time, Mr Chankhey?" chasing game; writing about your own day.
- Unit 3 The elephant and the tortoise (pp.20–25): a past-tense holiday dialogue (Dinesh and Lokesh: went to the zoo, watched a football match); the title story — a tortoise tricks an elephant who mocks his short legs by appearing to jump over the elephant's head (his wife hidden in the grass answers his "Aha!" with "Ehe!"), and the elephant promises to call him "Mr Tortoise" ever after; must/mustn't for rules; a born-on/born-in game (day, year, place of birth); grammar: singular and plural with -s/-es.
- Unit 4 A brave woman (pp.26–32): How much / How many questions (rice, books, money, times); a number puzzle; a seed-and-crop poem; a listening text on Mother Teresa; the biography of Pasang Lhamu Sherpa (born 2017 BS in Solukhumbu; Pisang Chuli 1989, Mont Blanc 1990, Everest at her fourth attempt on 22 April 1993 — the first Nepalese woman to climb it); vocabulary matching (attempt, succeed, mountaineer, finally, failed); punctuation: capitals for names of persons and places, full stop and apostrophe; writing birth facts (was born in/on/at…) and a paragraph about your sister.
- Unit 5 The largest living bird (pp.33–38): frequency adverbs (sometimes, everyday, always, never, usually, often) in "How often do you…?" exchanges; a shy-person poem; a listening text about Gopal's habits; the ostrich text (largest and strongest living bird, flightless, African, about 8 ft tall and up to 136 kg, runs about 65 km/h, two toes, eggs about 1.4 kg); opposites vocabulary; -er/-est spelling table; grammar: "the" with superlatives ("An ostrich is the largest bird"); writing a paragraph from sometimes/always/everyday/never prompts. (The unit's sections jump from 7 to 9 — there is no section 8.)
- Unit 6 A cruel landlord (pp.39–46): past-tense talk about yesterday ("What did you do yesterday? — I went to a party"); the "One, two, three, four, five" fish chant with a crossword; a listening text about Priti's bad morning (a frog jumps out of her bag); the title story — a cruel landlord who never smiles, laughs or dances and starves his servants is punished by a singing bird whose song makes him dance for three days and three nights, after which he reforms and feeds his servants; compound words (land+lord = landlord, motorbike, classroom, girlfriend, toothpaste, blackboard); grammar: present→past sentence conversion; a mime-your-day game; writing about what you did yesterday. (Sections jump from 10 to 12 — there is no section 11.)
- Unit 7 A clever little girl and an ogre (pp.47–53): invitations — "I'm going to the cinema. Will you come with me? — Yes, please / No, thank you"; a listening text about Tom and Jerry the spies (ages, heights, hair and coat colours compared); the title story — Mrs Ogre disguises herself with a dress and red scarf to lure a little girl, but the girl spots her hairy green legs and fourteen multicoloured toes, refuses to go, and Mrs Ogre flees, so the story doubles as a never-go-with-strangers lesson; a numbers chant (Two Twenty-Two, Three Thirty-Three…); blackboard bingo; copying and writing about a friend. (Sections jump from 8 to 10 — there is no section 9.)
- Unit 8 Two close friends (pp.54–61): Do/Does questions about animals ("Does a cow live in the shed? — Yes, it does"); an animal-sounds chant (cats purr, lions roar, owls hoot…) with a word-search; a listening text about Gopi the parrot; the title story — Dambaru the donkey and Rani the cow are friends; when Rani secretly has a calf in the field, Dambaru's hee-hawing brings the farmer's children Abdul and Juhi, who carry the calf to the cowshed; animal homes vocabulary (cowshed, kennel, den, tree, pen); irregular plurals (child/children, mouse, ox); "Where does a cow live?" pair-work; hide-and-seek preposition game (in/on/under).
- Unit 9 The six blind men and the elephant (pp.62–69): "What does it look like? — It looks like a ball/tiger/snake/camera"; an opposites chant (the monkey Chankhay always says the opposite) and an opposites crossword; a listening text on what Mickey Mouse likes to eat; the title story — six blind men in a town in China each touch one part of an elephant (wall, spear, snake, tree, flag, rope) and argue; the king teaches them they each felt only a part, and they never argue again; writing about an elephant; a touch-and-guess game ("It feels like…"); a mushroom riddle ("a room without any doors or windows").
- Unit 10 Deepawali (pp.70–76): ordinal numbers with days, months and alphabet letters ("What's the first day of the week? — Sunday"; "Which is the seventh month? — Kartik"; "What's the tenth letter? — J"); a cat-and-cream chant; a listening text on Holi; the Deepawali/Tihar text — five days in Kartik: crow day, dog day, Laxmi Pooja (candles and diyos, Deushi and Bhailo), Mhapooja for Newar people, and Bhaitika (sisters give tika and sweets, brothers give gifts); listing Nepali words from the lesson; writing a paragraph on Dashain; ordinal spelling FIRST, SECOND…; grammar: third-person -s in the present simple (He worships/goes/plays vs I/They worship/go/play).
- Unit 11 Sports day (pp.77–86): comparatives and superlatives in speech ("Who runs faster than you? — Manish. He is the fastest runner in his class"); a getting-up chant; a listening text on David Beckham; the Sports Day story — Mrs Gurung announces the races (50 metres, high jump, long jump, 100 metres, bag race, tomato-and-spoon race); Hemant wins the boys' 50 metres (Prakash is taller than Hemant but not the tallest); the girls' 100 metres ends in a dead heat between Priti and the girl in the yellow dress, with Pooja fifth and slower than Priti; punctuation capitals exercise; grammar table of comparatives/superlatives including more/most intelligent, beautiful, useful; "not as tall as" pair-work; a past-tense crossword; completing a birthday-party invitation letter.
- Unit 12 Listening texts (pp.87–88): the eleven listening scripts for Units 1–11, printed as "Unit Twelve / Listening text" — Text One (Anil and Anisha, for Unit 1), Two (Janak's day, Unit 2), Three (Pemba asks Lakpa the way to the library, Unit 3), Four (Mother Teresa, Unit 4), Five (Gopal, Unit 5), Six (Priti's bad day, Unit 6), Seven (Tom and Jerry the spies, Unit 7), Eight (Gopi the parrot, Unit 8), Nine (Mickey Mouse and his sister Kimmy, Unit 9), Ten (Holi/Fagu Purnima, Unit 10), Eleven (David Beckham, Unit 11).

### Grammar spine
The book teaches grammar in small named "Fun with grammar" slots inside a skills-and-story frame, in this order: exclamations "What a/an…!" with a vs an by initial sound (Unit 1) → punctuation of statements, exclamations and direct speech — full stop, exclamation mark, capitals (Units 1, 4, 11) → clock time with o'clock / half past / quarter to and past (Unit 2) → contractions I'm / I've got / don't / doesn't / she's (Unit 2) → must/mustn't (Unit 3) → singular/plural with -s and -es (Unit 3) → How much / How many (Unit 4) → frequency adverbs always/usually/often/sometimes/never/everyday (Unit 5) → comparative and superlative adjectives with -er/-est and the + superlative (Units 5 and 11, with more/most for longer adjectives and not as…as in Unit 11) → simple past tense, regular and common irregular forms, taught through present→past tables and sentence conversion (Units 1, 6, 11) → "going to" and "will" only inside dialogue and story language, never as a grammar topic (Units 7–8) → Do/Does…? questions with short answers Yes, it does / No, it doesn't (Unit 8) → looks like / feels like for resemblance and touch (Unit 9) → ordinal numbers first–tenth (Unit 10) → present simple third-person -s (worships, goes, plays) (Unit 10). Past-tense narration is carried by the stories from Unit 1 on, well before the past is named as a topic.

### Honest negatives
- No phonics, letter-sound work or handwriting instruction: spelling sections are word-level games (missing letters, anagrams, crosswords), never sound patterns.
- No present continuous, present perfect, passive voice, conditionals, reported speech or relative clauses anywhere, named or drilled; "going to" and "will" occur in stories and dialogues but are never explained.
- No question-formation teaching beyond the memorised frames (What time…?, How much/many…?, How often…?, Do/Does…?, What does it look like?); wh-questions are never analysed.
- No word lists, glossary or dictionary work; vocabulary is handled only by matching, puzzles and picture games inside each unit.
- No tests, revision units or self-assessment: the only checks are the in-unit tick-the-answer exercises.
- No teacher's guide is held for this grade, so there are no printed answer keys to cross-check; the listening texts at the back (pp.87–88) are the book's only key-like apparatus, and they are scripts, not answers.
- Colours are never taught as a set; numbers stop at counting chants, clock times and ordinals to tenth; days and months appear only inside the Unit 10 ordinals work.

### Slips and errors as printed
- Preface (unnumbered, PDF 3): the signature block reads "Governmen of Nepal" — "Governmen" for Government.
- Contents (unnumbered, PDF 4): the listening-texts entry is misnumbered "Unit 10 Listening texts 87", duplicating Unit 10's number; the body heads it correctly "Unit Twelve / Listening text" (pp.87–88).
- p.28: the reading text says Mont Blanc "is the tallest mountain in Europe" — wrong fact as printed (Mont Blanc is the tallest in the Alps/Western Europe; Elbrus is Europe's highest).
- p.29: the question prints "When was Pasang Lamhu Sherpa born?" — "Lamhu" for Lhamu; p.32 prints "Read about Pasang Lahmu Sherpa" — a second, different misspelling of her name in the same unit.
- p.49: the ogre story prints "“Litle girl, little girl,” said Mrs Ogre" — "Litle" for Little; p.50 prints "Mrs Orge" twice in the true/false exercise ("Mrs Orge was a good woman", "Mrs Orge's house") for Ogre.
- Unit 7 story questions (p.49) switch lettering mid-list: (i)–(iv) then (e), (f).
- Listening Text Seven (p.88) is internally inconsistent: "Clever Tom and Jerry are spies. Tom is from the USA and Jerry is from Britain. Tom is two years older than Jerry. He is twenty-two. But Jerry is the tallest. She is 10 cm taller than Tom." — Jerry is referred to as "She" while Tom gets the age line, and the Unit 7 listening questions (p.48) ask "Is Jerry older than Tom?" against a script in which Tom is the older one.
- Listening Text Eleven (p.88) gives David Beckham as "five feet eleven inch tall", while the Unit 11 listening exercise (p.79) offers "six feet eleven inch" as a statement to judge — the exercise statement is the false one by design, but the two heights sit unreconciled in the same book.
- Section numbering skips a number in three units: Unit 5 runs 7 → 9 (no section 8), Unit 6 runs 10 → 12 (no section 11), Unit 7 runs 8 → 10 (no section 9).
- Unit 10 grammar exercise (p.75) numbering runs (i), (ii), (iv), (v)… — there is no item (iii).
- p.80: the Sports Day list prints "50 Meters Race" with the American spelling "Meters" beside "100 Metres Race" in the same list.

### Copy completeness and cross-check
- Folio verdict: complete. Printed folios run 1–88 unbroken on PDF pp.5–92; no missing or duplicated leaves; the file ends on the last listening text (p.88). PDF pp.1–4 are cover, imprint, Preface and Contents, not missing content.
- Contents vs actual units: all eleven unit entries match the unit openings in number, title and page (Be careful! 1, Me and my habits 11, The elephant and the tortoise 20, A brave woman 26, The largest living bird 33, A cruel landlord 39, A clever little girl and an ogre 47, Two close friends 54, The six blind men and the elephant 62, Deepawali 70, Sports day 77). The only contents error is the listening-texts line, misnumbered "Unit 10" for Unit 12 (p.87) — see Slips.
- Cross-check vs companion book: no Grade 4 teacher's guide exists in the record or in hand, so no guide cross-check is possible; every listening exercise in Units 1–11 was checked against the printed scripts on pp.87–88 and each unit's script is present and matches its unit's questions (with the Tom-and-Jerry inconsistency noted above, which is inside the script itself).
- Swap-check vs the Grade 5 book: the Grade 5 file's interior is Grade V throughout (see its filing), and this file's interior is Grade IV throughout — the two are correctly labelled, unlike the swapped pair found in another country's set.

## Grade 5 — MY ENGLISH BOOK Grade V (student's book)

### Identity block
- Title as printed: cover (PDF 1, an illustrated cover with no text layer) "MY ENGLISH BOOK / Grade V" with a large "5" in the top corner and the spine text "My English Book Grade V"; the second page (PDF 2) repeats the cover title in text form, "MY ENGLISH BOOK / Grade V". There is no separate title page. The record calls it "My English Book Grade 5"; the book itself uses "Grade V". Interior confirms Grade Five throughout (Preface: "The present English textbook for Grade Five"; the Unit 1 model letter is signed "Nabin Basnet / Grade V"; Unit 7's reading calls Salma "a fifth grader"). **Swap-check verdict: this file is genuinely Grade 5 — no swap with Grade 4.**
- Publisher: Government of Nepal, Ministry of Education, Curriculum Development Centre, Sanothimi, Bhaktapur, Nepal (cover and imprint).
- Year / edition / ISBN as printed: the ISBN is printed incomplete — "ISBN 978-999-33-717-......." (imprint, PDF 3, verified by rendering): the last digits are literally replaced by dots, so no complete ISBN is printed in the book. "First Edition: 2007 / Reprint: 2010" (imprint). Price as printed: Rs. 31.20. The imprint records approval for private-sector printing and distribution in the sixteen districts of the Eastern Development Region from the academic year 2064 BS under the 'School Textbook Printing and Distribution Directives - 2063 BS'. Printer & Distributor is left as dotted blank lines.
- Developers/authors as printed: "originally written by Jaganath Awa, Krishna Raj Hamal and Deo Narayan Mukhiya" and "edited by Bishnu Prasad Parajuli of Curriculum Development Centre"; layout design by Navindra Rajbhandari and Jayaram Kuikel; illustrations by Abindra Man Shrestha (all in the Preface). Note the author spellings as printed: "Jaganath Awa" and "Deo Narayan Mukhiya" (the Grade 4 book's co-author is printed there as "Dev Narayan Mukhiya").
- Curriculum/series notes: the Grade 5 volume of the CDC primary English series introduced from Grade One in 2003 (Preface). Unlike Grade 4's story-named units, every unit here is named for a language function (Expressing wants, Expressing reasons, Describing locations, …) and follows a fixed twelve-part pattern: dialogue/chant → game → listening → reading text → Grammar → Spelling → reading-and-act → Word power → Tricky talk (a riddle whose answer is printed at the unit's end) → Pronunciation → Write. There is no teacher's guide for this grade in the record, and none is held.
- Extent: 101 PDF pages = illustrated cover (PDF 1) + cover title page (PDF 2) + imprint (PDF 3) + Preface (PDF 4, unnumbered) + Contents (PDF 5, unnumbered) + printed pp.1–20 (PDF 6–25) + printed pp.31–106 (PDF 26–101). **Printed pp.21–30 (the whole of Unit 3) and everything from printed p.107 on (the Listening texts) are missing from this copy** — see Copy completeness.
- Printed↔PDF offset: printed = PDF − 5 up to printed p.20 (PDF 25); after the missing ten pages, printed = PDF + 5 from printed p.31 (PDF 26) to printed p.106 (PDF 101). Established from the Contents list (Unit 1 at printed p.1 = PDF 6; Unit 4 at printed p.31 = PDF 26) and the printed folios, which run 1–20 and then jump straight to 31.

### Unit-by-unit summary
- Unit 1 Expressing wants (pp.1–11): "What do you want? — I want to borrow a book" dialogues (borrowing a book, going to the circus, ten rupees for drawing paper, cokes, a leave of absence); a want-to-fly chant; a want-chain game; listening about a girl buying fruit; the potato-garden letters story — an old man's son Babbu, in prison, tricks the police into digging the garden by writing that he buried "the dead bodies" there, so the old man can plant his potatoes; grammar: wants/want agreement and a/an; spelling: regular -ed/-d pasts and irregular pasts (dig/dug, write, go, sleep, leave, drink); pronunciation of -ed as /d/, /t/, /id/; word power: noun forms (ability, honesty, height…); writing a leave letter to the headmistress (model dated 16 July 2006, from Jumla).
- Unit 2 Expressing reasons (pp.12–20): "Why is he weeping? — because he has lost his book" exchanges; a likes chant using 'cos; a picnic-choice game giving reasons; listening about Anita and Biru's favourite subjects; the Lazy Crane story — a crane pretends fishermen will take all the fish and carries fish off daily to eat them on a rock, until a wise crab rides with it, sees the fish bones, and snaps its head off with its claws; grammar: sentence-joining with because; spelling: -ing forms dropping final -e; pronunciation of plural endings /s/, /z/, /iz/; a plurals word-search; writing a because-paragraph (Kumari does not eat meat because killing animals is sinful).
- Unit 3 Describing locations (pp.21–30): **entirely missing from this copy** — the Contents lists it at p.21 and Unit 4 begins at p.31, and the printed folios jump from p.20 (PDF 25) to p.31 (PDF 26). Nothing of the unit — dialogue, reading text, grammar or exercises — is present in the file, so nothing about its content can be filed from this copy.
- Unit 4 Talking about future (pp.31–38): going to and will for plans ("What are you going to do? — I'm going to play badminton"; "Will you go to Pokhara next week? — No, I won't"); a New Year classmates chant; listening about Goma's and Kunti's travel plans; the Three Big Fish story — a wise fish leaves the lake when he hears fishermen coming, a clever fish escapes by pretending to be dead, and the fatalistic third fish is killed in the net; grammar: building going-to/will sentences from place/person/duration/lodging prompts, with the note "We use going to and simple future tense to talk about our future plans"; spelling: contractions; pronunciation: falling tone in wh-questions; writing your Saturday plans on the model of Mr Dev's.
- Unit 5 Describing people (pp.39–47): "What is she like?" descriptions (build, clothes, carrying); the crooked man chant; listening to complete a table of grandfather/mother/brother (age, hair, height/size); a guess-who game; the Some Famous Scientists reading — James Watt (1736–1819, steam engine), Louis Pasteur (1822–1895, germs, pasteurization, vaccines for anthrax and rabies), Thomas Alva Edison (1847–1931, over 1000 inventions, light bulb, phonograph) and Alfred Nobel (1833–1896, dynamite, left his fortune for the Nobel Prizes), sourced to "Disney Children's Encyclopaedia"; grammar: has got / Her eyes are blue description patterns; spelling: -ly adverbs and -er/-est comparison table; writing a person description from name-place-age-class clues.
- Unit 6 Describing time (pp.48–57): when/what time/how long exchanges with yesterday, next month, in a week, for a month, on Saturday, at 5; a days-of-the-week chant; listening about the actor Kiran K. C.; Little Red Riding Hood in a changed version — the grandmother senses the wolf's disguise, hides, and beats the wolf off with her rolling pin herself, so no woodcutter appears; word power: male/female word pairs (father/mother, nephew/niece, tiger/tigress…); grammar: a past-tense cloze passage teaching the simple past, did-questions with the base verb, and the past perfect (had + pp) for the earlier of two past actions; spelling: homophone pairs (knight/night, later/letter, were/wear…); pronunciation of the /əʊ/ sound; writing a bio-data paragraph (Numa Sherpa model).
- Unit 7 Describing quantities (pp.58–66): How many / How much with prices (a kilo of sugar 45 rupees, a litre of kerosene 50 rupees, a jar of jam 90 rupees); a how-much-is-too-much chant; a chain-drill game; the Interview reading — Laxmi questions Prem (two children, spends 25% of income on food and clothes, 20% each on education and health, saves and spends savings on social service) and Purna (six children, 80% on food and clothes, 10% each on education and health, no savings); measurement words unscrambled (metre, litre, kilometre, millilitre, grams…); grammar: partitives (a jar of, a piece of, a packet of, a bottle of, a kilo of, a litre of) and many/much question building; spelling: noun suffixes -tion, -age, -iture, -ment; writing about a large family's daily quantities (Salma of Kailali, fourteen people).
- Unit 8 Describing possessions (pp.67–74): "Whose bag is this? — It is Mina's bag. It is hers" with possessive pronouns; a dinosaurs chant; listening about pandas; the Dinosaurs reading — fossils and how they form, carnivorous theropoda, herbivorous sauropoda, a third group printed as "ornithicia", armour and plates, and the asteroid-extinction theory (dust blocked sunlight, vegetation died, dinosaurs starved); grammar: the pronoun→possessive table (I/my/mine…) and possessive fill-ins; spelling: -er agent nouns (sister, doctor, painter, tractor…); writing a description of a dog, then a cow.
- Unit 9 Checking and confirming (pp.75–82): question tags across tenses ("Today is Sunday, isn't it?"; "You didn't come yesterday, did you?"; "You'll come tomorrow, won't you?"); a chant about rag-picking children ending "They are our brothers, aren't they?"; listening about Bhola's and Aite's families' jobs; the Bridhashram reading — Sunayena, 13, in grade seven, spends her Saturdays at the Pashupati old people's home cooking, washing clothes, bathing the elderly, sweeping and talking with them, because "I see God in them"; word power: adjectives for good and bad people; grammar: tag matching and completion; spelling: -ing forms with doubling (slam, trek, get, stop, shop, swim); writing a tag-question survey paragraph.
- Unit 10 Talking about months and seasons (pp.83–91): day/date/month/birthday exchanges ("I was born on July 14th, 1995"); a four-seasons chant; listening about a girl's admission and school terms; the Magician story — a flood sweeps a child-eating snake into Goswana village on the Ganga; a tattooed magician turns it into a stick and warns that if the stick is broken the snake returns; five years later a beggar breaks it trying to sell it, is swallowed, and the magician finally shuts the snake, turned into a fly, in his magic box; grammar: prepositions of time at/on/in with the printed rule; spelling: -ful adjectives (beautiful, harmful, useful…) with the note that in -ful "the last 'l' is genrally dropped"; a Peter-and-Dorje dialogue on Nepal's climate and seasons; silent letters pronunciation (talk/walk, knight/knee, comb/bomb); writing about the summer season from a winter model.
- Unit 11 Narrating events (pp.92–99): a picture-story of bulls, a tiger and a jackal whose lesson is "Unity is strength"; a unity chant; listening about Lakpa's return and trekking plans; a Chinese-whispers game; The Brave Little Parrot — in a Jataka-style tale the Buddha, born as a parrot, ferries drops of water to a forest fire (set by "lightening") until a god, disguised as a golden eagle, weeps tears that quench the fire and heal the animals; word power anagrams and an animals crossword; grammar: the four past tenses side by side — simple past, past progressive, past perfect and "past prefect progressive" [as printed] — with affirmative and negative model sentences; a dialogue in which Yangla describes a motorbike accident; pronunciation of deer/dear/dare-type sets; writing a folk tale in your own language.
- Unit 12 Talking about picnic (pp.100–106): a picnic-planning picture discussion (Godawari picnic park, pick-up points, food and equipment lists); a picnic-day chant; listening about Sanitya's early morning; a count/mass noun board game (sections are misnumbered — see Slips); the Nagarkot reading — Nepal's hills, Nagarkot about 19 km north-east of Bhaktapur, an hour's drive, sunrise views, and what a good picnic spot needs (drinking water, sunshades, food-preparation and rest places, guarding); word power jumbled words; grammar: the simple present for universal truth and habitual action ("The sun rises in the east", "Water boils at 100ºc") with a verb fill-in; alphabetical order; pronunciation: noun/verb stress pairs (export, present, record); writing eight to ten sentences describing your place.
- Listening texts (Contents p.107 onwards): **missing from this copy** — the file ends at printed p.106, the end of Unit 12, although the Contents lists "Listening texts 107" and every unit has a listening exercise keyed to them.

### Grammar spine
The book's grammar is function-led, one named topic per unit, in this order: wants/want subject agreement + a/an (Unit 1) → because reason clauses and sentence joining (Unit 2) → [Unit 3's grammar cannot be stated — the unit is missing from the copy] → going to + will future for plans, with contractions (Unit 4) → has got and is/are for describing people, -ly adverbs, comparison with -er/-est (Unit 5) → simple past with did-questions and the past perfect (had + past participle) for the earlier of two past actions (Unit 6) → How much / How many with partitives a jar of / a kilo of (Unit 7) → possessive adjectives and pronouns, mine/hers/his/yours/theirs (Unit 8) → question tags in present, past and future forms (Unit 9) → prepositions of time at/on/in (Unit 10) → the full past-tense set named together: simple past, past progressive (was/were + -ing), past perfect, past perfect progressive, in affirmative and negative (Unit 11) → simple present for universal truths and habits (Unit 12). Supporting systems run in parallel: -ed pronunciation /d/-/t/-/id/ and plural pronunciation /s/-/z/-/iz/ (Units 1–2), -ing spelling rules (Units 2, 9), noun-formation suffixes (Units 1, 7), homophones (Unit 6), silent letters (Unit 10) and word stress in noun/verb pairs (Unit 12).

### Honest negatives
- Unit 3 (Describing locations, printed pp.21–30) is wholly absent from this copy, and the Listening texts (printed p.107 to the end) are also absent: twelve of the book's listening exercises therefore have no script in hand, and nothing in this filing relies on them. What Unit 3 teaches cannot be reported from this copy at all.
- No present perfect or present continuous is taught as a topic (present continuous forms occur inside stories and the Unit 11 past-progressive contrast only); no passive voice, no conditionals as a topic (one "If you were here" line inside the Unit 1 story), no reported speech, no relative clauses.
- No modal verbs beyond want/can in dialogues; no future beyond going to/will in Unit 4.
- No tests or revision programme: assessment is the in-unit exercises plus the Tricky talk riddles; there are no answer keys in the student's book (Tricky talk answers are printed at each unit's end, upside-down-style in parentheses, and are the only keys).
- No glossary or word list: vocabulary is carried by the Word power sections and puzzles only.
- Phonics is limited to the Pronunciation slots (sounds, silent letters, stress pairs); there is no systematic reading instruction.

### Slips and errors as printed
- Imprint (PDF 3): the ISBN is printed unfinished — "ISBN 978-999-33-717-......." — with the final digits replaced by dots (verified by rendering the page).
- p.42 vs p.43: the Edison reading says he "made over 1000 new inventions" (p.42), but the fill-in exercise on p.43 states "Edison made over 100 inventions" — the two figures disagree inside the same unit.
- p.43: the matching exercise prints "Thomas A. Edition" for Thomas A. Edison.
- p.42: "They are given ever y year for Literature…" — "ever y" split; the same split recurs on p.66 ("five bottles of milk ever y morning").
- p.98: the grammar section prints "Past prefect progressive" for past perfect progressive, in the book's one presentation of the four past tenses.
- The Brave Little Parrot (pp.94, 96) prints "lightening" for lightning three times ("the lightening struck a dry dead tree"; in the word list "lightening laugh at suffering…").
- Dinosaurs (p.69): the herbivorous sauropoda are described as having "large powerful jaws and very sharp claws" — the carnivore traits are attached to the plant-eaters as printed; the third group is printed "ornithicia" (for Ornithischia).
- Unit 12 (pp.102–103): two consecutive sections are both numbered 5 ("5. Let's play a game" and "5. Read and answer") and there is no section 4 — the listening section is numbered 3, then the numbering jumps to 5 twice.
- Unit 4 dialogue (p.37): the conversation is headed "Rima: Hi, Rima. How are you?" — Rima greets herself; the first speaker line is misattributed (Nira speaks next and is clearly the greeter).
- Unit 5 chant (p.40): the crooked-man rhyme prints "He bought a crooked stile" where the rhyme needs the sixpence purchase — as printed he buys the stile and then "a crooked cat", and the sixpence is never bought.
- Unit 10 spelling note (p.90): "the last 'l' is genrally dropped out" — "genrally" for generally; the same unit's exercise head on PDF 85 prints "When we use -full - to the words" with the stray second hyphen.
- Unit 2 story (p.15): "He also said that if the fish wanted to be safe. He would carry them to another pond everyday." — a broken sentence (full stop after "safe") as printed.
- p.55: the grammar note is printed after the spelling exercise it belongs to, at the foot of the page, so the simple-past/past-perfect explanation follows the homophone work instead of the Unit 6 grammar cloze on p.55's facing material.

### Copy completeness and cross-check
- Folio verdict: **incomplete copy**. Printed folios run 1–20 (PDF 6–25), then jump to 31 and run 31–106 (PDF 26–101). Two blocks are missing: printed pp.21–30 (the whole of Unit 3, Describing locations) and printed pp.107 onwards (the Listening texts listed in the Contents, and anything after them). No leaves are duplicated, and within the held ranges no folio is missing or out of order. PDF 1 is the illustrated cover (image-only, no text layer); PDF 2 is the cover title page.
- Contents vs actual units: every held unit matches the Contents in number, title and start page (Expressing wants 1, Expressing reasons 12, Talking about future 31, Describing people 39, Describing time 48, Describing quantities 58, Describing possessions 67, Checking and confirming 75, Talking about months and seasons 83, Narrating events 92, Talking about picnic 100). The Contents also lists Describing locations at 21 and Listening texts at 107 — both absent from the copy, which is how the gaps are proved from inside the file itself.

- Swap-check vs the Grade 4 book: the Grade 4 file's interior is Grade IV throughout (see its filing) and this file's interior is Grade V throughout — the two are correctly labelled, unlike the swapped pair found in another country's set. ## Grade 6 — English Grade 6 (student's book)

### Identity block
- Title as printed: cover "English / Grade 6"; title page (PDF 2) "ENGLISH BOOK / Grade 6"; running head throughout "ENGLISH BOOK, Grade 6". The record calls it "English Book Grade 6", which matches the title page and running head; the cover shortens it to "English Grade 6". Interior confirms Grade 6 throughout (the Unit 4 reading is the instructions "to the students of grade six"; Unit 8's grammar dialogue has Hari "visit my relatives… Fewa lake" in a Grade 6 frame; the Preface addresses "this basic level (6 to 8) curriculum").
- Publisher: Government of Nepal, Ministry of Education, Curriculum Development Centre, Sanothimi, Bhaktapur (cover, title page and imprint). The cover (PDF 1) misprints the town as "Sanothimi, Bhaktpur" — see Slips.
- Year / edition / ISBN as printed: ISBN 978-9937-601-00-9 (imprint). "First Edition: 1994 / Revised Edition (Piloting): 2011 / Revised Edition: 2012" (imprint). Price and printer lines are printed but left blank ("Price:" / "Printed at:" with nothing after them). The imprint expressly permits photocopying "for teacher training or other not-profit-making purposes" [as printed]. Contact line as printed: Editing and Publishing Section, CDC, Phone: 01-6630-588, Fax: 01-6630-797, Email: cdc@ntc.net.np, Website: moescdc.gov.np.
- Developers/authors as printed: the Preface states the first edition (1994) was written by Naresh Prasad Gautam, Arun Kiran Pradhan, Christine Stone and Pramod Kumar Shah; the revision was done by a task force of Krishna Raj Hamal, Padam Prasad Pandey, Tulasi Prasad Acharya, Bishnu Prasad Parajuli and Madhu Upadhaya, with suggestions from Executive Director Khagaraj Baral, Shambhu Dahal, Dilli Ram Luitel, Smita Nepal and others, and special thanks to Kent Grosh and the ETA team; layout and cover artwork by Navindra Man Rajbhandari; illustrations by Sunil Ranjit, Gautam Manandhar and Laxman Bhujel. No illustrator or author is named on the cover or imprint.
- Curriculum/series notes: the first book of the basic-level (Grades 6–8) English course under the curriculum revised in 2011 (Preface). The Contents is a four-column syllabus table (Topic / Language function / Grammar / Exercise / Page) — the only book in this Nepal set that prints its grammar syllabus up front. Every unit follows the same symmetrical pattern: Look and answer → Read and answer (a text) → Ways with words → True or false → Read and answer again → Have your say → Time for grammar → Write/Listen sections → Test yourself. A large diagonal pink "Curriculum Development Centre" watermark is printed across the content pages of this copy.
- Extent: 114 PDF pages = cover (PDF 1) + title page (PDF 2) + imprint (PDF 3) + Preface (PDF 4, unnumbered) + Contents (PDF 5–6, unnumbered) + printed pp.1–108 (PDF 7–114), **with one block of leaves bound out of order** (printed pp.27–37 come before printed pp.20–26) and **everything after printed p.108 missing** — the Contents lists Units 13–17 (pp.109–150), a Listening script (p.151) and a Vocabulary section (pp.i–v), none of which is in this copy. See Copy completeness.
- Printed↔PDF offset: there is no single offset, because of the displaced block. Printed pp.1–19 = PDF 7–25 (printed = PDF − 6); printed pp.27–37 = PDF 26–36 (printed = PDF + 1); printed pp.20–26 = PDF 37–43 (printed = PDF − 17); printed pp.38–108 = PDF 44–114 (printed = PDF − 6 again, e.g. PDF 44 carries printed p.38 and PDF 114 carries printed p.108). Established from the Contents start pages and the printed folios, which were read in sequence for the whole file.

### Unit-by-unit summary
- Unit 1 Some birds and animals (pp.1–7): a reading text on danphe (the national bird), pandas in China, cheetahs (100 km/h for a few minutes), giraffes eating leaves 6 metres high, an African frog 35 cm and 3 kg, swifts staying airborne two years and flying 900 km a day, penguins in Antarctica, snakes smelling with their tongues, and Terai crocodiles; can/can't ability talk; writing about your village; grammar: making yes/no and wh-questions by sentence transformation, then a full lesson on a/an vs the (first mention vs known, room objects, the bank/the doctor) with a fill-in test.
- Unit 2 A brave female pilot (pp.8–19): the Amy Johnson story — her solo England-to-Australia flight of May 1930, her forced landing on a Yangon football field in heavy rain when she could not find the racecourse and ran out of petrol, students mending the broken wings with their shirts, the plane carried to the racecourse on a fire engine, take-off on Friday 16 May 1930 and arrival at Darwin on 24 May; a full describing-people word bank (build, face, nose, eyes, hair) with describe-your-friend and describe-Dolma tasks; a made-of/used-for guessing game; grammar: prepositions of place (at/on/in, over/above, under/below, between/among) and of motion (over, through, along, across) with picture exercises; describing clothes (stripes, checks, spots, sleeves, Kurta Salwar).
- Unit 3 Wit (pp.20–26; bound after Unit 4 in this copy — see Copy completeness): the title story — three Brahmin graduates of Benaras, each sure he is the cleverest, are challenged by a landlord to guess by astrology what is in his fist; one guesses food, one a grinding stone, and the third reasons that both are too big for a fist and answers a coin, winning them all food and shelter — book knowledge plus wit; vocabulary (astrology, shelter, landlord, grind); grammar: punctuation and capital letters (full stop, question mark, exclamation mark, comma, inverted commas, full stops in abbreviations Dr./M.A./P.M.); question-practice with do/does/did/can/am/are and wh-words; a zoo-visit listening; completing a late-for-school dialogue.
- Unit 4 A field trip (pp.27–37; bound before Unit 3 in this copy): a Grade 6 field-trip text — the teacher's instructions (groups of five, monitors Shambhu and Shanti, uniform compulsory, notebooks but no school books, beware pickpockets), the bus to Gorkha, lunch in the hotel because street food "left out in the open… not hygienic", the Photography Prohibited rule, Gorkha Durbar and Gorakhkali Temple, then Kurintar and the cable cars "hanging on a rope like bats" up to Manakamana temple; permission language in full (may/can for asking, must/mustn't, should/shouldn't, ought to, have to, needn't) with role-plays at home and school; writing a report from Shanti's notes; grammar: reflexive pronouns (myself…themselves, including "by herself" = alone); a health-rules listening; the Five Little Monkeys poem.
- Unit 5 A real hero (pp.38–47): greetings, introductions and leave-taking dialogues; the Temba Tshiri Sherpa biography — born 6 May 1985, son of a trekking guide; the April 2000 Everest attempt at fifteen, when he took off his gloves to tighten loose boots, was frostbitten, lost five fingers (two of the left hand, three of the right) to amputation and was turned back; his successful summit at 7 am on 23 May 2001, "just over sixteen", making him the youngest Nepali to have climbed Mount Everest, with a Spanish climber and another Sherpa; his wish to become a trekking guide; a crossword on the text's words; a School Poem; dinosaur listening questions; grammar: the simple present for routines with the he/she -s table (love/loves, go/goes, study/studies) and third-person rewriting of Sarala's morning routine.
- Unit 6 The earth and us (pp.48–58): an environment reading (from "Our World in Danger") — deforestation for roads, dams, farms and towns, rubbish and river pollution, leopards killed for fur coats and crocodiles for shoes, handbags and belts, extinct animals, tree-planting and wood-saving stoves; apologising phrases and responses (Sorry / It's OK / It doesn't matter / Forget it) with situation practice; Christina Rossetti's poem "Rainbow"; movement-word matching (sail, twirl, skip, float, drift, flow); grammar: the present continuous (am/is/are + -ing) for actions at the time of speaking, contrasted with the simple present, with picture description writing and a telephone-dialogue listening.
- Unit 7 The world cup (pp.59–67): expressing surprise ("What a big lion it is!", "How handsome he is!"); the World Cup reading — held every four years, first held in Uruguay in 1930 with thirteen teams, first Asian Cup in South Korea and Japan in 2002, the 1994 USA final watched by about 1.5 billion on television, and the winners list (Uruguay 1930/1950, Argentina 1978/1986, West Germany 1954/1974/1990, Italy 1934/1938/1982/2006, Spain 2010, Brazil five times: 1958, 1962, 1970, 1994, 2002); a "Read and complete" winners table (which misassigns the years — see Slips); the My Pets poem by Lacey Broughton; grammar: the simple past (regular -ed and irregular forms) and the past continuous (was/were + -ing), with a dog-and-reflection story to complete in past forms and a rice-cooking recipe to order.
- Unit 8 A lesson from a grass cutter (pp.68–77): the Bhanubhakta Acharya biography — born, as printed, "in 1814 BS" in Chundi Ramgha, Tanahu, educated by his grandfather, from a wealthy family; the grass-cutter who spent his savings digging a well for villagers and travellers teaches the comfortable young Bhanubhakta the value of being remembered for good work, and Bhanubhakta resolves to translate the Ramayan into Nepali (the passage's ending is carried in the arrange-the-sentences exercise); a bookshop dialogue; a full dictionary-skills lesson (alphabetical order by first, second and third letters, with word lists to order and look up); grammar: will for future actions, introduced through a Fewa Lake boating conversation and applied by rewriting Harka Bahadur's morning routine as tomorrow's plans; a cartoon-show listening.
- Unit 9 Fewa Lake (pp.78–83): expressing ability and disagreement; the Fewa Lake text — Pokhara the Lake City, the lake the second largest in Nepal and largest in Pokhara Valley at about 4.43 sq km with 46 million cubic metres capacity, fed by the Harpan River, dammed for irrigation and electricity, the Barahi Island Temple and the Machhapuchhre/Annapurna reflections as main attractions, Lakeside hotels, and its species counts (17 native and 4 exotic fish, 6 amphibians, 14 reptiles, 34 mammals, 104 birds); a fright chant (the "monkey's tail" on the floor); grammar: making statements negative with not, and yes/no questions with short answers; a can/can't class survey; a nonsense-words listening.
- Unit 10 Kites (pp.84–93): likes and dislikes; the Kites reading — 3,000 years of history from bamboo-and-silk China, 19th-century scientific use, military use in the 20th–21st centuries, Franklin's atmospheric-electricity experiments and Alexander Graham Bell's kite studies, Polynesian fishing kites, and kite-flying in Nepal in Bhadra and Ashwin during Dashain and Tihar; sentence building with like/enjoy/hate + -ing; letter writing (Sarita's party invitation, Om's thank-you letter, a thank-you note to write); grammar: short answers "Yes, I do / No, I don't" (with the note that we do not answer "Yes, I enjoy"), prefer, and a full active/passive voice lesson (present and past passive: "The rat is killed by the cat", "The race was won by him"); the peace poem "All We Want Is Peace (Please)" by Gordon David; an acrostic (FRIENDS) poem task.
- Unit 11 Automated Teller Machine (pp.94–101): expressing possession and giving directions; the ATM reading — how a cash machine works (card, PIN, options, dispensing with a receipt), withdrawing abroad in the local currency, and a short ATM history as printed (Luther George "considered the first person to develop the ATM", a machine in 1963, Chemical Bank's first US installation in New York in 1969, first use in Tokyo in 1966, UK use from 1972, booths open 24 hours); possessive adjectives (my/his/its/our/her/your) in a self-introduction text and pronoun-replacement exercises; matching e-text messages (textspeak) to their standard forms; grammar: relative clauses with who for people and that/which for things, defining people ("An architect is someone who designs buildings") and joining sentence pairs; map directions to the ATM booth, museum, hospital and post office; a restaurant-menu listening.
- Unit 12 (pp.102–108): the George Stephenson biography — born in England in 1781, a poor miner's son who minded a rich man's cows at eight, worked in a mine at twelve, learned to read and write at evening school from 1799 while mending shoes and clocks, his son Robert born 1803, his wife's death two years later, his engines, and "the Rocket" of 1829 pulling carriages at 48 kilometers an hour (US spelling as printed); his railways, lamps and clocks, his money given to build schools and libraries for poor children, his death in 1848 and Robert's railway and bridge work in Canada, Egypt, Germany and India; paragraph-title matching; grammar: past-tense negatives and questions (didn't / Did…?), was and were, and a comparative/superlative adjective lesson (better, oldest, more expensive, as…as, cheapest) — with the adjective exercise lettering restarting mid-list (see Slips); a letter-listening true/false task; Test yourself tables converting statements between affirmative, negative, question and answer forms. The unit ends at printed p.108, where this copy also ends.

### Grammar spine
This is the first Nepal book in the set with an explicit, named grammar syllabus (printed in the Contents), and as held (Units 1–12) it runs: sentence transformation into yes/no and wh-questions + articles a/an/the (Unit 1) → prepositions of place and motion (Unit 2) → punctuation and capital letters (Unit 3) → reflexive pronouns, inside a unit whose language work is the modal set may/must/should/ought to/have to/needn't for permission and obligation (Unit 4) → simple present tense for routines, with third-person -s (Unit 5) → present continuous for actions now, contrasted with the simple present (Unit 6) → simple past and past continuous (Unit 7) → will for the future (Unit 8, filed in the Contents as "Tense revision") → negation with not and yes/no questions (Unit 9) → active and passive voice in the present and past simple, with short answers Yes, I do / No, I don't and prefer (Unit 10) → possessive adjectives and relative clauses with who/that/which (Unit 11) → past-tense negatives and questions, was/were, and comparatives/superlatives with as…as (Unit 12). The Contents promises more that this copy does not contain: reported speech (Unit 13), may/might probability (Unit 14), polite request/suggestion structures (Unit 15) and may/might/must (Unit 17) — none of those units is in the held file.

### Honest negatives
- The copy is truncated: Units 13–17, the Listening script (Contents p.151) and the Vocabulary section (Contents pp.i–v) are not in the file, so no listening script for any held unit can be checked from this book, and the grammar the Contents promises for those units (reported speech, probability modals) is absent from the held copy.
- No answer keys are printed in the student's book; true/false and test-yourself exercises have no printed answers.
- Within the held units there is no present perfect, no past perfect, no conditional sentence and no reported speech — past time is simple past and past continuous only (Unit 7), with was/were revisited in Unit 12.
- Pronunciation/phonics is absent: no sound, stress or intonation work appears in any held unit (the missing Vocabulary section might have carried word lists, but that cannot be verified from this copy).
- Writing is tightly modelled throughout: reports from notes, letters from skeletons, descriptions from tables; there is no free composition beyond a paragraph, and no examination practice of any kind.

### Slips and errors as printed
- Cover (PDF 1): "Curriculum Development Centre / Sanothimi, Bhaktpur" — "Bhaktpur" for Bhaktapur (the title page and imprint spell it correctly).
- Contents (PDF 5–6): the four-column table is densely set and its Unit 12 row ("12. George Stephenson … 102") sits offset between the Unit 11 (Automated Teller, p.94) and Unit 13 (The Great Goose of Gosaikunda, p.109) rows — the first filing draft misread the table and claimed Unit 12 was omitted from the Contents; the citation check disproved that (the row is present in full) and this filing corrects it.
- Unit 1 grammar (p.4): the model transformation for the articles unit prints "A penguin is a bird. Is penguin a bird? Yes, it is." — the example question drops the article the unit is teaching.
- Unit 3 story (p.20): "Each of them thought he was better then the other two" — "then" for than.
- Unit 7 (p.60): the "Read and complete" winners table misassigns every row against the passage on p.59: it gives Uruguay "1978, 1986" (Argentina's years in the passage), West Germany "1934, 1938, 1982 and 2006" (Italy's years) and Brazil "2010" (Spain's year) — the country names and year lists are shifted one row apart.
- Unit 7 passage (p.59): "In 1994 football was a part of the Olympic Games. But because football was so popular, a new competition had to be held." — as printed the date is wrong and the reasoning garbled (the first World Cup, 1930, is then correctly given); 1994 is the year the passage itself gives for the USA World Cup two sentences later.
- Unit 8 passage (p.69): "Bhanubhakta Acharya was born in 1814 BS" — 1814 is his AD birth year; in BS he was born in 1871. The arranging exercise (p.71) prints the safer "born in 1814" without the era.
- Unit 10 (p.86): the true/false set prints "In Nepal, we fly kites only during Dashian" — "Dashian" for Dashain; the same unit's model letter (p.88) prints "I will order wounderful momos from Almond cafe" and holds the party "at Davis Fall" (for wonderful; Devi's Fall).
- Unit 10 pair-work (p.88): "Do you like playing crickets?" — "crickets" for cricket.
- Unit 11 ATM history (pp.94–95): "Luther George, an American, is considered the first person to develop the ATM. He built such a machine in 1963." and "The first ATM was used in Tokyo of Japan in 1966" are printed as fact; the history is garbled as printed (the names, dates and places do not match the documented ATM record), and the unit's own true/false item (p.96) restates the Tokyo date as 1969, contradicting the text's 1966.
- Unit 12 grammar (pp.106–107): the adjective exercise is lettered a–c on p.106 and then restarts at "d." twice — p.107 continues d–j with a second d ("My marks are…") after p.106's d ("I prefer that color…"), so the list has two items d and no clean a–j run.
- Unit 4 vocabulary (p.30): the crossword is headed "Do the following cossword puzzle" — "cossword" for crossword.
- Unit 2 (p.18): "Complete the description of your freind" — "freind" for friend.
- Unit 12 passage (p.102): "Stephenson didn't go to school until he was 18. He looked after a rich man's cows… At that time he was 8 years old." — the two sentences sit in contradictory order as printed (the schooling claim precedes the age-eight cow-minding it postdates by ten years).

### Copy completeness and cross-check
- Folio verdict: **all held leaves present, but the copy is both misbound and truncated.** Printed folios 1–108 are all present exactly once. The block printed pp.27–37 (Unit 4) is bound before printed pp.20–26 (Unit 3), so in PDF order the units run 1, 2, 4, 3, 5, 6…; every other leaf is in sequence. The file ends at printed p.108 (end of Unit 12). Against the Contents, the copy lacks Units 13 (p.109), 14 (p.119), 15 (p.128), 16 (p.136), 17 (p.141), the Listening script (p.151) and the Vocabulary (pp.i–v). (A caution for the checker: this PDF's text layer drops some bottom-of-page lines where the diagonal watermark crosses them — e.g. the cable-car lines at the foot of p.29 and the end of the World Cup passage on p.59 — but rendering those pages shows the print is complete there; the gaps are in the text layer, not the book.)
- Contents vs actual units: the Contents table's start pages match the held units exactly for Units 1–12 (1, 8, 20, 27, 38, 48, 59, 68, 78, 84, 94, 102), and its grammar/function columns describe the held units accurately. One disagreement: the Contents' Units 13–17, Listening script and Vocabulary have no corresponding leaves in the copy (the copy is truncated at p.108).
- Cross-check vs the Grade 6 Teacher's Guide: **the guide does not belong to this book.** The held guide is the 2022 edition for the newer eighteen-unit Grade 6 textbook (T. S. Eliot, Three Simple Rules, Beauty and the Beast, Festivals of Nepal…): not one of its unit titles, reading texts, page references or answer keys matches this student's book's units (Some birds and animals, A brave female pilot, Wit, A field trip…), and the guide even refers to a textbook page 163, which this 2012 book never reaches. The only title the two share is "The Musicians of Ilam" — Unit 14 (p.119, not held) in this book's Contents, but Reading I of Unit 15 in the guide's book. No key, transcript or procedure in the guide can be used with this student's book. Full detail is in the guide's own filing (bandB-g06-teacher.md).
