## Grade 6 — TEACHER'S GUIDE: ENGLISH GRADE SIX (teacher's guide)

### Identity block
- Title as printed: cover "TEACHER'S GUIDE / ENGLISH GRADE SIX". There is no separate title page; the cover is followed by the imprint page (PDF 2), the Preface (PDF 3) and the Table of Contents (PDF 4). The record calls it "Teacher's Guide Grade 6 English", which matches. **The interior proves a correction to what the record implies by pairing it with the held Grade 6 student's book: this guide is for a different, newer Grade 6 textbook — an eighteen-unit book that is not the held student's book** (see Copy completeness and cross-check).
- Publisher: Government of Nepal, Ministry of Education, Science and Technology, Curriculum Development Centre, Sanothimi, Bhaktapur (cover and imprint). Note the ministry's name as printed here — "Ministry of Education, Science and Technology" — where the three Nepal student's books in this set print "Ministry of Education".
- Year / edition / ISBN as printed: "Edition: 2022" (imprint). **No ISBN is printed anywhere in the file; no price, printer or copyright line is printed** — the imprint page carries only the publisher block and the edition year.
- Developers/authors as printed: written by Ms. Anita Paudel, Ms. Mallika Joshi Shrestha, Mr. Ramesh Kumar Ghimire and Ms. Srijana Sharma; edited by Mr. Nabin Kumar Khadka, Dr. Neil David Rose, Mr. Nim Prakash Singh Rathour and Mr. Shankar Adhikari; Director General Mr. Ana Prasad Neupane; subject committee members Prof. Dr. Jib Lal Sapkota, Prof. Dr. Rishi Ram Rijal, Dr. Gopal Prasad Pandey, Mr. Tuka Raj Adhikari, Mr. Madhav Prasad Ghimire and Ms. Maiya Niraula Pokharel; layout design by Mr. Shreehari Shrestha (all in the Preface).
- Curriculum/series notes: the guide to an eighteen-unit Grade 6 English textbook organised in nine tasks per unit (Getting started, Reading I, Speaking, Listening, Grammar, Reading II or Writing, Writing/Project work, Extra bit), with icons for each task type explained in the Introduction (pp.1–4). Its method is stated in the Introduction: maximise Student Talking Time, use English as the classroom language, teach vocabulary by Meaning-Form-Pronunciation (MFP) or Meaning-Pronunciation-Form (MPF), use delayed and peer correction, remedial teaching, and a full inclusion section (gender equality, seating for visual/hearing impairment, differentiation by Bloom's taxonomy). Unit time is budgeted per unit (e.g. Unit 1: "Total time for the unit 6 hours"). Each unit gives objectives, materials, stepwise procedures and printed answer keys for the textbook's exercises, and the book closes with Listening Transcripts for all eighteen units (pp.140–143).
- Extent: 147 PDF pages = cover (PDF 1) + imprint (PDF 2) + Preface (PDF 3, unnumbered) + Table of Contents (PDF 4, unnumbered) + printed pp.1–143 (PDF 5–147).
- Printed↔PDF offset: printed = PDF − 4. Established from the Table of Contents (Introduction at printed p.1 = PDF 5; Unit 1 at printed p.5 = PDF 9; Listening Transcripts at printed p.140 = PDF 144) and the printed folios, which match this offset throughout to p.143 (PDF 147).

### Unit-by-unit summary
- Introduction (pp.1–4): how the guide is organised; the textbook's task icons; teaching tips (exposure, classroom English, MFP/MPF vocabulary teaching, gestures, classroom language, Student Talking Time, error-correction techniques, monitoring and feedback, assessment without tests, remedial teaching, project work); and an inclusion framework (attitude, identifying barriers, gender-equal questioning and grouping, classroom environment, varied multisensory activities, pair/group work, seating, differentiated questions, cultural inclusion).
- Unit 1 (pp.5–11): Getting started on jobs; Reading I a biography of T. S. Eliot (vocabulary: playwright, critic, preparatory, philosophy, hernia, inclination, masterpiece; a year-and-incident table 1888–1965 and short answers, with a list of Eliot's works as the post-reading task); Speaking: introducing others; Listening: two students introducing themselves (answers: bus station, six, school); Grammar: present simple 'be'; Reading II: a message of congratulations (answers: Shijan Tharu, six, NRs. 20,000); Writing: a short biography (Bal Krishna Sama clues) and a congratulations message; Project work: a school registration form; Extra bit: capitalisation.
- Unit 2 (pp.12–18): Getting started on right/wrong behaviour and permission songs; Reading I "Three Simple Rules" (a story of Chulong and a bird that becomes a crow; vocabulary: strange, unseen, imprisonment, shattered, irritated, release, silly, disappear; the three rules as the story's answers); Speaking: school, library and classroom rules with asking/giving/refusing permission; Listening: a head teacher's announcement of school rules; Grammar: yes/no questions (inversion with be, do/does/did with other verbs); Writing: an informal letter (model from Nabina to Neetu, dated 15th March 2022, listing class rules); Reading II: a river poem (answers: river dark brown, sand golden); Project work: notes on Nelson Mandela; Extra bit on full stops.
- Unit 3 (pp.19–26): Getting started on request pictures; Reading I "Beauty and the Beast" (vocabulary from merchant, rose, beast, punishment to castle; true/false key and retelling by "Popcorn Storytelling"); Speaking: requesting and responding (the daughters' requests extracted from the story); Listening: a conversation with Dipak about joining a computer class (answers: Carol, computer, email, the US, waste); Reading II: a sick-leave application; Grammar: modals will/would/can/could and their uses (future information, 2nd-conditional imaginary, requests, permission); Writing: a model sick-leave application (Ram Karki, Jyoti Secondary School, Thankre-9, Dhading, 5th April 2022); Project work: a list of demands to parents in request form; Extra bit: will, would, can, could.
- Unit 4 (pp.27–32): the guide's only eight-task unit. Getting started on festival pictures and birthday wishes; Reading: "Festivals of Nepal", a factual text on five festivals with a completion table (Eid — Muslim community, last day of Ramadan, mosque and namaj; Tihar — Kartik, worship of crows, cows, Goddess Laxmi, oxen and brothers; Chhath — Terai people, Kartik Shukla Shasthi, river banks, offering to the setting and rising sun; Christmas — 25 December, church); Speaking: expressing good wishes by festival; Listening: a description of the Gaura festival with the Deuda dance; Grammar: imperatives made polite with please (key: "Answer the door please", "Don't forget your lunch", "Speak in English please", "Wait for me please"); Writing: punctuation correction and a festival paragraph (model on Dashain: tika, jamara, fifteen days from Ghatasthapana to Purnima); Project work: a local-festival poster.
- Unit 5 (pp.33–40): Getting started on helping people ("I would chase away the dog…") and "If I were a bird…"; Reading I "The Monkeys Go Fasting" (monkeys break their fast over bananas; sentence-ordering key given); Speaking: suggesting (You should… / If I were you, I would… / Why don't you…?); Listening: Suvam advising Ivan about a lost object; Grammar: conditional sentences type-2 (If I were a bird, I would fly in the sky…), with a rewriting key; Reading II: a history-of-computers text (sentence completion key: imagine, internet, microchips, calculator, invented, reported, dependent, smartphones; table key: 1958 Jack Kilby invented the microchip, 1970s home computers, 1980s computer games, 1989 Tim Berners Lee invented the World Wide Web); Writing: type-2 rewrites and an outline story; Extra bit: irregular past forms.
- Unit 6 (pp.41–47): Getting started on intentions with reasons; Reading I the drama "Tom Paints the Fence" (vocabulary: closet, switch, whitewash, fence; keys: Tom persuades Ben and Billy to whitewash, by giving an apple); Speaking: expressing intention with reasons ("I want to go to the dance class because I want to be a dancer"); Listening: an explanation of the Gorkha earthquake; Grammar: connectives because and because of (rule: because + subject and verb; because of + noun phrase) with completion and sentence-joining keys; Reading II "Volleyball: Our National Game" (because / in order to / to key; the court-size answer "because it doesn't need a big space for setting its court"); Writing: a paragraph on a favourite game; Project work: acting out the play.
- Unit 7 (pp.48–55): Getting started on predicting from pictures ("It will rain heavily…"); Reading I "Human and the Environment" (pollution); Reading II a week-long weather forecast (weather vs season game, with the key classifying hot, snowy, windy, sunny and cold as weather and fall, winter, summer, spring — and, questionably, "Rainy" — as season); Speaking: prediction expressions (will, be going to); Listening: a weather update by a news reader (transcript names him Ugin — see Slips); Grammar: will vs be going to vs present continuous for the future (predictions from personal opinion vs present evidence; future facts; arrangements); Writing: describing your school and future plans; Project work: a pollution scrapbook.
- Unit 8 (pp.56–64): Getting started on then-and-now pictures and past memories; Reading I "Gairigaun: Then and Now" (vocabulary: Chautaro, melodious, public, property, with a matching key); Speaking: describing past habits, picking "used to" out of the text; Listening: a village then-and-now description (key: thatched, stretcher, van; all three true/false statements keyed False); Grammar: used to / didn't use to, with two full answer keys (including the "use to" after did forms); Reading II: the Anne Frank timeline text (1929 born; 1933 Hitler to power and the move to Amsterdam; 1934 Dutch learned; 1939…), with a fill-the-table procedure run paragraph by paragraph; Writing: past-habits accounts with used to; Project work: a then-and-now locality display.
- Unit 9 (pp.65–70): Getting started on comparing picture pairs; Reading I a descriptive text on Rara Lake (vocabulary: altitude, amazing, mystery, crystal, magnificent, endangered, solitude, route; matching key: mystery — difficult to understand, magnificent — grand in appearance, endangered — going to die out, solitude — silence); Speaking: degrees of adjectives in questions (Student A/B answer keys: the best, the scariest, faster, the hottest, the worst, more dangerous…); Listening on comparison; Grammar: positive/comparative/superlative forms, regular -er/-est against "irregular" more/most (the guide calls beautiful-type adjectives irregular — see Slips); Reading II: a food menu, comparing the prices of Besisahar Chamena Griha and Deurali Restaurant, Lamjung (key items: 20, French Fries, cold drinks, Rs. 5); Writing/Project work on menus and comparison.
- Unit 10 (pp.71–77): Getting started on rules in zoo, park, home and road pictures; Reading I a brochure, "Rules and Safety Guidelines at Central Zoo" (true/false key; post-reading research task with a sample paragraph: Juddha Samsher's private zoo at Jawalakhel, 1932, government ownership after 1950, about 6 hectares, 942 animals of 127 species); Speaking: expressing obligations and prohibitions; Listening: a cycling conversation (Rehan and Sonam); Grammar: should, have to, must and allowed to (with model sentences and a sentence-making key); Reading II: museum rules; Project work: a chart-paper list of school rules; Extra bit: use of have to, must, should.
- Unit 11 (pp.78–86): Getting started identifying people, places and objects (tailor, teacher, pyramid, the Great Wall, barber, Lumbini); Reading I "Mobile Phones: Uses and Abuses" (vocabulary including tension, queue up, multipurpose, radiation, blurring and, oddly, nephron); Speaking: describing with the present continuous against the present simple; Listening: a text on Plato; Grammar: articles a/an/the together with the present continuous (is/am/are + v-ing rules); Reading II: a smartphone-invention text taught through eleven infographic cards (vocabulary: touchscreen, keyboard, unlock, official, urine, release, powerful, equipment, commercial, suffer, ore); Writing: correcting a punctuation-free paragraph about Alfred Nobel's factories (given and corrected versions printed side by side); Extra bit: Word class I (determiner, pronoun, preposition, conjunction, interjection).
- Unit 12 (pp.87–94): Getting started on apologising (song words: ignorance, hence, exploit, satisfied, hurt, forgive); Reading I a story of a king hit by a stone thrown by an old woman gathering mangoes, who is rewarded instead of punished (sentence-ordering and true/false keys); Speaking: apologising (nurse-and-boy and Srijana-and-Bikas conversations; a card-matching game taken outside the classroom); Listening: an apology for missing Nirmala's birthday party; Grammar: negation (the "Basic Rules of Negation": not/n't after the auxiliary; do/does/did + not with the main verb in base form when there is no auxiliary); Reading II: a flat-hunting text (key: solve, suitable, inspect); Writing: an apology letter/email (sample dated Nov 25th, Kusunti, Lalitpur, to Sanju); Project work and Extra bit on apology expressions.
- Unit 13 (pp.95–100): Getting started on ability (what a hen can and can't do); Reading I "The Baby Quail" (a quail chick saved from a forest fire; short-answer key ending "I feel quite happy about their kindness because they even risked their lives to save the little bird"); Speaking: expressing ability and inability (can/can't); Listening: a telephone conversation booking a doctor's appointment (true/false key; completion key printed with scrambled lettering — see Slips); Grammar: connectives because, since, as, because of (worked examples: "You don't see vehicles on the road because of a strike", "Since she behaves well, everyone wants to meet her"); Writing: reordering jumbled sentences into a titled story, and a paragraph on an interesting event; Reading II: a poem, with a rhyming-word key (hear — tear, fear, bear, near; rest — nest, breast; hay — day, away; tall — all, small; leaf — grief, relief).
- Unit 14 (pp.101–106): Getting started on units of measurement; Reading I a price list ("All in One Store", rice and daal items read aloud by nominated students); Speaking: stating units and quantities (How much / How many question completion key: muffins, coins, music, rain, earrings, toys, water); Listening: fruit and vegetable price lists, then making a stationery price list; Grammar: common nouns (many + plural countable, much + uncountable); Writing: a price-comparison paragraph (sample: the Geometry Box at NRs 150 is the most expensive, the Gel Pen at NRs 30 the cheapest, the Ruler NRs 40, the Marker NRs 50, the Pilot Pen NRs 80); Reading II: an advertisement; Project work: a grocery-store price list; Extra bit: common multiword verbs.
- Unit 15 (pp.107–113): Getting started describing pictures and a poem-song with hard vocabulary (chaos, bosom, vision, unfurled, slumber, Eros, Cronos; rhyming sets world/unfurled/curled/hurled); Reading I "The Musicians of Ilam" — the Bremen-style animal musicians retold with a donkey, dog, cat, cock, a narrator and four thieves as the reading roles (vocabulary: band, musician, crow, gang, bray, frightened, stumble, limp, stab, poke); Speaking: expressing degrees of probability from pictures; Listening: an interview with a meteorologist (Dr. Ali) on the week's weather; Grammar: modals may, might and must for probability (may = more likely, might = less likely or hypothetical, must = strong certainty) plus the simple future; Writing: punctuation rewriting (corrected paragraph about Saroj printed) and a population-growth paragraph; Reading II: the arrow-and-song poem (true/false key: False, False, True).
- Unit 16 (pp.114–122): Getting started recalling memorable events; Reading I "Umar in Disguise" (the Khalifah Umar's character map keyed: tall and well-built, good speaker, responsible, simple habits, kind-hearted, disciplined, fair complexion, ruler; who-said-what key); Speaking: narrating a sequence of events (what you did yesterday); Listening: instructions for making black tea, ordering six process pictures; Grammar: the past tenses in the story — underlining simple past, past continuous and past perfect verbs, tabulating them, and completing sentences in simple past or past continuous; Reading II: a festivals text (the procedure works through the word Ubhauli, "the festival of the Kirant community", and cites the textbook's page 163 — see Cross-check); Writing: a birthday paragraph in the past tense and an outline story; Extra bit: consonant sounds.
- Unit 17 (pp.123–131): Getting started on surprising pictures (a goat in a tree, a student writing with both hands) and sharing surprising events ("I won 1,00,000 in a lottery at the age of sixteen" as the teacher's model); Reading I "A Palm Reader" (vocabulary key: proper, noticed, rags, fortune, took hold of, hesitated, lunatic; multiple-choice work on a traveller who stops in a village for the night); Speaking: expressing surprise and dismay (Hurray! / Oh no! It's bad luck!, with five situation cards and model responses); Listening: a conversation about a Chitwan visit (key: last weekend; Chitwan National Park and Bishajari Taal; No, never; winter vacation); Grammar: past simple, past continuous and past perfect (identification key including "Columbus discovered America on August 3, 1492"); Reading II: a dialogue "At a Chemist" (prescription, drug, out of stock…); Writing: completing a hospital-appointment dialogue (key given) and composing a Lumbini-visit dialogue; Extra bit: vowels.
- Unit 18 (pp.132–139): Getting started asking for and giving information with reasons about family; Reading I a description of a bar graph of Kathmandu spending on computers and smartphones, 2015–2019 (key: 10 million; 35 million; smartphones); Speaking: asking for information and reasons (question-word cards; a make-the-question key: "Which is the tallest mountain in the world?", "What does WHO stand for?", "Which is the longest river in Nepal?"…); Listening: a newcomer asking directions (cinema, clinic, ATM booth, post office); Grammar: wh-questions (forming and rearranging; key: "Where does Mr. Thapa live?", "When was Shakespeare born?", "Whose mobile phone did she use?"…); Writing: describing a bar chart of Nepal's gold medals at the 13th South Asian Games and composing a poem (sample poem "My Good Friend" printed); Reading II: a news story on Gaurika Singh's four gold medals at the 13th South Asian Games (key: on December 10; four gold medals; Women's 100 metres freestyle; also 200 m freestyle, 200 m backstroke, 400 m freestyle), with a sample school-anniversary news story (Patan Secondary School); Project work: a bar graph of boys and girls per class.
- Listening Transcripts (pp.140–143): the full scripts for all eighteen units — Unit 1 Manju and Ranjan's self-introductions; Unit 2 the New Nepal Secondary School rules announcement (winter schedule 10:00 am–3:00 pm, no junk food, hand sanitizer, no ornaments, compulsory uniform, separate dustbins); Unit 3 Carol and Dipak on computer class and email; Unit 4 the Gaura festival description; Unit 5 Suvam and Ivan on suggestions; Unit 6 the earthquake explanation (focus and epicenter); Unit 7 the weather update (Biratnagar 32/19°C, Janakpur 34/23, Kathmandu 26/13 with 25 mm of rainfall, Pokhara 27/12 with 20 mm, Butwal 33/21, Birendranagar 25/13); Unit 8 the village then-and-now description; Unit 9 the shopping/order dialogue; Unit 10 Rehan and Sonam on the cycling course (starts 17 September, 7.00 am, arrive by 6.55); Unit 11 the life of Plato; Unit 12 the birthday-party apology; Unit 13 the doctor's-appointment telephone call; Unit 14 a price list; Unit 15 the Dr. Ali meteorologist interview; Unit 16 how to prepare traditional black tea; Unit 17 Suman and Shanta on Chitwan and Bis Hajari Taal; Unit 18 the directions conversation.

### Grammar spine
As the guide frames it, the eighteen units present grammar in this order: present simple 'be' (Unit 1) → yes/no questions by inversion and do/does/did (Unit 2) → modals will/would/can/could for future, imaginary situations, requests and permission (Unit 3) → imperative sentences and polite imperatives with please (Unit 4) → conditional sentences type-2 (Unit 5) → connectives because and because of (Unit 6) → will vs be going to vs present continuous for prediction and future (Unit 7) → used to / didn't use to for past habits (Unit 8) → degrees of adjectives: positive, comparative, superlative, -er/-est against more/most (Unit 9) → obligation and prohibition: should, have to, must, allowed to (Unit 10) → articles a/an/the with the present continuous (Unit 11) → negation with not/n't and do-support (Unit 12) → connectives because, since, as, because of (Unit 13) → common nouns with many/much (Unit 14) → modals may, might, must for probability, with the simple future (Unit 15) → past simple, past continuous and past perfect identified in a story (Unit 16) → past simple, past continuous and past perfect used and distinguished (Unit 17) → wh-questions (Unit 18). Capitalisation (Unit 1 Extra bit), punctuation (Units 4 and 15) and word classes (Unit 11 Extra bit) run as self-study extras.

### Honest negatives
- The guide contains nothing for the held Grade 6 student's book: no unit, text, exercise or page of that book is mentioned anywhere in it (see Cross-check). As a companion to the library's Grade 6 set it is unusable.
- No ISBN, no price, no printer and no copyright year line are printed in the guide — the imprint gives only the publisher and "Edition: 2022".
- The guide never prints the textbook's reading texts themselves: a teacher without the (new) student's book cannot reconstruct the Eliot biography, "Three Simple Rules", "Beauty and the Beast" or any other text from the guide, only their vocabulary lists, questions and keys.
- Answer keys are uneven: most comprehension and grammar exercises are keyed, but the speaking, project and most writing tasks have at most one sample answer, and some keys are defective (see Slips). There is no marking scheme, test bank or examination preparation anywhere in the guide.
- The guide assumes recorded (mp3) listening material throughout ("Recorded material (mp3) and audio player" in nearly every unit's materials list) but the audio itself is not part of this book; only the transcripts on pp.140–143 are supplied.

### Slips and errors as printed
- Preface (unnumbered, PDF 3): "Teacher's Guide is a complimentary material for providing training to teachers" — "complimentary" (free / flattering) for complementary; the same preface prints "The curriculum Development Centre cordially requests…" with the stray capital.
- Unit 1 key (p.7): the Eliot year table keys "1916 AD — completed PhD (from Harvard)"; Eliot completed his doctoral dissertation in 1916 but did not take the degree (he did not return for the oral examination), so "completed PhD" is wrong as printed.
- Unit 7 key (p.52): the weather-or-season key classifies "Rainy: season" while classifying snowy, windy, sunny, hot and cold as weather — inconsistent with the guide's own distinction in the same exercise.
- Unit 9 grammar (p.69): adjectives like beautiful are labelled "Irregular Adjectives like: beautiful, more beautiful and the most beautiful" — the more/most pattern is the regular pattern for long adjectives, not an irregularity, as framed here.
- Unit 11 vocabulary (p.80): the mobile-phones word list the teacher is told not to miss includes "nephron" — a kidney term with no place in the phone text's vocabulary (the list runs "tension, queue up, multipurpose, giant, abuse, overlook, convenience, access, essential, invention, radiation, blurring, invisible and nephron").
- Unit 12 ordering key (p.89): the key's lettering is scrambled as printed — items are keyed "a. b. The king took a rest under the shade of a mango tree", "b. f. Suddenly, the king got hit…", "c. a. The securities went to find the person…", so the exercise letters and the answer letters cannot be read off without reconstructing the mapping; the same key prints "The securities" for the security guards.
- Unit 13 listening key (p.98): the completion answers are lettered "b. office / b. 5:30 p.m. (in the evening) / c. register" — two items lettered b and no item a as printed.
- Listening transcript, Unit 7 (p.141) vs the unit body (p.53): the body calls the weather reader "Yugin, a news reader"; the transcript opens "Good evening. It's me Ugin." — the name is spelled two ways.
- Listening transcript, Unit 11 (p.142): the Plato script prints "He once was sold as a slave by an Athenian king. He later became the student of another great intellectual, Socrates." — the order is reversed (Plato was Socrates' student before the Syracuse episode), and it prints "the Academy was founded in 385 B. C." and "closed in 529 C. E. by emperor Justian" — "Justian" for Justinian.
- Unit 18 sample news story (p.139): the model story is datelined "Lalitpur, Sep. 15" and then reports the school "celebrated its 97th Anniversary yesterday on 14th August, 2021" — September and August disagree inside the model students are told to copy.
- Unit 16 procedure (p.120): the teacher is told the word Ubhauli "is in the second paragraph on page 163" — a textbook page reference far beyond anything in the held student's book, and one of the clearest internal proofs that this guide belongs to a different, longer textbook.

### Copy completeness and cross-check
- Folio verdict: complete. Printed folios run 1–143 unbroken on PDF pp.5–147, preceded by cover, imprint, Preface and Table of Contents; no missing or duplicated leaves; the file ends with the Unit 18 listening transcript on p.143.
- Contents vs actual units: exact — the Table of Contents (Introduction 1; Units 1–18 at pp.5, 12, 19, 27, 33, 41, 48, 56, 65, 71, 78, 87, 95, 101, 107, 114, 123, 132; Listening Transcripts 140) matches every unit opening in the body, in number and page, and transcripts for all eighteen units are present on pp.140–143.
- Cross-check vs the held Grade 6 Student's Book: **total disagreement — the two books are not a pair.** The student's book (first edition 1994, revised edition 2012, ISBN 978-9937-601-00-9) has seventeen syllabus units plus a listening script and vocabulary section; its units are Some birds and animals, A brave female pilot, Wit, A field trip, A real hero, The earth and us, The world cup, A lesson from a grass cutter, Fewa Lake, Kites, Automated Teller Machine and (in the held copy) the Stephenson unit. The guide teaches an eighteen-unit book whose units are the Eliot biography, Three Simple Rules, Beauty and the Beast, Festivals of Nepal, The Monkeys Go Fasting, Tom Paints the Fence, Human and the Environment, Gairigaun: Then and Now, Rara Lake, Central Zoo rules, Mobile Phones, The Baby Quail, a price list, The Musicians of Ilam, Umar in Disguise, A Palm Reader and a bar-graph unit. Not one reading text, exercise, answer key or transcript in the guide matches the student's book; the guide's task architecture (nine tasks, Getting started → Extra bit) does not exist in the student's book (Look and answer → Test yourself); the guide's references to textbook pages (e.g. "page 163", and a textbook whose Unit 8 Reading II sits on its p.80) cannot be followed in the student's book at all. The single shared title is "The Musicians of Ilam": Unit 14, p.119 in the student's book's Contents (a unit the held student's copy does not contain) and Reading I of Unit 15 in the guide's book, where the story is the animal-musicians tale the guide summarises. Conclusion for the record: the held guide is the 2022 CDC guide to the current eighteen-unit Grade 6 English textbook; the held student's book is the 2012 revised edition of the 1994 book. They should not be filed as companions.

## Grade 7 — ENGLISH Grade 7 (student's book)

### Identity block
- Title as printed: cover and title page (PDF p.1) "ENGLISH / Grade 7", under "Government of Nepal / Ministry of Education, Science and Technology / Curriculum Development Centre / Sanothimi, Bhaktapur". The record's wording "English Grade 7 (Final Updated Edition 2025)" is not a printed title: no words "Final" appear anywhere; the edition claim itself is printed on the imprint page (see below). The running footer throughout is "English Grade 7".
- Publisher: Government of Nepal, Ministry of Education, Science and Technology, Curriculum Development Centre, Sanothimi, Bhaktapur (cover, imprint). © Curriculum Development Centre. Printed at Janak Education Materials Centre, Sanothimi, Bhaktapur. "Price Rs.:" is printed with the amount left blank.
- Year / edition / ISBN as printed: imprint (front-matter p.2) prints "First Edition: 2022" and "Updated Edition: 2025" — so the record's 2025 updated-edition claim is TRUE as printed. The Preface is signed "2025 AD / Curriculum Development Centre". No ISBN is printed anywhere in the book (checked whole file).
- Developers/authors as printed: Preface (p.3) — the book was "initially prepared by a team that includes Mr. Shankar Adhikari, Mr. Matrika Subedi, Ms. Shikha Gurung and Mr. Narad Rijal", and "revised by a team comprising of Ms. Ramita Prajapati, Mr. Devraj Karki, Dr. Neil David Rose, Mr. Nim Prakash Singh Ratthour and Mr. Nabin Kumar Khadka". Director General Mr. Yubaraj Paudel; subject committee chairperson Mr. Ana Prasad Neupane; committee chair Prof. Dr. Jib Lal Sapkota with Profs./Drs. Rishi Ram Rijal, Gopal Prasad Pandey, Madhav Prasad Ghimire, Maiya Niraula Pokharel and Tuka Raj Adhikari. "The content and the language of this updated version are edited by Mr. Binod Raj Bhatta and Mr. Bijaya Kumar Ranabhat." Illustrations by Ms. Shaili Malla and Kushal Adhikari; layout by Mr. Shreehari Shrestha and Mr. Khados Sunuwar; updated-version layout by Mr. Bhakta Bahadur Karki.
- Curriculum/series notes: Preface states the book addresses "the main aims of the Basic Level (Grade 6-8) English Curriculum", aligns with "the National Curriculum Framework for School Education, 2076" and is "fully developed in accordance with the new Basic Level English Curriculum, 2077". Every unit follows the same skeleton: Getting started (song/chant), Reading I, Pronunciation, Grammar I, Listening, Speaking, Reading II, Grammar II, Writing, Project work, Extra bit (order varies slightly).
- Extent: 228 PDF pages = front matter printed pp.1–6 (PDF 1–6: cover p.1, imprint p.2, Preface p.3, Contents pp.4–6) + body printed pp.1–222 (PDF 7–228). The body's last text is the Glossary (printed pp.212–220); printed pp.221–222 (PDF 227–228) carry only their folios — two blank leaves. (The text file's 229th segment is an empty trailing split, not a page.)
- Printed↔PDF offset: the front matter is paginated 1–6 in the same folio series style, then the body RESTARTS at printed 1. For all body pages, printed = PDF − 6. Established from the Contents (Unit 1 at p.1) and the printed folios: Unit 1 opens on PDF 7 with folio "English Grade 7 1", and the offset holds on every page checked through the Glossary end (printed 220 = PDF 226). All page numbers below are PRINTED pages.

### Unit-by-unit summary
- Unit 1 (pp.1–9): Reading I is a job-interview dialogue (Mike interviews John for a Finance Department post; John is a part-time clerk). Pronunciation: initial sounds /p f θ ð k tʃ/. Speaking: introducing others. Grammar I: a/an/the. Listening: introductions (audio only). Reading II: a hostel interview notice (Jyoti Girls Hostel). Grammar II: be (is/am/are, negatives, questions). Writing: an introduction dialogue; a hotel check-in form. Project: a club notice. Extra bit: how to read a dictionary entry.
- Unit 2 (pp.10–21): permission dialogues (can/may). Reading I: the folk tale "The Small Bird's Wisdom" — a rich man frees a talking bird for three pieces of advice, then foolishly believes its joke about a diamond inside it. Poem Reading II: "May I go now?" (Susan A. Jackson) — an old speaker asking leave to die. Grammar I: countable/uncountable nouns. Grammar II: a/an/some/any, how many/how much. Listening: a workplace conversation about lunch breaks and dress code. Writing: an email asking leave; the parts of a personal letter; a letter to a sister asking permission for a school tour. Extra bit: homophone pairs (air–heir, principal–principle, etc.).
- Unit 3 (pp.22–34): requests. Reading I: "Kisa Gotami and the Mustard Seed" — a bereaved mother learns from the Buddha's impossible errand (a house where no one has died) that death is universal. Pronunciation: tongue twisters. Listening/Speaking: invitation and request dialogues; making and responding to requests. Grammar I–II: will/would/can/could for requests and ability (present and past). Reading II: a letter to the editor of The Rising Nepal on air pollution (Nitin Rai). Writing: a letter to an editor/ward chair about a local problem. Project: collecting requests from school notices. Extra bit: a tailor–customer dialogue.
- Unit 4 (pp.35–45): festivals and wishes. Reading I: "Some Festivals of Nepal" — Lhosar (Tamu, Sonam, Gyalbo), Gai Jatra, Buddha Jayanti. Writing I: describing a festival. Grammar I: I wish + past form. Listening: three people's birthdays (audio). Reading II: an email (Niru to Sangita) describing a surprise birthday party. Grammar II: simple past. Speaking: giving wishes. Writing II: a paragraph about a school celebration last year. Project: greeting cards. Extra bit: the three-wishes folk tale (banana wishes wasted).
- Unit 5 (pp.46–55): suggestions. Reading I: "The Three Merchants" — a dishonest merchant tricks the bathhouse keeper out of the partners' money bag; a judge upholds the "all three together" condition. Writing I: a thank-you message. Grammar I: suggestion forms (should, How about…, If I were you…, Why don't you…). Reading II: "Food Habits" — the body as a machine needing the right fuel; balanced diet. Grammar II: second (II) conditional. Writing II: a paragraph on one's own food habits; a poster message. Project: a list of food-habit suggestions. Extra bit: the poem "I will Soar".
- Unit 6 (pp.56–65): needs and wants. Reading I: "My Holiday Plan" — a girl plans yoga classes and gardening for the summer holiday. Grammar I: present simple (affirmative, question, negative forms table). Listening: needs vs wants (audio). Reading II: the poem "The Circus Elephant" (John Forster). Grammar II: first (I) conditional. Speaking: wants for future jobs with conditionals. Writing: a holiday essay; a past-tense story completion (the farmer's hidden treasure). Project: a needs/wants survey table. Extra bit: "Controlling Anger" (the nails-in-the-fence story).
- Unit 7 (pp.66–76): opinions. Opening poem: Wendy Cope's flat-earth argument poem. Reading I: "Fast Food" — an opinion essay against junk food. Pronunciation: phonemic transcriptions of five words. Grammar I: to-infinitive of purpose. Writing I / Extra bit: punctuation (tick the correct sentence; a punctuation-marks table). Reading II: the speech "The Pen is Mightier than the Sword". Speaking: opinion expressions. Grammar II: present simple vs present continuous. Writing II: an opinion paragraph on homework. Project: a class speech competition.
- Unit 8 (pp.77–87): the past. Reading I: "A Trip to a Village" (adapted from Longman's Basic Comprehension Passages) — three friends' car breaks down short of a mountain village; a night in the car. Pronunciation: -ed endings /t d ɪd/. Grammar I: connectives and/but/although/however. Reading II: an autobiographical text on Anuradha Koirala and Maiti Nepal (CNN Hero 2010). Grammar II: used to for past habits. Speaking/Writing: past-habit talk; a paragraph about a vacation; the crane-and-fish story from points. Project: interviewing an elderly person about past habits. Extra bit: the greedy-dog-and-bone story in past forms.
- Unit 9 (pp.88–96): comparison. Reading I: two bar charts of smartphone ownership 2011–2016 with a model interpretation text. Grammar I–II: comparative and superlative adjectives, then comparative/superlative adverbs; as…as comparisons. Listening: comparisons of people and of Los Angeles vs San Francisco (audio). Writing I: interpreting a Nepal population table. Reading II: Emily Dickinson's "A Bird, came down the Walk". Writing II: a similarities-and-differences paragraph. Project: a bar chart of school enrolment. Extra bit: comparing two pictures.
- Unit 10 (pp.97–106): obligations, rules, prohibitions. Reading I: a COVID-19 household-isolation leaflet (WHO-style poster text). Writing I: a safety leaflet. Listening: a grandmother-and-girl cooking dialogue (audio). Reading II: the Central Zoo (Jawalakhel, Lalitpur) visitor rules. Grammar: can/could/be allowed to/must/have to/mustn't/don't have to, including deduction uses (can't be, must be, might have gone). Speaking: reading signs with must/mustn't. Writing II: school/library rules. Project: COVID-19 rules display. Extra bit: "The Four Smart Students" (the burst-tyre re-test story).
- Unit 11 (pp.107–117): biography and description. Reading I: a biography of Dr. Sanduk Ruit (born 1954, Olangchunggola; Tilganga; Ramon Magsaysay Award 2006; Padma Shri 2018). Writing I: writing a biography from clues. Grammar I: relative clauses with who/which/where. Listening: describing objects (audio). Reading II: a description of Kathmandu (founded 723; Kasthamandap; 2015 earthquake). Grammar II: forming Wh- questions. Writing II: describing one's village/town. Project: a poster about a famous person. Extra bit: adjective vs adverb choice.
- Unit 12 (pp.118–127): appearances. Reading I: "Differences between People" — inherited hair, skin and growth differences. Pronunciation: plural endings /s z ɪz/. Grammar I: look vs look like (and be like / like). Listening: Tom and his father describing friends (audio). Speaking: appearance vocabulary table. Writing I: a paragraph describing a friend. Reading II: W. B. Rands' poem "Wonderful World". Grammar II: present simple practice. Writing II: a typical-day paragraph. Project: appearance word pictures. Extra bit: "The Stag and its Reflection".
- Unit 13 (pp.128–141): ability and reasons. Reading I: "A Terrifying Night" — a ceiling collapses onto the narrator's empty bed at his uncle's cottage. Grammar I: can/could for ability (present and past); because vs because of. Listening: a can/can't activity grid (audio). Speaking: "Can you…?" class survey; jobs and abilities matching. Writing: reordering "The Hare and the Tortoise"; writing the thirsty-crow story from hints. Reading II: William Blake's poem "The Echoing Green". Grammar II: because/because of again. Project: a professions can/can't table. Extra bit: a Xinhua sports report, "Nepal Wins Three Nations Football Tournament".
- Unit 14 (pp.142–153): quantity and measurement. Reading I: "Mt. K2" — a description of K2 (8,611 m; 1856 survey; 1954 first ascent). Writing I: describing Rara Lake from an idea map. Grammar I: concord (subject–verb agreement: one of my friends is…, twenty dollars is…, police were…). Listening: Panama Canal statistics (audio). Reading II: a school prospectus (Saraswati Secondary School, Ilam). Speaking/Writing: How tall/how much/how far questions; designing a school prospectus. Grammar II: quantity questions. Extra bit (the unit's grammar in the Contents): some/any and many, several, (a) few, a little, much, a bit of — presented only here, as study sentences.
- Unit 15 (pp.154–165): probability and the future. Reading I: "Climate Change" (a NASA-style explainer: greenhouse effect, ~2 °F warming in 100 years). Grammar I: simple future vs future perfect. Listening: a weather report (audio). Reading II: the poem "Gratefulness" (Joseph T. Renaldi). Speaking: will vs will have done exchanges. Writing II: a paragraph on "The Future of the Earth". Grammar II: may/might/must for deduction about the present. Project: interviewing elders about change. Extra bit: may/might/could/will/should/must/can't deduction sentences.
- Unit 16 (pp.166–178): narrating past events. Reading I: "A Lesson from a Son" in substance (no title is printed over the story) — Velan the carpenter ill-treats his father Kuppan; his son Mithu carves him a wooden plate and shames him into reform. Writing I: story reordering (the lion and the mouse) and story development (the bundle of sticks). Grammar I: past continuous. Listening: a school-day conversation (audio). Reading II: "A Party in a Restaurant" — a birthday supper defeated by a tourist invasion; only an omelette left. Speaking: past-continuous exchanges. Grammar II: past perfect. Project: a storyboard. Extra bit: picture sequencing in the past tense.
- Unit 17 (pp.179–188): surprise and dismay. Opening song: Roald Dahl's clever-pig poem (the byline is printed "Rolad Dahl"). Reading I: "Fair Shares" — Ali tricks his brother Abraham out of the cow's milk and the date-tree's juice until Abraham beats the cow's front half and starts felling the tree's trunk. Grammar I: past simple vs past continuous vs past perfect in one exercise set (the book's consolidation point). Listening: a prize-weekend conversation (audio). Reading II: "At the Hotel", a guest–receptionist dialogue (a slow watch; expensive rooms). Speaking: expressions of surprise and dismay. Grammar II: rewriting a wolf-and-girl story in past simple/continuous. Writing: punctuation correction of a story extract; a picnic dialogue. Project: harvesting surprise expressions from a film. Extra bit: surprise expressions explained.
- Unit 18 (pp.189–198): confirmation. Reading I: a hotel/restaurant menu (the Boat restaurant; the menu itself is a picture — its text is not in the text layer, and the questions test it). Writing I: making a menu. Grammar I–II: question tags (two passes, matching and completing, then tag-spotting in sentences). Listening: Hana and Daniel on cooking and eating out (audio). Reading II: "A Visit to a Market" — a festive-season market ordeal. Speaking: confirming with tags. Writing II: an email about a market visit. Project: restaurant menus with tag questions. Extra bit: classmate statements checked with tags.
- Unit 19 (pp.199–211): facts, truths and instructions. Reading I: "Elephants" — African vs Asian elephants, tusks, matriarchal herds. Grammar I: reporting statements — universal truths and facts keep the present tense in reported speech ("The teacher told us that the Sun sets in the west"). Listening: a voice message (audio) and dictation. Writing I: short messages (birthday, New Year, congratulations, lost coat, changed practice day). Reading II: "Use of a Personal Computer" — operating instructions (power button, F1–F12, Home/End/Delete/Backspace, Ctrl+V, shutdown). Grammar II: zero conditional. Writing II: instructions (hand-washing matching; writing teeth-brushing instructions). Project: an operation-manual display. Extra bit: human-body facts (from Nat Geo Kids).
- Glossary (pp.212–220): an A–Y alphabetical word list with phonemic transcription, part of speech and a short definition for each entry (no Z section; the last entry is "Yell").

### Grammar spine
Articles a/an/the and the be verb (U1) → countable/uncountable nouns with a/an/some/any and how many/how much (U2) → will/would/can/could for requests, can/could for past and present ability (U3) → I wish + past form; simple past (U4) → suggestion forms with should; second conditional (U5) → present simple in full (statements, questions, negatives); first conditional (U6) → to-infinitive of purpose; present simple vs present continuous (U7) → connectives and/but/although/however; used to (U8) → comparative and superlative adjectives, then adverbs, with as…as (U9) → obligation/permission modals: can, could, be allowed to, must, have to, mustn't, don't have to, plus modal deduction (U10) → relative clauses who/which/where; Wh- question formation (U11) → look/look like (U12) → can/could ability revisited with because/because of (U13) → subject–verb concord; quantity words some/any/many/several/(a) few/a little/much/a bit of (U14) → simple future vs future perfect; may/might/must deduction (U15) → past continuous; past perfect (U16) → past simple/continuous/perfect consolidation (U17) → question tags (U18) → reporting statements (truths keep the present tense); zero conditional (U19). Punctuation is taught as a writing strand in U7 and U17, not in the grammar slots.

### Honest negatives
- No passive voice anywhere: the word "passive" never occurs and no passive structure is taught or drilled (checked across the whole text).
- No reported questions, commands or requests, and no backshift teaching: U19 reports statements only, and only to show that facts/truths stay in the present tense.
- No present perfect or present perfect continuous as a topic (forms occur untaught inside readings, e.g. "I have been working part-time", U1); no past perfect continuous, no future continuous.
- No answer key, no tests or exam practice, and no tapescripts: every Listening task depends on audio that is not printed in the book.
- No phonics programme: pronunciation is a small slot per unit (initial sounds, -ed endings, plural endings, word stress, the letter 'r') and several units reuse the "listen to your teacher" formula.
- The Glossary defines words in English only — there is no Nepali translation anywhere in the book.

### Slips and errors as printed
- p.37 (Reading I, U4): "Kheer (sweet porridge) in cooked in every household during Buddha Jayanti" — "in" for "is".
- p.68 and contents p.4 (U7): "phenomic transcription" for "phonemic transcription" (twice on p.68: "The phenomic transcription of the words… as per the phenomic transcription").
- p.79 (U8, exercise E): "Write all the past forms of verbs from the above story 'The First day of Holiday'" — the story on pp.78–79 is titled "A Trip to a Village"; no text of that name exists in the book.
- p.119 (U12): "European people, in general, have paler skin if their ancestors come from Africa or Asia" — the sense is reversed (pale skin is being explained by the wrong ancestry).
- p.179 (U17): the clever-pig poem is signed "Rolad Dahl" for Roald Dahl.
- p.183 (U17, Grammar I): "France …… the football World Cup in 2012" — there was no World Cup in 2012 (France won in 2018).
- Glossary p.212: "Cafeteria … a place where you collect and pay or your food before you eat it" — "or" for "for". p.217: "Obviously /ˈɒbviəsli/ (n.)" — wrong part of speech. p.220: "Underprivileged /ʌndəˈprɪvəlɪdʒd/ (anj.)" — "(anj.)" for "(adj.)"; the same list labels "Abroad" a noun (p.212).
- p.2 imprint: "Price Rs.:" with no price printed. Contents p.5's folio is printed at the foot of a jumbled two-column layout in the PDF text order, but the contents entries themselves match the book (see below).

### Copy completeness and cross-check
- Folio verdict: complete. Front matter folios run 1–6 (PDF 1–6); body folios run 1–222 unbroken (PDF 7–228) with no missing or duplicated leaves; printed pp.221–222 are blank folio-only leaves after the Glossary, so the file ends cleanly rather than truncating.
- Contents vs actual: exact. All 19 contents unit rows match the units in number, reading titles and starting pages (1, 10, 22, 35, 46, 56, 66, 77, 88, 97, 107, 118, 128, 142, 154, 166, 179, 189, 199), and the Contents' "Glossary 212" matches the Glossary's start. The grammar topics named in the Contents match what each unit teaches, with one softness: U14's quantity words appear only in the Extra bit (p.153), not in a Grammar slot.
- Picture-only matter verified: the U18 menu (p.190) is an image with no text layer — the filing notes it rather than treating the page as blank.
- Cross-check vs companion: no Grade 7 teacher's guide exists in the record, so no cross-check is possible; nothing in this book refers to one.

## Grade 8 — ENGLISH Grade 8 (student's book)

### Identity block
- Title as printed: cover and title page (PDF p.1) "ENGLISH / Grade 8", under "Government of Nepal / Ministry of Education, Science and Technology / Curriculum Development Centre / Sanothimi, Bhaktapur". The record's wording "English Grade 8 (Updated Edition 2025)" is not a printed title; the edition claim is printed on the imprint page (see below). The running footer throughout is "English Grade 8". Units are not given theme titles inside the body — each unit opens with its number only (e.g. "1 Unit"); the descriptive unit titles exist only in the Contents.
- Publisher: Government of Nepal, Ministry of Education, Science and Technology, Curriculum Development Centre, Sanothimi, Bhaktapur (cover, imprint). © Curriculum Development Centre. Printed at Janak Education Materials Centre, Sanothimi, Bhaktapur. "Price Rs.:" is printed with the amount left blank.
- Year / edition / ISBN as printed: imprint (front-matter p.2) prints "First Edition: 2023" and "Updated Edition: 2025" — so the record's 2025 updated-edition claim is TRUE as printed. The Preface is signed "2025 AD / Curriculum Development Centre". No ISBN is printed anywhere in the book (checked whole file).
- Developers/authors as printed: Preface (p.3) — "This book is prepared by a team that includes Ms. Krishna Kumari Upadhayaya, Ms. Mallika Joshi Shrestha, Mr. Matrika Subedi, and Mr. Pushpa Raj Paudel." Director General Mr. Yubaraj Paudel and Mr. Baikuntha Prasad Aryal; subject committee chairperson Professor Dr. Bal Mukunda Bhandari, members Dr. Bamdev Adhikari, Dr. Netra Prasad Sharma, Mr. Basanta Raj Dhakal, Mr. Nawaraj Sapkota; contributions also from Mr. Tukaraj Adhikari and Mr. Shankar Adhikari. Each unit follows the same skeleton: Getting started, Reading I, Pronunciation, Speaking, Grammar I, Listening, Reading II, Grammar II, Writing, Project work, Extra bit (order varies).
- Extent: 262 PDF pages = front matter printed pp.1–8 (PDF 1–8: cover p.1, imprint p.2, Preface p.3, a blank folio-only p.4, Contents pp.5–8) + body printed pp.1–254 (PDF 9–262). The last content page is printed p.253 (U19 Extra bit); printed p.254 (PDF 262) carries only its folio — a blank leaf. There is no Glossary in this book. (The text file's 263rd segment is an empty trailing split, not a page.)

Established from the Contents (Unit 1 at p.1) and the printed folios: Unit 1 opens on PDF 9 with folio 1, and the offset holds on every page checked through printed 254 (PDF 262). All page numbers below are PRINTED pages.

### Unit-by-unit summary
- Unit 1 (pp.1–12): Reading I is a school notice with timetable, "A Tour to Central Zoo" (Saraswati Niketan Secondary School). Pronunciation: word stress. Speaking/Grammar I: asking for information with can/could/would; modal yes/no questions. Listening: a school timetable (audio). Reading II: "A Request Letter" — Grade 8 students ask their headteacher for more computer-lab facilities. Grammar II: present simple (repetition). Writing: interpreting a sports timetable; filling in a hostel registration form. Project: designing a class timetable. Extra bit: dictionary abbreviations.
- Unit 2 (pp.13–24): permission. Reading I: "A Father's Letter to his Son" — a boy travelling alone by train is frightened until he opens his father's envelope: his father is in the next compartment. Pronunciation: -ed endings /t d ɪd/. Speaking: asking/giving/refusing permission. Grammar I: forming yes/no questions from statements (all auxiliaries). Writing I: skeleton-story writing (the shoemaker and the elves outline). Reading II: the poem "Changing World" (attributed to Edward de Bono) — change yourself first. Listening: a permission conversation (audio). Writing II: an email asking permission to use the hall, and a headteacher's reply. Extra bit: permission expressions table.
- Unit 3 (pp.25–36): Reading I: "Public Announcements" — a Nepal Airlines pre-flight announcement and a school Parents Day announcement. Pronunciation: the letter 'r'. Writing I: writing an announcement. Speaking: making requests and responding. Listening: a cinema trip conversation (audio). Grammar I–II: reported speech for yes/no questions (asked if/whether, with backshift). Reading II: "How to Wash Clothes in a Washing Machine" — a seven-step instruction text. Project: writing instructions for a cultural food item. Extra bit: commas, semicolons and colons.
- Unit 4 (pp.37–50): Reading I: "A Visit to Mustang", a memoir (adapted from Phoenix Yu) — Pokhara, Tatopani, Kagbeni, Muktinath, Marpha. Pronunciation: diphthongs. Grammar I–II: present perfect vs past simple (two full passes). Listening: a volunteer's memories (audio). Writing I: a memorable-event account. Speaking: talking about experiences with present perfect. Reading II: "A Diary Entry" — Neil's day trip to a village in Dhading (dated Saturday, 5 March 2022). Writing II: a diary entry. Project: voting on diary entries. Extra bit: compound words (open, closed, hyphenated).
- Unit 5 (pp.51–63): persuasion. Reading I: "The Old Woman and the Lime Tree" (adapted from a Ukrainian folk tale) — a talking tree grants escalating wishes until the couple's greed loses everything. Pronunciation: syllable counting. Grammar I–II: reported speech for imperatives, requests, advice and warnings (told/asked/advised/warned + to-infinitive). Reading II: the poem "Free Birds" (students urged to fly). Listening: planning a tour (audio). Speaking: expressions for persuading. Writing: punctuation repair; a crow-and-peacock-feathers skeleton story. Project: collecting shopkeepers' persuasion expressions. Extra bit: prefixes and suffixes.
- Unit 6 (pp.64–78): getting things done. Reading I: "Traditional Marriage Customs around the World" — Indian, Japanese, Jamaican and Welsh weddings. Pronunciation: -s/es endings /ɪz z s/. Grammar I: causative verbs — have/get something done; make/let/have/get someone do. Writing I: describing a wedding in one's own culture. Listening: booking an appointment (audio). Speaking: what people let/make/get you to do. Reading II: "Life Saving Inventions" — Hippo-Rollers, LifeStraw, AidPod. Grammar II: subject–verb agreement. Writing II: paraphrasing (a model pair, then a dog paragraph). Project: an inventions collage. Extra bit: British vs American vocabulary.
- Unit 7 (pp.79–89): Reading I: a "Weather Forecast" table and text (Nepal stations, maxima/minima/rainfall). Pronunciation: weather phrases. Grammar I: future simple will vs be going to (degree of certainty). Speaking: predictions. Listening: a weather forecast (audio). Reading II: "Migration" — push and pull factors, benefits and drawbacks. Grammar II: future perfect. Writing: punctuating a paragraph (the same "Nitin" text recycled from Grade 7); paragraphs on Environment. Project: a picture forecast of local change. Extra bit: British/American spelling variations.
- Unit 8 (pp.90–104): Reading I: "Having Fun" — a father teaches his son that real fun is giving, by putting coins in a poor farmer's shoes. Pronunciation: -ed endings again. Grammar I: past simple vs past continuous vs past perfect (choice and completion exercises). Writing I: completing the misdirected-email skeleton story; the starfish skeleton story. Listening: an activity-week survey (audio). Reading II: "Historical Inventions" — steam engine, motor car, electricity, aeroplane, spaceship, radio/TV, telephone/mobile. Grammar II: used to for past habits. Writing II: a narrative essay about a ceremony. Project: interviewing elders about past habits. Extra bit: idioms.
- Unit 9 (pp.105–115): comparison. Reading I: "The Leap between High School and College" — a compare-and-contrast essay (cost, workload, social life). Pronunciation: college vocabulary. Grammar I: comparative and superlative adjectives. Speaking: comparison questions. Listening: frogs vs toads (audio). Writing I: comparing Bhujung and Bhada villages from notes. Reading II: "Smartphones in our Life" — an essay on dependence and retrieval habits. Grammar II: comparative and superlative adverbs. Writing II: a before/after-internet comparison essay. Project: a smartphone-use survey chart. Extra bit: irregular adjectives and adverbs table.
- Unit 10 (pp.116–127): invitations. Reading I: a UN Women "Vacancy Announcement" (ICT and Administrative Support; Driver; closing date 21 Nov. 2021). Pronunciation: -tion and -sion sounds. Writing I: a vacancy advertisement. Grammar I: prepositions of place and time. Listening: a party invitation conversation (audio; the friend lives at "104 Supaive …"). Speaking: making invitations and responding. Reading II: an informal invitation email (Rahim invites Pratik to a business-opening ceremony) and a formal Bratabandha invitation card. Grammar II: question tags, including correcting wrong tags. Writing II: an invitation message. Project: a birthday invitation card. Extra bit: inviting/accepting/declining expressions.
- Unit 11 (pp.128–144): Reading I: "Sir Isaac Newton" — a biography (apple, gravitation, telescopes, absent-mindedness anecdotes). Pronunciation: tongue twisters with -ch words. Speaking: describing people, places and objects. Grammar I: relative clauses (who, where, whose, which, that, when). Writing I: a biography of Pasang Lhamu Sherpa from data. Reading II: "Khaptad National Park" — a leaflet-style description with entry fees and park regulations. Writing II: a leaflet and rules for a local place. Grammar II: articles a/an/the. Listening: Sirubari homestay tourism (audio). Project: describing a famous place of the province. Extra bit: informal expressions (gonna, wanna, gotta…).
- Unit 12 (pp.145–156): likes, dislikes, preferences. Reading I: "Nepal's Bird Man" — a memoir of ornithologist Hari Sharan Nepali 'Kazi Dai' (adapted from Rajendra N. Suwal, Nepali Times). Pronunciation: 'ch' sounds. Speaking: likes/dislikes expressions. Grammar I: verb + -ing vs verb + to-infinitive (like/love/hate both; mind, avoid, admit + -ing; want, would like + to). Writing: an essay "My Hobbies and Interests". Listening: preferences (audio). Reading II: Kenn Nesbitt's poem "My Teacher Ate my Homework". Grammar II: forming yes/no and Wh- questions. Project: a hobby poem/song. Extra bit: -ology words.
- Unit 13 (pp.157–171): charts and quantity. Reading I: "Nepal is the First Country to Double its Tiger Population" — the TX2 report (121 tigers in 2009 to 235 in 2018; adapted from ntnc.org.np, 29 July 2022). Pronunciation: chart-interpreting words. Writing I: interpreting an engineering-enrolment chart. Listening: an apple-pie recipe conversation with quantities (audio). Grammar I: quantifiers (many/much, (a) few/(a) little, some/any, a lot of/lots of, less/fewer, enough, no). Reading II: "A Mousetrap", a short drama — the animals who refused the mouse help are eaten one by one. Grammar II: the four present tenses (simple, continuous, perfect, perfect continuous) identified and practised. Writing I(second): describing a flowchart (orange juice) and a tea-manufacturing diagram. Project: a class enrolment bar graph. Extra bit: chart-description vocabulary.
- Unit 14 (pp.172–188): conditions. Reading I: "The Magic Mirror" — the king of Granada's barber tests brides with a non-magic mirror; only the shepherd girl dares to look. Pronunciation: British vs American pronunciation pairs. Grammar I–II: conditional types 1, 2 and 3 (matching, sentence building, completion). Speaking: conditional chains. Writing I: completing the lamb-and-wolf story with correct verb forms. Listening: a technician's working day (audio). Reading II: the poem "If I was a Superhero" (Sally Gray). Writing II: analysing and writing a news story ("Nepal begins exporting cement to India"). Project: an "If I were the Prime Minister" speech. Extra bit: the parts of a news story.
- Unit 15 (pp.189–200): Reading I: "Conservation of Earth" — habitats, extinction and the biosphere. Pronunciation: consonant sounds. Speaking: giving explanations (because, in order to). Grammar I–II: connectives — because/because of, so that, in order to, although, however, therefore, in spite of. Writing I: an argumentative/opinion essay on radio and TV for students. Reading II: an official notice (Ministry of Education notice of school closure for COVID-19, dated 26th Poush 2078). Writing II: writing a school notice. Listening: a zoo-animals opinion talk (audio). Project: a class bulletin board. Extra bit: connectives and transition words by function.
- Unit 16 (pp.201–213): narrating events. Reading I: "Why I Became a Vegan" — a first-person anecdote (adapted from Jessica Sharp, gentwenty.com; Whole30, the Almost 30 Podcast). Pronunciation: silent letters. Grammar I: past simple, past continuous and past perfect in narration (identification, questions, completion). Listening: a bus-shelter incident narrative (audio). Writing I: story ordering (bad habits and the guava tree) and picture-story writing. Reading II: "A History of Pens" — reed, quill, steel-point, fountain and ballpoint pens. Writing II: paragraphs on a historical event. Project: a paragraph on last month's school events. Extra bit: blending (brunch, motel, infotainment…).
- Unit 17 (pp.214–225): offers. Reading I: "Naresh and the Stranger" — a poor farmer shelters a stranger who repays him with a buried pot of money. Pronunciation: contractions. Speaking: making and accepting offers. Grammar I–II: the passive voice (matching actives to passives across tenses; changing sentences into the passive). Writing I: replying to a marketing email offer (Nepal Sunglass House). Listening: visiting a friend (audio). Reading II: R. L. Stevenson's poem "From a Railway Carriage". Writing II: a reply letter to a party invitation. Project: collecting offer expressions. Extra bit: a full passive-voice tense chart.
- Unit 18 (pp.226–237): giving opinions. Reading I: "Road Accidents in Nepal: Are Poor Roads Solely to Blame?" — an opinion article (adapted from Sugam Gautam, The Himalayan Times) blaming drivers, youth and drink as much as roads. Pronunciation: road-safety words. Speaking: opinion conversations. Grammar I: subject–verb agreement (a second pass). Writing I: an opinion essay on extracurricular activities. Listening: Sweden vs Canada (audio). Reading II: the poem "Long Life" (Tricia Mae Chua). Grammar II: intensifiers too and enough (too + adjective + to-infinitive; adjective + enough). Writing II: punctuation repair; an opinion essay on restaurant vs homemade food. Project: an opinion survey. Extra bit: a pronoun table.
- Unit 19 (pp.238–253): reporting. Reading I: "A Tale of Two Birds" — two nestlings, one raised by robbers and one by a hermit, learn the speech of their company. Pronunciation: dictionary work on ten words. Speaking: reporting dialogues in threes. Grammar I–II: reported speech in full — statements, yes/no and Wh- questions, commands and requests, with tense backshift and pronoun changes; universal truths keep the present tense. Writing I: a story from an opening line. Reading II: a news report, "Pokhara International Airport Inaugurated" (Himalayan News Service, Jan 2023). Writing II: a news report from clues (a hospital in Doti). Listening: two crime stories (audio). Project: designing a newspaper front page. Extra bit (pp.253): direct-to-indirect time/place word changes (today → that day, here → there, this → that…). Printed p.254 is blank.

### Grammar spine
Modals can/could/would for information questions; present simple (U1) → yes/no question formation (U2) → reported speech: yes/no questions (U3) → present perfect vs past simple (U4) → reported speech: imperatives/requests/advice (U5) → causatives have/get/make/let; subject–verb agreement (U6) → will vs going to; future perfect (U7) → past simple/continuous/perfect together; used to (U8) → comparative/superlative adjectives and adverbs (U9) → prepositions of place and time; question tags (U10) → relative clauses; articles (U11) → verb + -ing vs to-infinitive; question formation (U12) → quantifiers; the four present tenses (U13) → conditionals types 1, 2, 3 (U14) → connectives of reason, purpose and contrast (U15) → past-tense narration consolidated (U16) → passive voice across the tense system, with a full chart (U17) → subject–verb agreement again; too/enough (U18) → reported speech complete: statements, questions, commands, with backshift and deictic shifts (U19).

### Honest negatives
- No Glossary (unlike Grade 7): the book ends with Unit 19 and a blank leaf.
- No answer key, no tests or exam practice, and no tapescripts: every Listening task depends on audio that is not printed in the book.
- No future continuous and no past perfect continuous as taught topics (the past perfect continuous appears once, as an example sentence to identify in U13: "I'd been studying at this school when you were born").
- No modal-perfect forms (should have / could have) as a topic; no gerund/infinitive naming as grammar terms — U12 teaches the patterns by verb lists only.
- Reported speech is split across the book (U3 questions, U5 imperatives) and only assembled in full in U19; there is no single early reference summary.
- No Nepali translation anywhere; all vocabulary support is English-only definitions and synonym work.

### Slips and errors as printed
- p.12 (Extra bit): dictionary abbreviations list prints "connj." for conjunction and "etc. Et Cetera" capitalised.
- p.38 (U4): "January in Nepal is usually somewhere between 00 to -150 C in the Himalayas" — the degrees are garbled (0° to −15 °C is plainly meant).
- p.41 (U4, Pronunciation): the diphthong word list prints "allowance" twice.
- p.42 (U4): "Megha .................... (ran/has run) two marathons last year. He could not succeed with either" — "He" for Megha.
- p.69 (U6): "Rewite the following sentences" — for "Rewrite".
- pp.97–100 (U8, "Historical Inventions"): "Michael Faraday, who invented electricity, is called the father of electricity" — Faraday did not invent electricity; "Television was invented in 1939 by John Logie Baird" — Baird demonstrated television in 1925–26, not 1939; the heading word is spelled "The areoplane" (p.98); and question e on p.100 asks "When was the first spacecraft launched to the moon?" while the text (p.99) says only that Sputnik 1 was launched on October 4, 1957 — it was not launched to the moon.
- p.103 (U8, idioms): the "see eye to eye" entry is printed in mangled form ("see eye to / eye") and its example reads "Mr. Sam never sees eyes to eyes with us" — not the idiom's form.
- p.117 (U10, vacancy text as reproduced): "Knowledge of the other UN working knowledge is an asset" is garbled, and "indigeneous groups" is misspelled.
- p.118 (U10, Pronunciation): the -tion/-sion word list includes "rovolution" for "revolution".
- p.136 (U11, Writing I): Pasang Lhamu Sherpa "climbed Mt. Blank(4848m)" — for Mt. Blanc.
- p.138 (U11): the park-regulations box is set inside the fauna paragraph, splitting the sentence "Some of the common ones are … Dhanphe, flycatcher, bulbul, cuckoo, and eagle" across the box.
- p.146 (U12): "International Union for the Conversation of Nature" — for Conservation; the same slip recurs on p.158: "the National Trust for Nature Conversation's tiger expert".
- p.148 (U12): question C.a names Kazi Dai's friend "Parasar Narayan Shrestha"; the text (p.146) names him Parasar Narayan Suwal.
- p.151 (U12): the model exercise sentence "He admitted of ............... (steal) the money" teaches the non-standard "admitted of".
- p.152 (U12): "What support do you need to persue them?" — "persue" for "pursue".
- p.162 (U13): "Listen to the the audio" — doubled word. p.163: quantifiers "used with dferent types of nouns" — "dferent".
- p.196 (U15, official notice as reproduced): "This is to notify all the concerned everyone that the Ministry…" — garbled; and the lead-in question on the same page asks "Who do you think the students are doing?" for "What…".
- p.210 (U16): "The fountain pen was patented by Petrache Poenaru, a Roman inventor" — Poenaru was Romanian, not Roman; also "quick- dry in" for "quick-dry ink" on the same page.
- p.215 (U17): "His only friend were his oxen" — subject–verb error inside the model story.
- pp.227–228 (U18): the road-accidents article repeats itself — "Safe driving is a mixture of discipline, experience and maturity - traits young people lack" and the licence-age proposal each appear twice with slight variation; p.228 also prints the run-together string "overlookedwhilediscussingthedeathscausedbyroadaccidents".
- p.237 (U18, Extra bit pronoun table): the 2nd-person object pronoun is printed as "us" (the row reads "2nd person: you / us / yours / your / yourself-yourselves") — it should be "you".
- p.239 (U19): run-together words in the story: "There was thunder,lightning,andrain.Thewindblew down many trees." p.247 has the same fault: "standardsandguidance.AswetransitionawayfromtheCOVID-19 pandemic".

### Copy completeness and cross-check
- Folio verdict: complete. Front matter folios run 1–8 (PDF 1–8, with p.4 a blank folio-only leaf before the Contents); body folios run 1–254 unbroken (PDF 9–262) with no missing or duplicated leaves; printed p.254 is a blank folio-only leaf after the last Extra bit, so the file ends cleanly.

- One layout fault inside an otherwise complete copy: the Khaptad regulations box interrupts a sentence on p.138 (noted under Slips); no text is lost.
- Cross-check vs companion: no Grade 8 teacher's guide exists in the record, so no cross-check is possible; nothing in this book refers to one.
