## Grade 9 — ENGLISH Grade 9 (student's book)

### Identity block
- **Title as printed:** Cover: "ENGLISH Grade 9". Title/imprint page (PDF 2) names the book only as the Grade 9 English textbook of the Curriculum Development Centre; no separate title page wording beyond the cover. The record's wording "English, Grade 9, 2079" is NOT what the book prints: **no "2079" appears anywhere in the book** (full-text search: 0 occurrences). The only BS year printed is "2078", once, in the Preface, as the curriculum year ("Secondary Level English Curriculum, 2078").
- **Publisher:** Government of Nepal, Ministry of Education, Science and Technology, Curriculum Development Centre (CDC), Sanothimi, Bhaktapur.
- **Year/edition/ISBN as printed:** Imprint prints "First Edition: 2022" only — no updated/revised edition statement, no price line. Preface signed "2022 AD". **No ISBN is printed anywhere in the book.**
- **Developers/authors as printed (Preface):** initially written by Nim Prakash Singh Rathaur, Ananda Dhungana, Pashupati Pandya; revised by Dr. Gopal Prasad Pandey, Dr. Neil David Rose, Ramesh Dhakal, Shankar Adhikari, Nabin Khadka; Director General Mr. Ana Prasad Neupane; committee chair Prof. Dr. Jib Lal Sapkota; members include Prof. Dr. Rishi Ram Rijal, Madhav Prasad Ghimire, Maiya Niraula Pokharel, Tuka Raj Adhikari, Parshu Ram Tiwari, Matrika Subedi; illustrations Shaili Malla and Kushal Adhikari; art editing Shreehari Shrestha.
- **Curriculum/series notes:** Preface states the book is prepared in line with the National Curriculum Framework 2076 and the Secondary Level English Curriculum, 2078. Running footer throughout the body: "English 9".
- **Extent:** 248 PDF pages = 6 front-matter pages (cover, imprint, Preface, Contents ×3; unnumbered) + body printed pp. 1–236 (PDF 7–242) + Glossary on PDF 243–248 (6 pages; no folio captured in the text layer, so its printed pagination — presumably 237–242 — is not verifiable from the text).
- **Printed↔PDF offset:** body printed page = PDF page − 6, established from the Contents unit-start pages (Unit 1 at printed p. 1 = PDF 7) and confirmed by the running folio "English 9 <n>" on every body page checked (e.g. PDF 242 carries printed 236).

### Unit-by-unit summary
Every unit follows the same skeleton: Getting started, Reading I, Pronunciation, Speaking, Grammar I, Writing I, Reading II, Listening, Grammar II, Writing II, Project work, Extra bit.
- **Unit 1 — Travel and Holidays (pp. 1–12):** Reading I is a day-by-day "Poon Hill Yoga Trek" itinerary (Kathmandu–Pokhara–Nayapul–Ghorepani/Poon Hill–Ghandruk). Reading II is Robert Frost's poem "Stopping by Woods on a Snowy Evening" (printed pp. 9–10). Speaking: making plans and a telephone conversation expressing future intentions. Listening: a hotel-booking conversation and an interview. Grammar: future forms — present continuous for fixed plans, "going to", "will", simple future continuous (Grammar II drills timetabled future and predictions). Writing: a letter.
- **Unit 2 — Health and Hygiene (pp. 13–26):** Reading I is an interview on telehealth services with Chun-Kai Chang of Advantech. Reading II is the folk-style story "Health is Wealth" (a bulky king cured by a holy man's regimen). Grammar I: modals should / ought to / had better for suggesting, advising and warning. Grammar II: conditional types 2 and 3, including inverted "Had she studied hard…". Writing: paragraph description and a recipe. Project: a presentation.
- **Unit 3 — Family, Market and Public Places (pp. 27–37):** Reading I is the story "Thomas and Jerry" (a father takes his small son to catch a train; the boy's questions expose the father's muddled time-keeping). Reading II is the comic story "Dance", whose narrator finally gets a dance at a disco on his twentieth wedding anniversary. Speaking: making requests and responding to them. Listening: a souvenir/gift-shop conversation. Grammar: modals would / could / can for requests (Grammar II extends them to bookings and recommendations). Writing: a letter to the head teacher. Project: narrating a special event.
- **Unit 4 — Life and Death (pp. 38–51):** Reading I is an excerpt from Sadhguru, "Death is a Fiction". Reading II is Tennyson's poem "Crossing the Bar". A printed condolence notice (for Namgyal Sherpa) is studied; speaking practises expressing condolence and sympathy; listening is a funeral conversation (Tim and Heather). Grammar I: present simple with subject–verb agreement. Grammar II: adverbs of frequency. Writing: a newspaper cutout and an email.
- **Unit 5 — Ethics, Norms and Values (pp. 52–64):** Reading I is the article "Why 'I'm Sorry' Doesn't Always Translate" (Maddux, Kim, Okumura & Brett) on apology in American vs Japanese culture. Reading II is JetBlue CEO David Neeleman's printed apology letter to customers after an ice-storm week. Speaking: apologising and responding to apologies (the practice clues — eating chocolates, spilling a drink, stepping on a toe — are the unit's core drill). Grammar I and II are both the present continuous (identifying it, then controlled rewriting). Writing I: interpreting pie charts of house ownership in Nepal. Writing II: an apology letter/email. Extra bit: simple, compound and complex sentences.
- **Unit 6 — Custom and Culture (pp. 65–78):** Reading I is Matthew Carney's account "Sky Burials" of witnessing a Tibetan sky burial near Tagong. Reading II, "Surprising Customs", covers lip-pointing in Nicaragua, US tipping, noodle-slurping in Japan and Yoruba kneeling/prostrate greetings. Speaking: asking for permission. Listening: marriage traditions matched to countries. Grammar I: forming yes/no questions. Grammar II: yes/no questions in reported speech. Writing: an essay "Our Culture Our Pride" and an essay from an outline. Extra bit: the four types of essays.
- **Unit 7 — Ecology and Environment (pp. 79–92):** Reading I, "How do Animals Spend the Winter?", is a five-section text on migration, adaptation, shelter, hibernation and dormancy. Reading II, "The Resistant Moths", tells how pesticide use bred resistant potato moths for farmer Mahendra Yadav, who returns to natural methods. Speaking: making, accepting and rejecting offers. Grammar I and II: conditional type 1 (with unless). Writing: paragraphs on deforestation/endangered animals; a letter to the editor (model: a letter to The Rising Nepal on road accidents). Extra bit: male, female and young of animals.
- **Unit 8 — Science and Technology (pp. 93–106):** Reading I is a Xinhua news report by Shristhi Kafle on the humanoid robot Sophia's visit to a Kathmandu innovation conference. Reading II, "The Alternative Energy Sources", surveys six ideas (space-based solar, body-movement harvesting, wave energy, hydrogen, flying wind farms, fusion/ITER). Speaking: describing people, places and things (including a sales pitch for a fridge). Grammar I and II: relative clauses. Writing: a news story from an outline (Nepal's robot "Yantrika") and a news article on green energy. Project: a debate on AI. Extra bit: components of a news story.
- **Unit 9 — Work and Leisure (pp. 107–119):** Reading I is W. Somerset Maugham's story "The Ant and the Grasshopper" (industrious George versus his idle, blackmailing brother Tom). Reading II is W. H. Davies's poem "Leisure". Speaking: having/getting things done. Grammar I: causatives with have, get and make. Grammar II: the passive voice, with a full active/passive tense table. Writing I: interpreting a bar chart using trend verbs. Writing II: an essay on work and leisure. Extra bit: words ending in "-ism".
- **Unit 10 — The Earth and Space (pp. 120–131):** Reading I, "A Message from Another Planet", is a story in which Suren Majhi is taken aboard a spaceship and given a recycling message for Earth's leaders. Reading II is Christina Rossetti's poem "Up-Hill". Speaking: locating places on a street map. Listening: a talk on Pluto's demotion to dwarf planet. Grammar I: prepositions in/at/on. Grammar II: dependent prepositions and prepositions in a narrative. Writing: an account of meeting someone from another planet; park rules and regulations. Extra bit: the format of account writing.
- **Unit 11 — Gadgets and Instruments (pp. 132–144):** Reading I, "Save your Smartphone", gives phone-care advice (charging habits, heat, cleaning, screen lock, app permissions). Reading II, "Weather Instruments", describes the thermometer, barometer, hygrometer, anemometer, wind vane and rain gauge. Speaking: giving instructions (screenshot steps, making pizza). Grammar I: reported speech for statements. Grammar II: reported speech for imperatives and requests. Writing: a recipe; a newspaper article on the impact of science and technology. Extra bit: transitive and intransitive verbs.
- **Unit 12 — People and Places (pp. 145–157):** Reading I is a biography of the Brazilian author Paulo Coelho (mental-hospital stays, hippie years, the Santiago pilgrimage, The Alchemist). A review of The Alchemist is the Writing I model. Reading II is Langston Hughes's poem "Madam and Her Madam" (the maid Alberta answers her mistress back). Speaking: giving directions. Pronunciation: homonyms. Grammar I and II: articles a/an/the, including zero article. Writing II: a biography of Madhav Prasad Ghimire from an outline. Extra bit: idioms.
- **Unit 13 — Organisation Profile and Authority (pp. 158–168):** Reading I is an organisational profile of the Asian Development Bank Institute (ADBI), Tokyo. Reading II is a letter from Anil Gurung congratulating Mrs Rai on becoming Chief Editor of The Nepal Times. Speaking: describing purpose and function ("What is X used for?"). Grammar I: purpose structures (to / in order to / so that / for). Grammar II: joining sentences with connectives (despite, even though, although, because…). Writing I: designing advertisements (model: Tiger Shoes). Writing II: messages of congratulations (model: the ENRD message honouring Mahabir Pun's Ramon Magsaysay Award, 2007). Extra bit: plurals of words ending in -f/-fe.
- **Unit 14 — History and Civilization (pp. 169–184):** (Contents spells the title "Civilization"; the unit heading prints "Civilisation".) Reading I, "The Maya Empire", is an eight-section history of Maya civilisation from 1800 BC to the Classic-period collapse. Reading II, "The History of Money", runs from barter and cowrie shells through Lydian coinage, leather money, Chinese paper money and the gold standard to bitcoin. Speaking: narrating past events with used to. Grammar I: used to / didn't use to. Grammar II: simple past, past continuous and past perfect, ending in an error-correction anecdote about a shared packet of biscuits at a station. Writing: developing stories from outlines; an essay on the importance of history. Extra bit: uses of the semicolon.
- **Unit 15 — People and Lifestyle (pp. 185–197):** Reading I is the 20 June 1942 entry from Anne Frank's "The Diary of a Young Girl" (the "Kitty" entry, with the list of anti-Jewish decrees). Reading II, "The Rautes", describes Nepal's nomadic Raute people (about 180 people, Karnali Province, monkey hunting, division of labour). Speaking: "How long…?" with for/since. Listening: a talk on Gross National Happiness. Grammar I: present perfect and present perfect continuous. Grammar II: present perfect with since/for. Writing: a week-long diary; an essay on junk food. Extra bit: words for phobias and manias.
- **Unit 16 — Games and Sports (pp. 198–209):** Reading I is an article on Wimbledon (history since 1877, events, ballot, courts, trophies, the 2019 champions Djokovic and Halep). Reading II is a general article "Sports". Pronunciation works through Trenité's poem "The Chaos". Speaking: expressing ability with can / can't / be able to. Grammar I: question tags. Grammar II: negation (matching and converting sentences to negatives). Writing I: a news story from an outline (Mt. Ama Dablam clean-up). Writing II: paraphrasing (model paragraph given). Extra bit: how to paraphrase.
- **Unit 17 — Global Warming and Climate Change (pp. 210–223):** Reading I is a review of the documentary "Chasing Ice" (James Balog's Extreme Ice Survey). Reading II is the short drama "A Noisy Party" (Ramila and Ramesh suspect neighbour Mr Dahal; he is burying dead goldfish). Speaking: expressing degrees of certainty with must / can't / may / might. Grammar I: future forms again, including the future perfect. Grammar II: wh-questions, including wh-questions in reported speech. Writing: a film review; a school notice. Extra bit: linking verbs.
- **Unit 18 — Transportation and Communication (pp. 224–236):** Reading I is a biography of the Wright Brothers from the toy helicopter to the 1903 Flyer and the 1904 practical airplane. Reading II is John Hill's poem "Rickshaw Ride" (an old Hong Kong rickshaw puller). Speaking: making comparisons of animals and vehicles. Listening: a taxi conversation about reaching a museum. Grammar I: adjectives and adverbs. Grammar II: comparative and superlative forms. Writing: interpreting a bar chart of electric-vehicle use; an account of a journey. Extra bit: types of adverbs.
- **Glossary (PDF 243–248; folios not captured):** an alphabetical pronunciation glossary (IPA transcription + part of speech + definition) running from "acorn" to "yield", keyed to words from all 18 units.

### Grammar spine
In the order the book actually presents it (the Contents table names only the headline topic per unit; each unit in fact carries two grammar sections):
future forms — present continuous for plans, going to, will, future continuous (U1) → modals of advice should / ought to / had better (U2 Grammar I) → conditional types 2 and 3 (U2 Grammar II) → request modals would / could / can (U3) → present simple and subject–verb agreement (U4 Grammar I) → adverbs of frequency (U4 Grammar II) → present continuous, taught twice (U5 Grammar I and II) → yes/no questions (U6 Grammar I) → reported yes/no questions (U6 Grammar II) → conditional type 1 with unless (U7, both sections) → relative clauses (U8, both sections) → causatives have / get / make (U9 Grammar I) → passive voice across all tenses (U9 Grammar II) → prepositions in/at/on, then dependent prepositions (U10) → reported statements (U11 Grammar I) → reported imperatives and requests (U11 Grammar II) → articles a/an/the and zero article (U12, both sections) → purpose connectives to / in order to / so that / for (U13 Grammar I) → concession and reason connectives (U13 Grammar II) → used to (U14 Grammar I) → past simple / past continuous / past perfect (U14 Grammar II) → present perfect and present perfect continuous (U15 Grammar I) → since / for (U15 Grammar II) → question tags (U16 Grammar I) → negation (U16 Grammar II) → future perfect and future forms revisited (U17 Grammar I) → wh-questions and reported wh-questions (U17 Grammar II) → adjectives and adverbs (U18 Grammar I) → comparison of adjectives and adverbs (U18 Grammar II).

### Honest negatives
- No answer key and no tapescripts are printed anywhere in the student's book; every Listening section depends on audio the book does not reproduce.
- No ISBN is printed; no price is printed; no edition statement later than "First Edition: 2022" exists in the book.
- The conditional type 0 is never named or taught; conditionals appear only as type 1 (U7) and types 2–3 (U2).
- The passive voice is taught only once (U9 Grammar II) and is not named in the unit's Contents grammar entry beyond the word "Passive"; it is never revisited.
- Reported speech is split across three widely separated units (questions in U6, statements/imperatives in U11, wh-questions in U17) and is never drawn together.
- There are no tests, exams or revision units; "Project work" and "Extra bit" are the only non-core sections.
- Pronunciation is never more than word-lists and read-aloud poems — no phoneme chart, no minimal-pair drilling beyond U8's confusable pairs, no stress system beyond U14's stress marks.
- Modals are taught only in functional slices (advice U2, requests U3, certainty in U17 Speaking); there is no consolidated modal system and no modal perfects as a grammar topic.
- The book contains no index and no maps section; the Glossary is the only back matter.

### Slips and errors as printed
- Contents table, Unit 4 row: "subject verb agrement" [sic] — "agreement" misspelled in the front matter (Contents, PDF 4).
- Contents prints the Unit 14 title as "History and Civilization"; the unit's own heading (p. 169) prints "History and Civilisation".
- P. 62: the Grammar II daydream text names its character both "Sophiya" and "Shophiya" in the same exercise.
- P. 80: "the Arctictern" (run together; Arctic tern). P. 81: "hibernation.Water" (missing space). P. 82: a true/false item spells "hybernate".
- P. 102: the Altaeros turbine "self-docks" is printed as "sell-docks in emergency cases".
- P. 110: the self-assessment scale in Unit 9 is labelled "Lazy Grashopper" [sic].
- P. 128: the Pluto listening table is headed "S.N. Statemants" [sic].
- P. 140: multiple-choice options for the barometer question are numbered "ii. digital ii. mercury iii. aneroid". P. 141: a distractor offers "i. wind wave" for wind vane.
- Pp. 146–147: the subject's name is misprinted twice in the Coelho biography — "Paolo Coelho's break-through" (p. 146) and "Coehlo's travels" (p. 147) — against "Paulo Coelho" used elsewhere.
- P. 156: Writing II names "Madav Prasad Ghimire" in the instruction while the outline below it prints "Madhav Prasad Ghimire".
- P. 179: "Peter the Great's reign (1682–1725 BCE)" — the era marker should be CE.
- P. 183: "most people had alredy left" [sic].
- P. 191: a Grammar I item is malformed: "My father ………………. (never/eats) chocolates."
- P. 199: "rain has sometimes forces play on this day" (for "forced"). P. 200: a comprehension question asks why Centre Court was "fitted with a rectangle roof" (for retractable roof).
- Pp. 210–211: the "Chasing Ice" review header dates the film "Release Date: Aug 10, 2016", while the review text says it "opens in Los Angeles on Nov. 23" and frames it around Hurricane Sandy (2012) — the header and text do not agree.
- P. 219: multiple-choice options in the "A Noisy Party" questions are misnumbered (i, iii, iii) in two consecutive questions.
- P. 223: the model school notice says the school "will remain closed from 15th June 2021 to 15th July 2021" but then "Regular classes will resume from 16th June 2021" (should be 16th July).
- P. 234: the taxi listening options are numbered i, ii, i.
- Glossary (PDF 244–246): "disseminate" is tagged "(n.)" though defined as a verb; "nasty" is tagged "(adv.)"; and a block of entries (aneroid, precipitation, meteorology, musing…) is printed out of alphabetical order after "mallet".

### Copy completeness and cross-check
- **Folio run:** complete and in order. All 248 PDF pages were read; body folios run printed 1–236 with no missing or duplicated leaves; the only unnumbered parts are the 6 front-matter pages and the Glossary (whose folios the text layer does not capture).
- **Contents vs actual:** the Contents page numbers agree exactly with the 18 unit starts as printed, and every reading text named in the Contents is present. Two Contents-level faults: the "agrement" misspelling, and the Contents grammar column names only one topic per unit although every unit teaches two grammar sections (e.g. Unit 9's passive voice, Unit 17's reported wh-questions are invisible in the Contents).
- **Text-layer note:** many pages from Unit 14 onward carry a diagonal "Curriculum Development Centre" watermark whose letters interleave into the extracted text; this is a copy artefact, not a print error, and the printed pages themselves are legible.
- **Cross-check vs the companion teacher's guide (g09-teacher.pdf):** the guide does NOT key to this book. It is built for a different, older 20-unit Grade 9 textbook (this book has 18 units), with different unit order and mostly different texts. Verified agreements are limited to texts the older book and this book happen to share, in scattered positions: the disco story "Dance" (guide Unit 3 ↔ this book's Unit 3 Reading II — the guide's key "a comedian / 40 years old / danced at last" fits this story), a shopkeeper–customer request listening (guide Unit 3 ↔ Unit 3 here), the apology situations of Unit 5 Speaking (guide Unit 9), "Madam and Her Madam" (guide Unit 6 ↔ Unit 12 Reading II here), "The Resistant Moths" (guide Unit 14 ↔ Unit 7 Reading II here), "Leisure" (guide Unit 15 ↔ Unit 9 Reading II here), the Mahabir Pun congratulation message, the Anil Gurung congratulation letter and the Tiger Shoes advertisement with its purpose-grammar table (guide Units 5 and 11 ↔ Unit 13 here), "A Noisy Party" (guide Unit 16 ↔ Unit 17 Reading II here), the "Rickshaw Ride" poem (guide Unit 17 ↔ Unit 18 Reading II here), and the station-biscuits anecdote (guide Unit 18 ↔ Unit 14 Grammar II here). The guide's main readings for Units 3, 4, 8, 12, 19 and 20 (a china-chip keepsake story, a bicycle-accident tapescript, a diary entry, an Apollo tapescript, a pandas passage, a paracetamol text) appear nowhere in this book. The guide cannot be used as this book's answer key except for the carried-over items listed.

## Grade 9 — Teachers' Guide English Grade 9 (teacher's guide)

### Identity block
- **Title as printed:** The title page (PDF 1) prints only: "Teachers' Guide / English / Grade 9 / DRAFT". No other title wording appears. The record's name "Teachers' Guide English Grade 9" matches the title page's elements, but the book's self-declared status — **DRAFT** — is part of what it prints: the word stands as its own mark on the title page, and its Introduction says the Curriculum Development Centre "has developed a teachers' guide" (the word DRAFT does not occur inside that sentence — verified by the citation check).
- **Publisher:** No imprint page exists in this copy. The Introduction and the "How to use" section are signed "Curriculum Development Centre", and the Acknowledgements state that financial and technical support was provided by the Secondary Education Development Project (funded by HMGN and ADB) and the Nepal Secondary Education Project (funded by DFID UK) — funding lines of an older, pre-2022 textbook generation. No publisher's imprint, place or press is printed anywhere in the copy.
- **Year/edition/ISBN as printed:** **None.** No year, no edition statement and no ISBN are printed anywhere in this copy — no imprint page survives (or was ever included) in the file.
- **Developers/authors as printed (Acknowledgements, PDF 4):** the page is partly garbled/lost in this copy: it opens mid-list ("l Mr …"), so at least one contributor's name and role are missing. What survives: Editor — Madan Nath Yogi; a further name, Durpada Sapkota (role not legible); Designers — Tarzan Rai; Illustrator — Raju Babu Shakya.
- **Curriculum/series notes:** The Introduction says the guide serves "the new Grade 9 curriculum… based on a functional or communicative approach", implemented by the CDC, and states the guide covers **all 20 units of the textbook**, each broken into 6–8 lessons. Each unit box states which curriculum unit the textbook unit "links with" — and the links are not one-to-one (e.g. guide Unit 6 ↔ curriculum Unit 11; Unit 8 ↔ 19; Unit 9 ↔ 6; Unit 11 ↔ 13; Unit 12 ↔ 9; Unit 14 ↔ 17; Unit 15 ↔ 18; Unit 16 ↔ 20; Unit 17 ↔ 14; Units 18 and 19 ↔ 16). A prefatory section "Equal opportunities for all" (roman pp. i–iii) gives gender/inclusion guidance.
- **Extent:** 112 PDF pages = title page (PDF 1) + unnumbered front matter (Introduction, Structure/How to use, Acknowledgements; PDF 2–4) + roman-numbered pp. i–iii (PDF 5–7) + four separated runs of arabic pages: printed 26–54 (PDF 8–36), 62–74 (PDF 37–49), 82–96 (PDF 50–64), 104–151 (PDF 65–112).
- **Printed↔PDF offset:** there is NO single offset, because leaves are missing. Per surviving run: PDF 8–36 = printed page + 18; PDF 37–49 = printed + 25; PDF 50–64 = printed + 32; PDF 65–112 = printed + 39. Established from the footer "GRADE - 9, TEACHERS' GUIDE <n>" printed on every surviving page.

### Unit-by-unit summary
Only 15 of the guide's 20 units are present in this copy; Units 1, 2, 7, 10 and 13 are missing entire (see Copy completeness). Each present unit opens with a "What you will teach" box (function, language, grammar, topics, student activities), then lesson-by-lesson teaching steps with full answer keys and, where relevant, a tapescript.
- **Unit 3 (printed pp. 26–34) — Making requests:** 7 lessons on modals would/could/can and polite request forms. Readings: a story about a mother's keepsake piece of broken china (Lesson 1–2), and a disco story (Lesson 6–7) whose keyed answers are "(i) a comedian / (iii) 40 years old / (ii) danced at last". Includes the shopkeeper–customer tapescript (Sudha tea, Ilam ghee, Chandani soap, biscuits).
- **Unit 4 (pp. 35–41) — Expressing condolence and sympathy:** 8 lessons (box says 7). Condolence messages and letters (a SCAEF condolence text), role-played sympathy conversations, a condolence notice keyed to a death on "March 3rd", a newspaper report "Flood toll reaches 82 in Bangladesh", and a telephone tapescript (Kumar/Deepak) about a bicycle accident.
- **Unit 5 (pp. 42–48) — Expressing congratulation:** 7 lessons. A congratulations message to Mahabir Pun (Ramon Magsaysay Award 2007 — misprinted "Roman", see Slips), biography writing, a telephone tapescript (Jubeda congratulates Krishna on a best-female-singer award), and the Anil Gurung letter congratulating Miss Rai on becoming Chief Editor of The Nepal Times. The guide itself flags a misprint in its textbook here: "teach students making clear about the misprint in the textbook" (both speakers are girls).
- **Unit 6 (pp. 49–54) — Giving instructions:** 7 lessons; grammar is imperatives with sequence markers (first/then/next). A first-aid reading on mouth-to-mouth resuscitation, a "Chinese whispers" instructions game, physical-fitness instructions, a TV cookery tapescript ("Cook Your Own Food": curd fish), and the poem about Alberta the maid ("Madam and Her Madam").
- **Unit 8 (pp. 62–67) — Expressing ability and inability:** 7 lessons on can/can't, be able to, know how to. Reading: a diary entry by Amrita envying her baby sister. Listening: a video-library membership tapescript (Beena Sharma, Pushpa Ghale; Rs. 500 deposit, Rs. 15 per film). Includes condolence-message writing as a secondary activity.
- **Unit 9 (pp. 68–74) — Apologising and responding to an apology:** 7 lessons of set phrases. Readings: short conversations (smoking in a no-smoking zone, queue-jumping), a fellowship-rejection letter (Kirti Subba Trust), a drama about Bimala's marriage and the dowry system, and a party-accident telephone tapescript (Ali/Kumar).
- **Unit 11 (pp. 82–88) — Describing purpose and function:** 7 lessons; grammar: to-infinitive of purpose and for + gerund. Advertisement readings (keyed table includes "Tiger shoes — for sports — use with special Tiger socks"), a tools game, model advertisements, an FM-radio tapescript (Prem and Maya), and a poem about two boys and a donkey collecting driftwood in Spain.
- **Unit 12 (pp. 89–96) — Describing:** 8 lessons; grammar: defining relative clauses (who/which/that/where), with an added passive-structure exercise. Readings: a newspaper front page ("Stinking protest at TIA"), headline-writing, and a documentary tapescript on the Apollo moon mission (printed throughout as "Apollo II" — see Slips).
- **Unit 14 (pp. 104–111) — Criticising:** 5 lessons; grammar: present perfect with should ("You shouldn't have…"). The reading is the resistant-moths pesticide story (farmer Mahendra), followed by picture-based "should have" writing (Ramesh in the Head Teacher's room) and a shop-complaint tapescript (a returned radio; the customer "should have kept the receipt").
- **Unit 15 (pp. 112–118) — Persuading:** 8 lessons; grammar: if clauses — "If I were you…", "Wouldn't it be better if…?". A house-buying drama (Mr Hada's house, Rs. 150,000), a mother–son tapescript persuading the son to lose weight and join a gym, and W. H. Davies's poem "Leisure" as the reading poem.
- **Unit 16 (pp. 119–124) — Expressing degrees of certainty and probability:** 8 lessons; grammar: the "that" clause ("I'm sure that…", "There's no doubt that…"). The reading is the drama "A Noisy Party" (Ramila, Ramesh and Mr Dahal's goldfish), followed by summary/letter writing, a radio-interview tapescript on deforestation in Nepal (Mr Pradhan), and a poem about a boy competing with a singing bird.
- **Unit 17 (pp. 125–132) — Talking about the past (1): narrating past events:** 8 lessons; grammar: simple past. Readings: the story of Ryan, a young driver killed by overconfidence; picture-story narration (a robbery); a travel tapescript (Maya and Anand: Ilam, Biratnagar, Koshi Tappu, Jomsom); and the "Rickshaw Ride" poem (Hong Kong), with adjective-matching for rider and puller.
- **Unit 18 (pp. 133–138) — Talking about the past (2): interrupted continuous action:** 8 lessons; grammar: past continuous with while/when clauses. Readings: anecdotes including the station-biscuits story ("The Other Biscuits"), a story of soldiers lazing in barracks (Sher Bahadur and the Sergeant-Major), a writers' tapescript (Maya and Prasad), and warning-sign language ("No parking…").
- **Unit 19 (pp. 139–144) — Talking about the past (2) [sic — same label as Unit 18]: past actions with present significance:** 8 lessons; grammar: present perfect and present perfect continuous, since/for. Readings: a factual passage on giant pandas, news stories (an RNAC item; a climber's 16-hour-56-minute record), a tourist-and-local tapescript on Kathmandu's changes since 1978, and a revision of relative clauses.
- **Unit 20 (pp. 145–151) — Talking about the present / describing an object or a place:** 6 lessons plus a final tapescript; grammar: simple present. Readings: a factual text on paracetamol (analgesic/antipyretic, overdose and decontamination), describing-people writing, a landlady-and-tenant tapescript (Mamata; house rules), the poem "Rain sizes", and a closing project on drug abuse. This final unit is visibly the least edited part of the copy (see Slips).

### Grammar spine (as the guide frames it)
The guide is function-first: grammar is whatever structure carries the unit's function. In its own order: questions with can/could/would and if-clauses for requests (U3) → set phrases only (U4 condolence, U5 congratulation — the guide explicitly labels these "Grammar: Set phrases") → imperatives with sequence markers (U6) → modals can/can't, be able to, know how to (U8) → set phrases (U9 apology) → to-infinitive of purpose and for + gerund (U11) → defining relative clauses, with a passive-structure exercise attached (U12) → present perfect with should — "should have / shouldn't have" (U14) → if clauses: "If I were you", "Wouldn't it be better if…?" (U15) → the "that" clause for certainty (U16) → simple past (U17) → past continuous interrupted by past simple, with while/when (U18) → present perfect and present perfect continuous with since/for (U19) → simple present (U20). Units 1, 2, 7, 10 and 13 are missing from this copy, so their place in the guide's grammar sequence cannot be stated.

### Honest negatives
- The copy contains **no imprint, no publication year, no ISBN, no price** — nothing that dates or formally publishes the book.
- Units 1, 2, 7, 10 and 13 are absent entire; the guide in this copy never shows how it introduces the course, and roughly a quarter of its units are missing.
- The guide provides **no keys at all for the 2022 student's book** as a whole: it keys a different (20-unit) textbook (see cross-check).
- There is no glossary, index or appendix in the copy, although the "How to use" section tells teachers difficult words can be found "in the Glossary at the back of the book" — that glossary belongs to the textbook it serves, and no such back matter survives here.
- Assessment guidance is limited to marking some lessons as "can be set as a unit test or as homework"; there are no marking schemes, no exam papers and no tapescript for Units 4's flood report or several other listenings beyond the ones printed.
- The guide never mentions the 2022 curriculum, the National Curriculum Framework 2076, or the Secondary Level English Curriculum 2078 named in the current student's book.

### Slips and errors as printed
- Front matter (How to use the Guide): "looked up in a dicitionary" [sic].
- Acknowledgements (PDF 4): the contributor list is broken — it begins mid-entry ("l Mr …") and at least one name/role is lost; the surviving text cannot be reassembled.
- Unit 5 box and lessons (pp. 42–43): "The Roman Magsaysay Award 2007" — for Ramon Magsaysay Award (the student's book prints "Ramon" correctly). The error is repeated three times.
- Unit 5, Lesson 4 (p. 45): "read the typescript" for tapescript; (p. 44) "many different conservations" for conversations.
- Unit 9 box (p. 68): "Student Reading short conversations…" — the activities table is jumbled (text runs across columns).
- Unit 12 (pp. 89–96): the moon mission is printed as "Apollo II" throughout, including the tapescript ("On the 16th of July 1969, Apollo II was launched") — Apollo 11 is meant.
- Unit 12, Lesson 6 key (p. 95): "The man who is waiting for a bus is a treacher" [sic].
- Unit 14 (pp. 105–111): "access use of pesticide" (for excessive use); answer text "affected furture use of the land bye using pesticides"; "pratise"/"practises" for practise; "write their guesses on the boars" (p. 109); the tapescript's manager is labelled "Manger:" and says "Did you cheek the radio… it was not a cheep radio"; table entry "been ao aggressive".
- Unit 16 box (p. 119): "He probabily won't come" [sic] in the unit's own language summary.
- Unit 17, Lesson 7 key (p. 131): the comparison table is headed "Rckshaw-rider" [sic].
- Unit 18 box (p. 133): topics line prints "similtaneous events" [sic]; Units 18 and 19 are both labelled "Talking about the past (2)".
- Unit 19, Lesson 7 key (p. 143): "climbing the highest peak in 16 hours and 56 miutes" [sic].
- Unit 20 (pp. 145–151) is typo-dense throughout: "Deeontamination" in the vocabulary key; "symploims of overdose on Paracelemol"; "uncompliated overdose"; "cold and flue"; "keeps pulic order"; "Teourists travel"; Lesson 4 headed "a simple writing activity descrbing people"; "witrh sellotape"; "they sholud speak"; Lesson 7 "in the writining exercise", "how thsy could possibly contribute"; the poem lesson repeats "peom"/"peoms" and prints "as anickel whole" and "Butn sometimes". The unit's "What you will teach" box (p. 145) is also scrambled — Function/Language/Grammar/Topics lines are out of order, unlike the tidy boxes of earlier units.

### Copy completeness and cross-check
- **Status as printed:** the book calls itself a **DRAFT** on its title page and again in its Introduction. Nothing in the copy claims to be a final published edition.
- **Folio run:** INCOMPLETE. The copy preserves printed pp. i–iii (front section), 26–54, 62–74, 82–96 and 104–151. Missing entire: printed pp. 1–25 (Units 1 and 2), 55–61 (Unit 7), 75–81 (Unit 10) and 97–103 (Unit 13). Within the surviving runs the folios are continuous, in order, with no duplicated leaves. The copy does contain the guide's own ending — Unit 20, Lesson 7 and its tapescript close the book on printed p. 151 — so what survives is a beginning-less, three-times-gutted excerpt whose final unit is intact.
- **Interior coherence as a guide:** for the 15 units present, the interior is coherent and usable: each unit has its orientation box, stepped lesson procedures, answer keys and tapescripts, and later units cross-refer to earlier ones ("See Unit 1, Exercise 5") — including references to the missing Units 1, 2 and 7, which proves those units existed in the complete draft. Coherence degrades at the end: Unit 20's box is scrambled and its pages carry far more misprints than any earlier unit, consistent with an unfinished draft rather than a finished book.
- **Cross-check vs the Grade 9 student's book (g09-student.pdf, First Edition 2022):** the guide does NOT key to that book. It serves a 20-unit textbook; the 2022 book has 18 units in a different order, and the guide's own curriculum-unit links are scrambled relative to its unit numbers. Verified overlaps are carry-over texts only: the disco story and shopkeeper tapescript (guide U3 ↔ student U3), the apology situations (guide U9 ↔ student U5 Speaking), "Madam and Her Madam" (guide U6 ↔ student U12 Reading II), the Mahabir Pun message and the Anil Gurung letter (guide U5 ↔ student U13), the Tiger Shoes advertisement and purpose table (guide U11 ↔ student U13), "The Resistant Moths" (guide U14 ↔ student U7 Reading II), "Leisure" (guide U15 ↔ student U9 Reading II), "A Noisy Party" (guide U16 ↔ student U17 Reading II), "Rickshaw Ride" (guide U17 ↔ student U18 Reading II), and the biscuits anecdote (guide U18 ↔ student U14 Grammar II). For those items the guide's keys do fit the 2022 texts (e.g. its Unit 3 key for the disco story; its Unit 14 vocabulary answers combat/swamped/avalanche/devour/fallow match the student's Unit 7 exercise). Everything else in the guide — its main readings for Units 3, 4, 8, 12, 19, 20 and all its unit ordering — belongs to the older textbook and must not be cited as this student's book's key.

## Grade 10 — ENGLISH Class 10 (student's book)

### Identity block
- Title as printed: cover reads "ENGLISH Grade 10"; the title page reads "ENGLISH Class 10". The record's wording "English Grade 10" matches the cover, not the title page.
- Publisher: Government of Nepal, Ministry of Education, Curriculum Development Centre (CDC), Sanothimi, Bhaktapur.
- Year/edition/ISBN as printed: First Edition: 2017. The imprint page prints "ISBN:" followed by nothing — the ISBN line is blank; no ISBN is printed anywhere in the book. Printed at Janak Education Materials Centre Ltd.
- Authors as printed (title page): Bishow Raj Joshi, Parbati Dhungana, Chandra Kanta Pandit. Preface (2017) credits contributors including Baburam Poudel (Executive Director), Prof. Dr. Chandreshor Mishra, Prof. Dr. Bal Mukunda Bhandari, Dr. Rishi Ram Rijal, Ganga Dhar Hada, Sita Sharma, Homnath Amgain, Arun Kumar Rai; edited by Chandra Kanta Bhusal and Ramesh Prasad Ghimire.
- Curriculum/series notes: the Preface states the book was developed in accordance with the Secondary Level English Curriculum, 2071 (2014). Nineteen functional units, each built on the sequence Reading / Grammar / Listening / Speaking / Writing, most with a Project work and a Fun corner (riddle) section; a Glossary closes the book.
- Extent: 233 PDF pages = cover (PDF 1) + title page (PDF 2) + imprint (PDF 3) + Preface (PDF 4) + Contents (PDF 5) + printed pp. 1–228 (PDF 6–233).
- Printed↔PDF offset: printed page = PDF page − 5. Established from the Contents (unit 1 begins on printed p. 1, which falls on PDF p. 6) and confirmed by the printed folios throughout, including the last printed page 228 on PDF p. 233.

### Unit-by-unit summary
- Unit 1 — Giving, Withholding and Reporting Permission (pp. 1–12): Reading "A World Guide to Good Manners" (adapted from Norman Ramshaw in New Headway English) on permission customs across countries; grammar can / may / Is it OK if… and reporting permission; listening on manners; speaking asking/giving/refusing permission; writing an invitation card.
- Unit 2 — Reporting Statements (pp. 13–22): Reading is a debate transcript — Karma argues for a single language, Kabita against, MC Amrit Timilsina; grammar reported statements with tense backshift and reporting verbs; writing an essay, "Our Culture, Our Identity".
- Unit 3 — Reporting Questions (pp. 23–33): Reading W. Somerset Maugham's "The Ant and the Grasshopper" (from Sixty Five Short Stories); grammar reported questions; a riddle (answers on p. 215); writing a model essay, "Relevance of Involving School Graduates in Community Services".
- Unit 4 — Reporting Commands (pp. 34–47): Reading Govinda Raj Bhattarai's "Memoirs of my Visit to France" (Paris, the Rodin museum); grammar reported commands and requests; writing a cooking recipe (vegetable momo).
- Unit 5 — Giving Advice and Warnings (pp. 48–58): Reading a letter from aunt Manisha to Kunsang (7 April 2017) and Dan Kois's Washington Post review of the film "2012" (13 Nov 2009); grammar modal verbs for advice, obligation and warning (the book lists "have" among modals); writing advice.
- Unit 6 — Expressing Conditions (I) (pp. 59–70): Reading Tom Wayman's poem "Did I Miss Anything?" and a Nepal Tourism Board brochure on Achham; grammar zero conditional, first conditional and imperative conditionals; listening is a minimal-pairs pronunciation exercise.
- Unit 7 — Expressing Conditions (II) (pp. 71–82): Reading "Rampant pesticide use risks health" (The Himalayan, 19 July 2009), Chelsea Harvey's Washington Post piece on climate inequality (8 Dec 2015) and an MVERC workshop schedule; grammar second and third conditionals, unless, provided that, inverted Had…; writing formal letters.
- Unit 8 — Asking for Reasons, Purposes and their Responses (pp. 83–93): Reading a passage on human trafficking; grammar because, because of, so that, in order to, therefore; writing a letter to the editor (model: Bhup Poudel's traffic letter, Rising Nepal, 10 April 2016).
- Unit 9 — Expressing Unexpected Results (pp. 94–106): Reading Robert Frost's "The Road not Taken" and "A Scary Secret of Two Sisters" (adapted from Savage & Mayer, Effective Academic Writing); grammar concessives although / despite / in spite of and sentence transformation; listening a biography of Louis Braille.
- Unit 10 — Describing Events (pp. 107–118): Reading Barbara Nimri Aziz's "Yogamaya: Poet, Teacher, Insurgent" (from Heir to a Silent Song); grammar causatives make / get / have, passive-to-active conversion and relative clauses; writing a place description (Devghat model).
- Unit 11 — Expressing Preferences (pp. 119–132): Reading Vishnu S Rai's story "Parents" (from Martyr and Other Stories) — a father accompanies his 13-year-old daughter Anuja to meet her e-friend and finds the friend's mother has done the same; grammar prefer / like / would rather and articles; writing a CV (model: Dipa Tiwari) and a job application.
- Unit 12 — Talking about Personal Experience (pp. 133–144): Reading Wordsworth's "I Wandered Lonely as a Cloud" and an interview on typhoid with Dr. Rajan Poudel (Civil Hospital); grammar subject-verb agreement; speaking in the present perfect continuous about experiences.
- Unit 13 — Talking about the Past (I) (pp. 145–153): Reading William Blake's "The Chimney Sweeper"; grammar past simple vs past continuous.
- Unit 14 — Talking about the Past (II): Interrupted Continuous Actions (pp. 154–164): Reading the story "Reunion of the Family" (grandmother Pabitra leaves after a quarrel and returns); grammar interrupted past continuous and used to; a riddle; writing a biography (Hemingway model from New Headway Intermediate; students write on Narayan Gopal).
- Unit 15 — Talking about the Past (III): Comparing Past and Present (pp. 165–173): Reading Thomas Hood's "Past and Present" (from The Golden Treasury); grammar used to / didn't use to for past-vs-present contrast; listening a biography of Ram Raja Prasad Singh.
- Unit 16 — Confirming and Denying (pp. 174–182): Reading a text on the Jitiya Festival; grammar So / Neither + auxiliary and question tags; writing an essay, "Advantages and Disadvantages of Computers in Education".
- Unit 17 — Agreeing and Disagreeing (pp. 183–191): Reading the drama "Sushila's Determination" (adapted from Forte Longman, 1989) — her parents want her married; she wants nursing training, and her mother is persuaded to fund her education; grammar expressions of agreement and disagreement; a riddle.
- Unit 18 — Indicating Time and Motions (pp. 192–202): Reading "Habit Cultivation", an excerpt from Shiv Khera's You Can Win; grammar prepositions of time (at / on / in) and of movement.
- Unit 19 — Interpreting Tables and Charts (pp. 203–214): Reading an Ilam school bar chart (2011–2015), an SLC 2072–2073 English results table and pie chart, and a brick-making process text; grammar a connectives review; listening a review of Anne Frank's memoir; speaking on a National Census 2068 BS religion table.
- Answers of the Riddles (p. 215) for Units 3, 14 and 17; Glossary (pp. 216–228).

### Grammar spine
Reported permission (can / may / Is it OK if…) → reported statements → reported questions → reported commands → modal verbs (advice and warnings) → conditionals I (zero, first, imperative) → conditionals II (second, third, unless, provided that, inverted Had) → reason and purpose connectives (because, so that, in order to, therefore) → concessives (although, despite, in spite of) → causatives and relative clauses → preference structures (prefer, would rather) and articles → subject-verb agreement → past simple vs past continuous → interrupted past continuous and used to → used to for past/present comparison → So/Neither + auxiliary and question tags → agreement/disagreement expressions → prepositions of time and movement → connectives review. Reported speech is the spine of the first four units; the past-tense system is spread across Units 13–15.

### Honest negatives
- No examination preparation of any kind: no SEE past papers, no model question sets, no exam strategy or marking guidance anywhere in the book. The SLC appears only as chart data in Unit 19 and as a line in the model CV in Unit 11.
- No answer keys for the exercises (the only answers printed are the Answers of the Riddles, p. 215).
- No separate literature section: the poems, story and drama sit inside functional units as reading texts.
- No phonology strand beyond a minimal-pairs listening exercise (Unit 6) and scattered pronunciation points; no systematic treatment of word stress or intonation.
- No ISBN is printed (the imprint's ISBN line is blank), so the book cannot be citation-matched by ISBN.

### Slips and errors as printed
- "bussiness card" (p. 2); "in some counties" for countries (p. 2); "decide wehether" (p. 4).
- "Februrary 23rd 2018" in the Unit 1 writing tasks (pp. 10, 11); "Bedetar" for Bhedetar (p. 12).
- Unit 2 multiple-choice item (p. 17) prints options a, b and c identically.
- "Mont Carlo" (p. 26); "Wahington Post" (p. 50); the same review's photographer appears as both "Harald Klose" and "Kloser" (p. 50).
- "Swnar" (p. 63); "Rainfall 1794 ml" for mm (p. 64); "Acchham" (p. 65); "a newspapers" (p. 71); "Feelng sleepy" (p. 79); "a quite atmosphere" for quiet (p. 82).
- Unit 8 reading: a sentence ends "and other." (incomplete, p. 84); "Ministry of Women, Children and Welfare" drops "Social" (p. 84).
- "converstions" (p. 90); "make any further request further" (p. 97); "Neither Nitesh or Nripesh appreciate her" (p. 101); "she has not replied me" (p. 103); "he did not got discouraged" (p. 104); "Body paragrap 2" (p. 105); "Let the assembly begun" (p. 111); "Other go to collect a Shaligram" (p. 118).
- The Unit 10 reading spells its subject both "Yogmaya" and "Yogamaya"; "afterwords" (p. 122); "vegeterian" and "homeopathy" (p. 127); Marsha Ivins quoted as "It can be crowed" for crowded (p. 139); "alchoholic" (p. 141); "past partciple" (p. 153); "sometning" (p. 157).
- Unit 15's gap-fill summary (p. 168) mislabels which stanza of Hood's poem contains what.
- "Aftewards" (p. 175); the Jitiya text is internally inconsistent — Jimutavahana is "Masabashi's son" yet Masabashi is "an unmarried princess", and Shero is called a fox and then a jackal; "Disadvatages" (p. 181).
- Unit 17's follow-up letter says the parents agreed "to let me go to university", though the drama concerns a nursing institute.
- "should mine or gesture" for mime (p. 200); "8.08 had to be satisfied" omits the % sign (p. 207); "andstakeholders" run together (p. 207).
- Glossary: "flourish" is entered twice with different transcriptions; "hazard" is labelled (adj) but defined as a noun; "double (adj)" is defined as "grow to twice the size".

### Copy completeness and cross-check
- Folio run: printed pp. 1–228 continuous; no missing or duplicated leaves observed in this copy.
- Contents vs actual: the Contents page (PDF p. 5) start pages match the units as printed throughout (Units 1–19 and Glossary at p. 216).
- Cross-check against the held teacher's guide: the guide does NOT agree with this book. Its 18 thematic units (Current Affairs and Issues, Festivals and Celebrations, …) match neither this book's 19 functional unit titles nor their order, texts or grammar sequence, and it contains no keys for this book's tasks. The guide is the companion to a newer Grade 10 textbook edition; the only shared text is Vishnu S Rai's "Parents" (Unit 11 here; Unit 7 in the guide, where the author is named "Vishnu Singh Rai").

## Grade 10 — Teacher's Guide: English 10 (teacher's guide)

### Identity block
- Title as printed: the title page (PDF p. 1) reads "Teacher's Guide / Feedback Copy / English 10", Government of Nepal, Ministry of Education, Science and Technology, Curriculum Development Centre, Sanothimi, Bhaktapur. The record's wording "Teacher's Guide English 10 (2024)" is accurate as far as it goes, but the book itself claims a more specific status: "Feedback Copy" is printed on the title page itself, between the title and the book name.
- Status the book claims for itself: a feedback copy — i.e. a pre-final circulation copy of the guide. Despite that label, the interior is a complete, fully worked teacher's guide (see completeness verdict below); it is not a partial draft, an outline, or sample pages.
- Publisher: Ministry of Education, Science and Technology (MoEST), Curriculum Development Centre (CDC), Sanothimi, Bhaktapur.
- Year/edition/ISBN as printed: First Edition: 2024 (imprint, PDF p. 2). The imprint's "Printed by:" line is left blank — no printer is named. No ISBN is printed anywhere in the book.
- Developers as printed (Preface, PDF p. 3): developed by Basu Dev Osti, Pashupati Padhya, Nabin Kumar Khadka, Bharat Sigdel, Muna Khanal, with guidance from Director General Baikuntha Prasad Aryal and Ima Narayan Shrestha; subject committee chaired by Prof. Dr. Bal Mukunda Bhandari, with Dr. Bamdev Adhikari, Dr. Netra Prasad Sharma, Tukaraj Adhikari, Kunti Adhikari, Basant Raj Dhakal, Nawaraj Sapkota.
- Curriculum/series notes: the Preface claims the guide "is fully based on the contents and tasks of Grade 10 English textbook" (CDC). That claim holds for a newer, thematic Grade 10 textbook — it does NOT hold for the held First Edition 2017 student's book (see cross-check below). The guide also directs teachers to audio at www.moecdc.gov.np.
- Extent: 308 PDF pages = title page (PDF 1) + imprint (PDF 2) + Preface (PDF 3) + Contents (PDF 4) + blank page (PDF 5) + printed pp. 1–303 (PDF 6–308).
- Printed↔PDF offset: printed page = PDF page − 5. Established from the first printed folio (1 on PDF p. 6) and confirmed at the end: the last printed page, 303, falls on PDF p. 308.

### Unit-by-unit summary
- General chapters (pp. 1–4): how the guide is organised; principles of learning facilitation; communicative tasks; assessment as, for and of learning; remedial teaching; dictionary use; obtaining the listening audio from the CDC website.
- Unit 1 — Current Affairs and Issues (pp. 5–20; 8 hrs): Reading I a news article, "How Driverless Cars will Change Our World"; Reading II an open letter to UN Secretary-General Antonio Guterres; listening a talk on climate change; grammar reported speech (statements) with change of tense; writing a newspaper article on AI and an official letter to a municipality mayor.
- Unit 2 — Festivals and Celebrations (pp. 21–35; 8 hrs): Reading "Battle of the Oranges" and "Thanksgiving around the World"; grammar reported questions I & II; writing a festival essay and a news story; extra bit word stress.
- Unit 3 — Health and Wellness (pp. 36–55; 10 hrs): Reading a patient's complaint letter and the speech "A Healthy Diet for a Healthy Life" (EU Joint Programming Initiative, Horizon 2020); listening a doctor interview on lactose intolerance; grammar reported imperatives I & II and reporting verbs; writing an apology email and a speech on obesity.
- Unit 4 — Work and Leisure (pp. 56–72; 9 hrs): Reading the short story "Cabbage White" and Tagore's poem "Leave this Chanting and Singing" (Gitanjali 11); listening a talk on working cultures; grammar modals, imperatives and their functions; writing a job application and rules and regulations (Chitwan National Park model); project a speech contest, "Work is Worship".
- Unit 5 — Science and Experiment (pp. 73–86; 10 hrs): Reading "Yes, You Can Boil Water at Room Temperature" and Whitman's "The Voice of the Rain"; listening a density experiment; grammar conditionals — the guide presents all four types with structures (§5.4); writing instructions to draw a jellyfish and a complaint letter; extra bit words often confused.
- Unit 6 — Food and Cuisine (pp. 87–104; 10 hrs): Reading the essay "Strange Foods from Around the World" and a Nepali Times interview with chef Santosh Shah; listening a talk on food science; grammar connectives of reason and purpose, present continuous and time adverbials; writing a recipe (gajar halwa; chicken momo sample) and paragraph writing.
- Unit 7 — Cyber Security (pp. 105–121; 8 hrs): Reading the story "Parents" and the essay "Cyber Security: Basic Internet Safety Tips"; grammar modals (should / ought to / must) and articles; speaking criticising with should have / shouldn't have; writing an email reply and a newspaper article; project a cybercrime pamphlet; extra bit intonation.
- Unit 8 — Hobbies and Interest (pp. 122–135; 9 hrs): Reading "Common Hobbies" and Amy Lowell's poem "Climbing"; grammar voice — passives including "being" and "having" passives, I & II; speaking preferences; writing a hobby essay, an advertisement, and paraphrasing; extra bit idioms.
- Unit 9 — History and Culture (pp. 136–152; 10 hrs): Reading "Pratap Malla" and "Flowers in Russian Culture"; listening on Windsor Castle and the Queen's Gurkha Engineers; grammar past tenses in narrative (simple past, past continuous, past perfect) I & II; pronunciation the apostrophe sounds /s z ɪz/; writing paragraphs on Nepal's journey from Panchayat to Multiparty System and story writing; extra bit loan words.
- Unit 10 — Games and Sports (pp. 153–171; 10 hrs): Reading "The First Olympic Games" (the Pelops/Tantalus myth; adapted from Jean Richards & Kat Thacker) and the essay "Popularity of Different Sports in the World"; listening sports news (Three Nations Cup); grammar present perfect vs present perfect continuous, and voice; writing a paragraph and dialogue writing; project a pamphlet on a favourite sport.
- Unit 11 — Ethics and Morality (pp. 172–185; 8 hrs): Reading Tolstoy's "Wisdom of Little Girls" and the short drama "World Record" (Mabel Phillips's record attempt); grammar negation, affirmative forms and subject-verb agreement; pronunciation sentence stress; writing a moral story and a condolence/sympathy message.
- Unit 12 — Nature and Development (pp. 186–202; 10 hrs): Reading a text on the Kathmandu–Terai/Madhesh Fast Track and Wordsworth's "Composed upon Westminster Bridge, September 3, 1802"; listening on the Pan-American Highway; grammar future tense and its aspects; speaking degrees of probability; writing a report and a notice.
- Unit 13 — Population and Migration (pp. 203–218; 8 hrs): Reading "A Century of World Population Trends: 1950 to 2050" (World Population Prospects 2022) and "Discovering Migration: What Birds Reveal"; listening a WWF wildlife-decline report; grammar connectives of contradiction I & II; writing chart interpretation and an opinion essay; project a report on migratory birds/animals; extra bit types of adjectives.
- Unit 14 — Travel and Adventure (pp. 219–231; 9 hrs): Reading "Expect the Unexpected" (Kathmandu/Pokhara/Chitwan) and Hardy's poem "Weathers"; grammar question tags I & II; speaking confirming and denying; pronunciation different pronunciations of the same letter; writing a travelogue and a brochure; extra bit contractions.
- Unit 15 — People and Places (pp. 232–246; 10 hrs): Reading a biography of Jhamak Kumari Ghimire and "Sugarloaf Mountain: 5 Interesting Facts"; grammar relative clauses and prepositions; pronunciation heteronyms (read / live / wind / wound / tear); writing a biography and a letter/email describing a place; extra bit transition words.
- Unit 16 — Success and Celebration (pp. 247–265; 8 hrs): Reading a biography of Walt Disney and Kipling's "If"; listening a Barack Obama speech; grammar prepositions and prepositional phrases; speaking congratulating; writing a congratulation message/letter and a speech, "Failure as the Pillar of Success"; extra bit archaic words.
- Unit 17 — Countries and Towns (pp. 266–277; 10 hrs): Reading "London vs Paris" and the poem "The Country Mouse and the City Mouse" (Richard Scrafton Sharpe); grammar adjectives and adverbs, and connectives; speaking comparing and contrasting; pronunciation intonation; writing paragraphs and describing a place; extra bit clauses. (The unit introduction uses "country" in the sense of countryside, not nation.)
- Unit 18 — Media and Entertainment (pp. 278–292; 8 hrs): Reading a film review of "Hachi: A Dog's Tale" and "Interesting Social Media Stories" (including the Nathan Apodaca/Ocean Spray story); grammar question formation and causative verbs; speaking asking for and giving opinions; pronunciation saying telephone numbers, years and numbers; writing a film review and an essay on the impact of social media.
- Listening Script (pp. 293–303): full scripts for the listening tasks of all 18 units.

### Grammar spine (as the guide frames it)
Reported statements → reported questions → reported imperatives and reporting verbs → modals and the functions of imperatives → conditionals (zero, first, second and third, presented together with structures in Unit 5) → connectives of reason and purpose; present continuous with time adverbials → modals (should / ought to / must) and articles → voice, including "being" and "having" passives → past tenses in narrative (simple past, past continuous, past perfect) → present perfect vs present perfect continuous, and voice again → negation and subject-verb agreement → future tense and its aspects → connectives of contradiction → question tags → relative clauses and prepositions → prepositions and prepositional phrases → adjectives, adverbs and connectives → question formation and causative verbs. Every unit frames its grammar as a named section with presentation, controlled practice and answer keys, and most units add a pronunciation or "extra bit" strand (word stress, intonation, idioms, loan words, contractions, heteronyms, archaic words, types of adjectives, clauses).

### Honest negatives
- The guide contains nothing for the held First Edition 2017 Grade 10 student's book: no shared unit titles beyond coincidence, no keys to that book's tasks, no page references to it.
- No ISBN is printed, and the imprint's "Printed by:" line is blank — neither a printer nor an identifier is given.
- A few sample answers are left unfinished, the only traces of the "Feedback Copy" status inside: the Unit 3 sample apology email keeps the template placeholders "[Hospital Name]" and "[Your Contact Information]" and is signed "XYZ" (p. 43); a Unit 15 sample answer leaves dotted blank lines (pp. 235–236); the Unit 12 sample report is given as lead sentences only (p. 195).
- The guide never explains its own "Feedback Copy" status — there is no note saying what feedback is sought, from whom, or by when.
- No separate literature teaching strand: literary texts appear only as unit readings.

### Slips and errors as printed
- Unit 1 key table names the same man "Richard Jink" and "Richard Jinks" (p. 11); key: "Dorje apologized to me that he couldn't lend me any money that day" (p. 18); "Suryavinak-4" for Suryavinayak (p. 19).
- §2.7 heading "Thanksgiving around the Word" for World (p. 29); key: "My friend inquired her if she had done her homework" (p. 27).
- Unit 3: "The Liberian ordered to keep our mobile phone in flight mode" for Librarian (p. 41); listening key "68 percentage of the world population is lactose intolerant" and a garbled sentence beside it (p. 49); key: "He advised Mrs. Tamang that he should see a doctor" — pronoun should be "she" (p. 50).
- Unit 4 listening/reading materials spell "caterpillars" throughout (pp. 59–61).
- Unit 5: the jellyfish drawing instructions ask for a "natural and creaky appearance" (p. 80); the sample "complaint letter" (p. 84) is a complaint about garbage, dated "Nevember 25, 2023", although the stated objective is a letter to the editor about Kathmandu's water shortage.
- Unit 6 pronunciation key contradicts itself: "Amateur: AM-a-teur (stress on the second syllable)"; "Gardener: GAR-den-er (stress on the second syllable)" (p. 92); crossword key "TONDOORI" for tandoori (p. 98); "preservation of the original test" for taste (pp. 98–99); "if the stamen agrees with the information" for statement (pp. 98, 157); "drive-troughs" (p. 103).
- Unit 7 overview table has two rows numbered "9." (PDF p. 111); the speaking key gives wrong advice — "I'm feeling drowsy in class" is keyed to "You shouldn't have gone to bed early" — and skips item g (p. 112).
- Unit 8 keys: "Unrevealed (synonym): exposure"; "Frame (synonym): easel" (p. 124); "slerosis" for sclerosis (p. 125).
- Unit 9: the pre-reading sample answer discusses Yognarendra Malla although the reading text is Pratap Malla (p. 138); listening key "James' Palace" for St James's Palace (p. 149); the grammar key itself notes "(e. is missing in the textbook)" (p. 150).
- Unit 10 passive key: "The painting will be drawn beautifully by her" (p. 168), with the key letter k duplicated across pp. 168–169; "summersault" for somersault, twice (p. 179).
- Unit 12 overview: "Have conservations" for conversations (p. 186); the pre-reading answer calls the Westminster Bridge picture "the tower bridge which is known as London Bridge" — the wrong bridge (p. 196); the sample notice announces a programme for "30th Feb, 2024", a date that does not exist (p. 201); the unit ends with a stray "–)–" instead of the guide's usual ΩΩΩ end mark (p. 202).
- Unit 13 overview repeats row number 9 (pp. 203–204) and its Reading II task list includes "Write the names of sports shown in the picture", copied from Unit 10 (p. 203); the antonym of "cluster" is keyed as "chump" (p. 212); a key sentence is truncated: "some birds choose to during the night" (p. 213); "pronouncing the the silent /r/" (p. 207).
- Unit 14: "Swargadwariis" and "BoudhanathStupa" missing spaces (p. 220); the interpretation of Hardy's "Weathers", pasted from eliteskills.com and credited in the text, describes four stanzas/seasons, contradicting the guide's own note one paragraph earlier that the poem has two nine-line stanzas (pp. 226–227); vocabulary key "g. duns" for "dons" (p. 227).
- Unit 15: the tear verb/noun transcriptions are swapped (p. 236); "Nikolai Otrovskys" (p. 235); the Ghimire book title appears as both "Jivan Kadan Ki Phul" and "Jivan Kanda Ki Phool" in the same section (p. 235); sample email header "To: audemoret1234Agmail.com", missing the @ (p. 244).
- Unit 16: the Disney chronology key places the cartoon company's collapse after the 1931 breakdown (Laugh-O-Gram in fact collapsed in 1923, before Oswald and Mickey) (p. 250); Pinocchio, Fantasia and Bambi are called "commercially useless at first" (p. 251); "extraordinarily" is given two different transcriptions (pp. 252–253); the stress of "animation" is listed as "First & Third" (p. 253); "Rata" for Rita (p. 254); "Master's of Arts" (p. 254); "study propositions and their uses" for prepositions (p. 255).
- Unit 17 intonation section claims rising intonation is used in wh-questions (p. 270) — wh-questions normally fall.
- Unit 18: "adaption" for adaptation (pp. 279–280); "from the left to write" for right (p. 286); "lip singing" for lip-syncing (p. 286).
- Listening Script slips: Unit 4 script contradicts itself — "Many people don't even use all of their time. They use all of their vacation days." (p. 295); Unit 5 script "the syrup is denser than the grip" for grape, and "gives a really nicer fact" (p. 296); Unit 9 script "Alder shot" for Aldershot (p. 298) and "guard the Queen's Elizabeth II" (p. 299); the Unit 11 script announces "seven mentalities" of the eagle but prints only five, ending at No. 5 (pp. 299–300); Unit 12 script "Duran gap" (p. 300) against the guide key's "Darian Gap" (p. 199) — the Darién Gap; Unit 13 script is introduced by "Ann Lee" but signed off "Ian Lee, CBS News" (p. 300); Unit 16 script "JK Rawlings" for Rowling (p. 302).

### Copy completeness and cross-check
- Folio run: printed pp. 1–303 continuous; no missing or duplicated leaves observed. Every unit ends with the guide's ΩΩΩ end mark (the Unit 12 stray mark noted above is a misprint, not a missing leaf), and the Listening Script (pp. 293–303) supplies scripts for all 18 units.
- Completeness verdict: complete. All 18 units carry the full apparatus — unit introduction, overview table with estimated hours, objectives, materials, step-by-step procedures, full answer keys and sample answers. The "Feedback Copy" wording on the title page is the book's only claim of provisional status; nothing inside is missing except the minor unfinished sample-answer traces listed under Honest negatives.
- Contents vs actual: the Contents (PDF p. 4) unit titles and start pages match the units as printed.
- Cross-check against the held Grade 10 student's book (First Edition 2017): NO agreement. The guide's 18 thematic units do not correspond to the student book's 19 functional units in title, order, reading texts or grammar sequence, and none of its keys answers the held book's tasks. The single overlap is the story "Parents": Unit 11 of the student book, credited there to Vishnu S Rai, and Unit 7 of the guide, credited there to "Vishnu Singh Rai". Conclusion: this guide belongs to a newer Grade 10 textbook edition than the student's book held; the two must not be filed as a matched pair.
