## Grade 11 — English (Grade 11) (student's book)

### Identity block
- Title as printed: the cover (pre-verified) reads "English Grade 11"; the title page (PDF p. 2) reads "English (Grade 11)", Government of Nepal, Ministry of Education, Science and Technology, Curriculum Development Centre, Sanothimi, Bhaktapur. The record's wording "English Grade Eleven" matches neither exactly; the book's own forms are "English Grade 11" / "English (Grade 11)".
- Publisher: Curriculum Development Centre (CDC), Sanothimi, Bhaktapur.
- Year/edition/ISBN as printed: Edition: 2077 BS (2020 AD), per the imprint (PDF p. 3). The imprint's "Printing:" line names no printer in the text of the copy held. No ISBN is printed anywhere in the book.
- Author as printed (Preface, PDF p. 4, dated 2020): written by Mohan Singh Saud; edited by Prof. Dr. Bal Mukunda Bhandari, Dr. Ganga Ram Gautam, Dr. Gopal Prasad Pandey, Prof. Dr. Jiba Lal Sapkota; contributions from Director General Keshab Prasad Dahal, Dr. Lekh Nath Poudel and Director Tuka Raj Adhikari; illustration Dev Koimee; layout Khados Sunuwar.
- Curriculum/series notes: the Preface states the book follows the National Curriculum Framework 2076 and the Secondary Level Curriculum 2076 for Grades 11–12, and is built in two sections — Section One: language development (20 thematic units, each with reading, vocabulary, grammar, listening, speaking, writing and a project) and Section Two: literature (stories, poems, essays, plays), headed "Literature" in the contents and "Part Two: Literary Studies" on its divider page.
- Extent: 339 PDF pages = cover (PDF 1) + title page (PDF 2) + imprint (PDF 3) + Preface (PDF 4) + Contents (PDF 5–8) + Section One divider (PDF 9, unnumbered) + printed pp. 3–332 (PDF 10–339). (Printed pagination begins at 3; there are no printed pp. 1–2.)
- Printed↔PDF offset: printed page = PDF page − 7. Established from the Section One divider (PDF p. 9, unnumbered) followed by printed p. 3 on PDF p. 10, and confirmed by the running folio "English: Grade 11" with the printed number on every page through printed p. 332 on PDF p. 339.

### Unit-by-unit summary
Section One — Language Development
- Unit 1 — Education and Humanity (pp. 3–12): Reading Malala Yousafzai's UN Youth Assembly speech of 12 July 2013 ("Education First"); dictionary use and alphabetical order; word classes; writing a personal narrative; listening on choosing a major; speaking good wishes and contracted forms; project a poster on a local woman's struggle for education.
- Unit 2 — Communication (pp. 13–21): Reading Peter Elbow's "Freewriting"; adjective/adverb formation (-ly / -y); grammar the position of adverbs; writing freewriting and paragraph organisation; listening note-taking (Clare and Mark's history exam notes); speaking agreement and disagreement; project hobby statement cards.
- Unit 3 — Media and Society (pp. 22–30): Reading Aulia Maharani Karli's blog "Social Media: Its Influence and Control over People's Lives" (including the Prita Mulyasari hospital-email case, Indonesia); grammar prepositional verbs and prepositions of direction; writing an advantages/disadvantages essay on social media and a piece on cyberbullying; listening a TV programme on the functions of mass media; speaking directions and instructions; project a Facebook-status survey.
- Unit 4 — History and Culture (pp. 31–41): Reading Gabriel Peralta's "Qin Dynasty"; homographs and word stress; limericks; grammar past simple; writing an email on Prithvi Narayan Shah's unification and paragraphs on six Nepali political movements 1951–2006 from a table; listening a third-eye documentary; speaking narrating events; project a Qin vs Rana Regime chart.
- Unit 5 — Life and Love (pp. 42–52): Reading Chekhov's "The Looking Glass" — Nellie dreams her whole married future at a mirror on New Year's Eve and wakes to find she fell asleep; -ed / -ing adjectives; grammar will vs be going to; writing paragraphs about a dream; listening vacation plans; speaking decisions, intentions and plans; project a picture series on Nepali lifestyles.
- Unit 6 — Health and Exercise (pp. 53–61): Reading Emma Young's "You May Scoff…" on alternative causes of obesity (adenovirus-36, stress, warm homes, endocrine disrupters, light at night, air pollution, sleep loss); -ion / -ness suffixes and silent letters; grammar subject-verb agreement; writing a yoga essay and ten mental-health tips; listening exercise and a company team; speaking describing places; project "Eat Healthy Stay Healthy".
- Unit 7 — Ecology and Development (pp. 62–70): Reading Wangari Maathai's "Foresters without Diplomas" (the Green Belt Movement; the first seven trees, 5 June 1977; ten million by 1988); prefixes; grammar transitive, intransitive and linking verbs; writing personal letters and a community-forests essay; listening an interview with Maria Ivanova; speaking opinions; project an Environment Day speech.
- Unit 8 — Humour and Satire (pp. 71–78): Reading Robertson Davies's "A Few Kind Words for Superstition"; derivation and word origins; grammar present simple vs present continuous; writing an argumentative essay on superstition; listening emergency preparedness; speaking suggesting and advising; project superstition interviews written up as a news article.
- Unit 9 — Democracy and Human Rights (pp. 79–87): Reading a biography of Nelson Mandela and his Inauguration Speech (Pretoria, 10 May 1994); pronunciation of -d/-ed and -s/-es endings; grammar should / had better; writing a biography of a Nepali freedom fighter and a five-minute speech; listening an election campaign (Richard Cookson); speaking persuading; project a community-issues questionnaire and report.
- Unit 10 — Home Life and Family Relationship (pp. 88–98): Reading Kamala Das's "The Tattered Blanket" (transl. K. M. Sherrif) — Gopi returns after five years to sell his share; his senile mother does not know him and asks the "visitor" to tell her son to send a red blanket; spelling with suffixes and syllables; grammar connectives (but / however / although / despite); writing a diary entry (model: a COVID-19 lockdown diary, Saturday 28 March 2020, "Makar"); listening a job interview; speaking reactions; project an article on senior citizens living alone.
- Unit 11 — Arts and Creation (pp. 99–108): Reading "Why Is the Renaissance Important?" by Milada Broukal (byline printed reversed, "Broukal Milada"); idioms and phrasal verbs; grammar relative clauses; writing essays; listening a BBC Forum on Leonardo (Bridget Kendall with Kemp, Bambach, Wallace); speaking describing people; project a profile of a Nepali artist.
- Unit 12 — Fantasy (pp. 109–117): Reading Lewis Carroll's "Down the Rabbit Hole" (Alice, ch. 1, through the EAT ME cake); minimal pairs /s/–/ʃ/ and /s/–/z/; grammar wish (I wish / If only); writing a folktale narration and a strange-dream description; listening an extract of Martin Luther King Jr.'s "I Have a Dream"; speaking regrets; project read a whole novel and write a short review.
- Unit 13 — Career and Entrepreneurship (pp. 118–129): Reading an interview with Mahabir Pun (Nangi wireless networking and telemedicine; the National Innovation Centre, est. 9 Dec 2012, inaugurated 18 Oct 2019 at TU Kirtipur); irregular plurals; grammar present perfect vs past simple (with been / gone); writing an informal email reply and a press release (model: the MoFA Gyawali–Pompeo call, 24 June 2020); listening a disabled woman's narration; speaking experiences (Have you ever…?); project interview a local entrepreneur.
- Unit 14 — Power and Politics (pp. 130–136): Reading "Napoleon Bonaparte" by Milada Broukal; compound noun phrases; grammar may / might / must / can't for deduction; writing an essay "Power and Politics" (~500 words) and paragraphs on an Anglo-Nepal War hero; listening an Obama speech; speaking certainty and probability; project a description of a Nepalese warrior.
- Unit 15 — War and Peace (pp. 137–144): Reading Hermann Hesse's "Shall there be Peace?" (WWI, Brest-Litovsk); vocabulary matching; grammar sentence functions (S-V-O-C-A); writing ~500-word essays on war and peace; listening a highway-protest news report (reporter Chris); speaking encouraging and discouraging; project a picture story book.
- Unit 16 — Critical Thinking (pp. 145–151): Reading Bertrand Russell's "What Is the Soul?"; a crossword; grammar used to; writing critical pieces on God's existence and on religion and ethics; listening Jessica on her father; speaking past vs present habits; project find Vivekananda's "Is the soul immortal?" online and review it critically.
- Unit 17 — Globalisation and Diaspora (pp. 152–161; contents title "Globalisation and Identity"): Reading Abdalhadi Alijla's "What Lost Identity? The Diaspora and Globalisation"; collocation; grammar infinitive and gerund; writing a news story (model: Rising Nepal on the Motihari–Amlekhgunj pipeline inauguration, 10 Sept 2019); listening a globalisation gap-fill; speaking obligations; project an NRNA article.
- Unit 18 — Immigration and Identity (pp. 162–171): Reading Jonathan Yardley's Washington Post review of Naipaul's Half a Life — Willie Chandran remakes himself in London, then in Portuguese Africa with Ana, and never finds a proper place; homophones and confused words; grammar reported speech (statements, questions, commands, backshift); writing book and film reviews in both formats; listening a spoken review of "Fun in the City"; speaking reporting speech; project interview someone living far from family and report on homesickness.
- Unit 19 — Travel and Tourism (pp. 172–180): Reading Megan Leung's "Discovering West Nepal – The Wild Frontier" — a five-week walk (Dhaulagiri Circuit, Mustang, Dolpa, Jumla to Rara) begun by taking the wrong Tatopani bus; consonant clusters /sk/ /sp/ /st/; grammar voice — active and passive, including get/have passives; writing a travelogue (~300 words) and an essay "Importance of Tourism in Nepal" (~500 words); listening an Arches National Park documentary; speaking compliments; project a brochure of a local site.
- Unit 20 — Science and Technology (pp. 181–191): Reading Christina Baker Kline's "Taking my Son to College, Where Technology has Replaced Serendipity" — her son Hayden starts at Yale; technology made him better prepared but may cost serendipitous discovery; commonly confused words (affect/effect, borrow/lend, raise/rise, say/tell, speak/talk, sensible/sensitive, principal/principle, accept/except, noise/sound, advice/advise); grammar articles; writing interpreting graphs and charts (model answer on Australian teenagers' fast food, sourced from ieltsbuddy.com, IELTS Academic Writing Task 1); listening a reconstructive-surgery documentary; speaking requesting and offering; project an oratory contest.

Section Two — Literature (divider p. 192). Every text carries an author introduction, glossary, Understanding the text, Reference to the context and Reference beyond the text.
Stories:
- "The Selfish Giant" — Oscar Wilde (pp. 193–199): the Giant bans children from his garden and winter stays; he helps a small boy up a tree and knocks down the wall; years later the boy returns with nail-wounds, "the wounds of Love", to take the dead Giant to Paradise.
- "The Oval Portrait" — Edgar Allan Poe (pp. 200–205): a wounded narrator in an Apennine chateau reads that the painter of a lifelike portrait drained his young wife's life into the canvas; she died as he finished it.
- "God Sees the Truth but Waits" — Leo Tolstoy (pp. 206–214): Aksionov, falsely convicted on a planted bloody knife, spends 26 years in Siberia; he will not betray Makar Semyonich's escape tunnel and forgives him after his confession; he dies just before the release order arrives.
- "The Wish" — Roald Dahl (pp. 215–219): a boy crosses the hall carpet treating red as hot coals, black as snakes, yellow as safe, to win a puppy; stuck in the splits, he falls and his hand goes into the black; his mother searches for him outside.
- "Civil Peace" — Chinua Achebe (pp. 220–226): Jonathan Iwegbu rebuilds after the Nigerian Civil War; his £20 ex-gratia "egg-rasher" is robbed the same night by thieves invoking "Civil Peace"; he shrugs: "Nothing puzzles God."
- "Two Little Soldiers" — Guy de Maupassant (pp. 227–234): Breton friends Jean Kerderen and Luc Le Ganidec share Sundays near Bezons; a dairymaid gives them milk; Luc secretly courts her on weekdays; Jean, heartbroken, leans from the bridge and drowns.
- "An Astrologer's Day" — R. K. Narayan (pp. 235–240): a fake astrologer who fled home believing he had killed Guru Nayak recognises him as a night client, tells him his enemy is dead and sends him north; he tells his wife the load is gone — the man is alive.
Poems:
- "Corona Says" — Vishnu S. Rai (pp. 241–244): the virus speaks — it was invited by man's conduct; the earth is not man's property alone; worse will come if ego stays.
- "A Red, Red Rose" — Robert Burns (pp. 245–247): love like a red rose and a melody, lasting till the seas run dry.
- "All the World's a Stage" — William Shakespeare (pp. 248–250): the seven ages of man, from infant to second childishness.
- "Who are you, little i?" — E. E. Cummings (p. 251–252): an adult recalls his five/six-year-old self watching a November sunset.
- "The Gift in Wartime" — Tran Mong Tu (pp. 253–255): a widow offers roses, her wedding gown and her youth to her dead soldier husband; he "gives" medals, blood and sightless eyes; she keeps shrapnel as a token to meet in the next life.
Essays:
- "Sharing Tradition" — Frank LaPena (pp. 256–260): oral tradition carries Native American culture across generations; elders' knowledge dies unless the young listen.
- "How to Live Before You Die" — Steve Jobs (pp. 261–267): the Stanford 2005 commencement address — connecting the dots; love and loss (fired from Apple); death as life's change agent; "Stay Hungry. Stay Foolish."
- "What I Require from Life" — J.B.S. Haldane (pp. 268–273): work, freedom, health and friendship for himself; socialism for society; Socrates' enviable death.
- "What is Poverty?" — Jo Goodwin Parker (pp. 274–279): a first-person account of rural American poverty — smell, dirt, tiredness, the shame of asking for help; $78 a month for four people.
- "Scientific Research is a Token of Humankind's Survival" — Vladimir Keilis-Borok (pp. 280–284): a scientist's real rewards are freedom and discovery; his seismic-wave theory helped the nuclear test-ban talks in Geneva; basic research guards civilisation.
Plays:
- "Trifles" — Susan Glaspell (pp. 285–301): Minnie Wright is held for strangling her husband John; the men miss the motive; Mrs Hale and Mrs Peters find the canary with its neck wrung, hidden in a pretty box, and hide it — "knot it".
- "A Sunny Morning" — Serafín and Joaquín Álvarez Quintero (pp. 302–315): elderly Doña Laura and Don Gonzalo bicker over a Madrid park bench, discover they are the separated lovers Laura Llorente and Gonzalo, each invent a heroic death for the other, and both choose not to reveal themselves.
- "Refund" — Fritz Karinthy (pp. 316–332): Wasserkopf demands his school fees back after 18 years; the staff conspire to pass his deliberate nonsense answers (the Thirty Years' War measured as "seven meters") until his exact-to-the-heller fee calculation — 6,450 crowns 50 heller — proves his mathematics, and he is thrown out.

### Grammar spine
Word classes → adjective/adverb formation and the position of adverbs → prepositional verbs and prepositions of direction → past simple → will vs be going to → subject-verb agreement → transitive, intransitive and linking verbs → present simple vs present continuous → should / had better → connectives (but, however, although, despite) → relative clauses → wish (I wish / If only) → present perfect vs past simple → may / might / must / can't (deduction) → sentence functions (S-V-O-C-A) → used to → infinitive and gerund → reported speech → voice (active/passive, get/have passives) → articles. Vocabulary work is a second spine: dictionary skills, homographs, -ed/-ing adjectives, suffixes (-ion/-ness), prefixes, derivation, -d/-s pronunciation, spelling with suffixes and syllables, idioms and phrasal verbs, minimal pairs, irregular plurals, compound noun phrases, crosswords, collocation, homophones and confused words, consonant clusters, and the confused-words set of Unit 20.

### Honest negatives
- Examination preparation: NONE. The whole book was checked: there are no SEE or NEB past papers, no model question sets, no exam strategy, no marking schemes. Examinations appear only incidentally — an IELTS-test speaking prompt (Unit 1), a listening prompt "for study and examination" (Unit 2), "after your exam" (Unit 5), "Susan has failed her exams" as a regret example (Unit 12), an IELTS Academic Writing Task 1 model answer borrowed from ieltsbuddy.com in Unit 20 solely to teach chart interpretation, and the fictional school re-examination inside the play "Refund" (literature, not preparation).
- No answer keys anywhere in the student's book.
- No teacher's guide for Grade 11 is held in the record, and none is referred to in the book.
- No ISBN is printed.
- Section One contains no poems, stories or plays as set literature — all literary texts are confined to Section Two; conversely Section Two teaches no grammar.
- No unit teaches essay-length argumentation about examinations, study skills for board exams, or timed writing practice.

### Slips and errors as printed
- Preface (unnumbered): a contributor is printed as "Passhupati Pandey" (double s). Contents (unnumbered): "Foaquin Alvarez Quintero" for Joaquín Álvarez Quintero.
- Unit 1: "reject prejudice based on cast, creed" for caste (p. 6); "Khyber Pukhtoon Khwa" (p. 5); the signature "MalalaYousafzai" run together (p. 7).
- Unit 2: Elbow's "The teacher less class" for teacher-less (p. 14).
- Unit 3: "Indeonesia" for Indonesia (p. 23).
- Unit 4: heading "Qi Shi Huangdi" (p. 32); "The Qin statesman Shang Yang (356-338 BCE) advocate total war" — wrong dates and a broken verb (p. 32); "Li Siu" for Li Si (pp. 33–34); a "Grand Canal" photo caption sits inside the Qin achievements discussion (p. 33) — the Grand Canal is Sui-era; the Warring States period is dated 426-221 BCE (p. 31; conventionally 475–221 BCE); homograph list "CONtest (n.): a game or event of completion" for competition (p. 36); garbled exercise sentence "I Rojina in town in a few days ago" (p. 39).
- Unit 5 epigraph attributed to "Helen Keler" for Helen Keller (p. 42).
- Unit 6: "their deceased sensitivity to insulin" for decreased (p. 56); "What do you do you to keep yourself fit?" (p. 59).
- Unit 7: "My filed is biology" for field (p. 65); the garbled phrase "the UN's "women' decade"" (p. 62).
- Unit 8 prints two vocabulary sections both labelled "C" (p. 74).
- Unit 10 diary tips: "the writer's personality nd explain their feelings" (p. 93).
- Unit 11: "when life gives you lemon" for lemons (p. 102); a garbled phrasal-verb matching table (p. 103).
- Unit 12: the /s/–/ʃ/ practice list includes the pair "sit/shit" as printed (p. 114).
- Unit 13: the irregular-plural list contains "fungus" twice (p. 123); a speaking dialogue spells "Michale" (p. 128).
- Unit 14: the Napoleon text claims he "wrote the Code's original 2,281 articles himself" and that the laws of the time were all "written in Latin" (p. 131); calls him "one of fourteen children" (p. 130); "Perhaps Napoleon would have an even greater ruler" — "been" missing (p. 132).
- Unit 16: Russell's essay prints "if the sun grew could" for cold (p. 147).
- Unit 18: "a "big light-eyed man" man" — word duplicated (p. 163); "the central issue Naipal has raised" for Naipaul (p. 166); exercise prompts with "depicted int he novel", "the similaries", "in the noivel" (p. 166).
- Unit 19: the speaking section prints "complements" for compliments throughout (p. 180).
- Literature: the Shakespeare introduction places the "All the World's a Stage" monologue in "Act II Scene VI" of As You Like It — it is Act II, Scene VII (p. 248); the Tolstoy glossary defines "knot" as a string fastening although the story's "flogged with a knot" is the knout, a whip (pp. 213–214); the Achebe glossary garbles the gloss of "Na tief-man… hopen de door" as "I am a thief of my accomplices" and prints "destitute… life' poverty-stricken" with a stray apostrophe (p. 225); the "Refund" comprehension questions ask about "Waserkopf", missing an s (p. 331).

### Copy completeness and cross-check
- Folio run: printed pp. 3–332 continuous; no missing or duplicated leaves observed in this copy.
- Contents vs actual: the start pages in the Contents match the units as printed, BUT the Contents table itself is column-jumbled and misassigns several grammar/writing items against the units that actually teach them — relative clauses are actually Unit 11 (contents implies Unit 17); "wish" is actually Unit 12's grammar (contents says infinitive and gerund); present perfect vs past simple is actually Unit 13 (contents Unit 16); may/might/must/can't is actually Unit 14 (contents Unit 15); infinitive and gerund is actually Unit 17; "used to" is actually Unit 16 (contents Unit 10); reported speech is actually Unit 18 (contents Unit 20); voice is actually Unit 19 (contents Unit 18); articles are actually Unit 20 (contents Unit 19); and "The Tattered Blanket" is Unit 10's reading, not Unit 9's. All summaries above follow the units as actually printed.
- Cross-check: no teacher's guide for Grade 11 exists in the record, so no companion cross-check is possible. The book's own claim to follow the Secondary Level Curriculum 2076 (Grades 11–12) is consistent with the Grade 12 volume's preface, which cites the same framework.

## Grade 12 — English Grade 12 (student's book)

### Identity block
- Title as printed: the cover (pre-verified) reads "English Grade 12"; the title page (PDF p. 1) reads "English / Grade 12", Government of Nepal, Ministry of Education, Science and Technology, Curriculum Development Centre, Sanothimi, Bhaktapur. The record's wording "English Grade Twelve (Compulsory)" does not match the book: the word "Compulsory" appears nowhere in the book's title, cover, title page or imprint. The book's own title is simply "English Grade 12".
- Publisher: Curriculum Development Centre (CDC), Sanothimi, Bhaktapur.
- Year/edition/ISBN as printed: First Edition: 2021 AD (imprint, PDF p. 2). The imprint prints a "Price:" line with no price filled in; it names the printer, Janak Education Materials Centre Ltd., Sanothimi, Bhaktapur. No ISBN is printed anywhere in the book.
- Developers as printed (Preface, PDF p. 3, dated 2021): compiled and prepared by Dr. Bam Dev Adhikari, Khum Prasad Sharma, Bal Bahadur Thapa; extensively improved and edited by Prof. Dr. Jib Lal Sapkota, Prof. Dr. Rishi Ram Rijal, Dr. Gopal Prasad Pandey, Nim Prakash Singh Rathaur, Shankar Adhikari, Nabin Kumar Khadka; contributions from Director General Ana Prasad Neupane, Director Tuka Raj Adhikari, Madhav Prasad Ghimire, Maiya Pokharel; revision and tasks by Ananda Dhungana, Lal Mani Joshi, Matrika Subedi, Ramesh Dhakal, Pashupati Pandya; illustration Shree Hari Shrestha; layout Khados Sunuwar.
- Curriculum/series notes: the Preface states the book follows the National Curriculum Framework 2076 and the Secondary Level Curriculum 2076 for Grades 11–12 — the same framework cited in the Grade 11 volume. Two sections: Section One, language development (20 thematic units, each with reading, vocabulary/pronunciation, writing, grammar, listening, speaking and a project), and Section Two, literature (short stories, poems, essays, one-act plays).
- Extent: 308 PDF pages = title page (PDF 1) + imprint (PDF 2) + Preface (PDF 3) + blank page (PDF 4) + Contents (PDF 5–8) + printed pp. 1–300 (PDF 9–308).
- Printed↔PDF offset: printed page = PDF page − 8. Established from the Section One divider (PDF p. 9), which itself carries printed folio 1, with printed p. 2 on PDF p. 10, and confirmed by the running folio "English: Grade 12" through the last printed page, 300, on PDF p. 308.

### Unit-by-unit summary
Section One — Language Development
- Unit 1 — Critical Thinking (pp. 1–8): Reading "Know Thyself", an extract from R. J. Palacio's Wonder — Auggie in Mr. Browne's English class and the September precept, "WHEN GIVEN THE CHOICE BETWEEN BEING RIGHT OR BEING KIND, CHOOSE KIND"; definitions of convergent, divergent, critical and creative thinking; grammar question tags; writing an email about an interesting class; listening a motivational talk; speaking expressing disappointment; project interview Grade XI students.
- Unit 2 — Family (pp. 9–16): Reading Alan John Barnard's encyclopedia-style text on the family (nuclear/extended families, patriarchy, industrialisation, family law, marriage, children, succession); prefixes; grammar modal verbs; writing essays on the family and on nuclear vs extended families; listening a woman on living alone vs Chinese family values; speaking arguing and defending a point; project the story of an abandoned elderly couple or widow(er).
- Unit 3 — Sports (pp. 17–26): Reading the AP news story "Italy wins Euro 2020, beats England in penalty shoot-out" (London, July 12); word origins and cricket terms; pronunciation /aɪ/ vs /eɪ/; grammar determiners and quantifiers; writing a punctuation exercise, a news story from notes (Argentina–Brazil Copa America) and a ~500-word argument essay on hosting international sports events; listening a radio programme on new sports (wakeboarding, roller derby, Wakestock); speaking asking for and giving reasons; project interview a famous player.
- Unit 4 — Technology (pp. 27–35): Reading a "Hyperloop" explainer (Musk's Hyperloop Alpha 2013, Goddard's vactrain, the Crystal Palace Pneumatic Railway 1864); prefixes hyper- / ultra- / up- / over- / multi-; grammar subject-verb agreement; writing a press release (Bahamasair model, rewritten for Nepal Airlines); listening space-exploration news; speaking summarising; project a report on traditional technologies.
- Unit 5 — Education (pp. 36–43): Reading "A Story of My Childhood" by A. P. J. Abdul Kalam (from My Life: An Illustrated Autobiography) — a teacher brings his exam results home; Arabic tuition and a maths class before school; a new teacher separates him from his Brahmin friend Ramanadha Sastry; Sivasubramania Iyer's seashore lesson on how birds fly sets his career; life-story genre words and intonation; grammar connectives (although / despite); writing a short autobiography; listening a talk on education as learning to use ourselves; speaking degrees of certainty; project an oratory contest.
- Unit 6 — Money and Economy (pp. 44–52): Reading a report-style text on QR code payments (ISO/IEC 18004, EMVCo 2017, Alipay/WeChat Pay, mVisa/Masterpass QR, Singapore's open loop, bKash, EcoCash; security); abbreviations; pronunciation /ʊ/ vs /uː/; grammar questions (types of questions); writing a punctuation exercise and a news article on digital payments in Nepal; listening a talk on barter and the history of money; speaking expressing necessity.
- Unit 7 — Humour (pp. 53–60): Reading David Robson's article on Sophie Scott's laughter research, "Why do We Laugh Inappropriately?"; synonyms and antonyms of laugh; syllables; grammar adverbs (frequency adverbs); listening a talk on anger and mindfulness; speaking expressing feelings, emotions and attitudes; project mimicry of a TV comedy character.
- Unit 8 — Human Culture (pp. 61–68): Reading James Fallows's "Land of Plenty" — Japanese sodaigomi bulky-garbage nights; his American family in Yokohama furnishes its house from the piles, then gives back; suffixes and word formation; waste-management vocabulary; grammar adjectives and adverbs (comparison); writing a paragraph on the 3Rs and a letter to the editor on garbage problems; listening a waste-management audio (Helsinki); speaking making comparisons and contrasts; project a report on a second-hand shop visit.
- Unit 9 — Ecology and Environment (pp. 69–78): Reading Grace Wyatt's "Living in a Redwood Tree" — Julia Butterfly Hill's 738 days in the redwood Luna (10 Dec 1997–18 Dec 1999), ending in an agreement saving Luna; compound words; ecology terms; pronunciation /e/ vs /eɪ/; grammar reported speech; writing a book/film review and drafting a speech ("Let's save the trees…"); listening an interview with Julia Butterfly Hill; speaking reporting; project a community-forest report.
- Unit 10 — Career Opportunities (pp. 79–87): Reading "Presenting Yourself", a chapter on academic CVs by Rebecca Boden, Debbie Epstein & Jane Kenway ('staying' vs 'leaving' CVs; academic vs two-page non-academic CVs); employment-related terms; pronunciation /ɪə/ vs /eə/; grammar conditional sentences; writing a job application and a CV for an MM Construction Pvt. Ltd. advertisement; listening a job interview; speaking clarifying and a mock job interview; project interview someone who passed a Public Service Commission / Teacher Service Commission examination, for interview tips.
- Unit 11 — Hobbies (pp. 88–96): Reading Sristi Bhattarai's "On Walking" — a personal essay (Kathmandu childhood, Sydney school years, solitary Minneapolis years; walking as grounding in every place); grammar passive voice; writing an essay on the advantages of a morning walk (with a Thoreau quotation); listening walking vs running; speaking reminding; project interview classmates about their hobbies.
- Unit 12 — Animal World (pp. 97–103): Reading Lewis Thomas's "The Medusa and the Snail" — selfhood in nature; the Bay of Naples nudibranch–medusa pair that can only live as each other's "specific others"; grammar passive voice again (the second consecutive passive unit); writing an essay "Independence vs Interdependence" (~250 words) and a newspaper article on individualism in Nepali society; listening a conversation about a book; speaking expressing counter arguments; project field observation of interdependence.
- Unit 13 — History (pp. 104–113): Reading Amitav Ghosh's "After the World Trade Centre" — WTC construction manager Frank De Martini stayed on the 88th floor on 9/11 helping others evacuate and died; his wife Nicole escaped; defining professionals; vowel sounds; grammar prepositions and future tenses (simple, continuous, perfect, perfect continuous); writing a description of an event and a ~500-word essay, "An Unforgettable Event in my Life"; listening a 9/11 presidential address (George W. Bush); speaking speculation; project a library report on a deadly event in Nepal's history.
- Unit 14 — Human Rights (pp. 114–121): Reading Desmond Tutu's "'I am Sorry' — The Hardest Three Words to Say" (from The Book of Forgiving) — a childhood under an abusive father, apartheid and Bantu Education, children schooled in Swaziland, his father's death before a last talk; forgiveness as self-interest; suffix word-formation; grammar connectives (when / while; because / so / since / due to); writing paragraphs on equal education in Nepal; listening an interview about Nelson Mandela; speaking criticising (should have…); project a paragraph on discrimination witnessed or experienced.
- Unit 15 — Leisure and Entertainment (pp. 122–129): Reading Anand P. Shrestha's "A Journey Back in Time!" — a weekend trip to Bandipur, a living museum of Newar culture three hours from Kathmandu; a crossword and content words; grammar "Miscellaneous agreements" (miscellaneous usage and agreement points); writing a business letter proposing a tourism venture; listening a holiday-planning conversation; speaking expressing indifference; project a group travel report.
- Unit 16 — Fantasy (pp. 130–139): Reading O. Henry's "The Romance of a Busy Broker" — broker Harvey Maxwell, absorbed in business, proposes to his stenographer Miss Leslie, forgetting they were married the previous evening; stock-market terms (NEPSE etc.); pronunciation odd-one-out; grammar relative clauses; writing summary writing, with a model text on Nepal labour migration and its printed model summary; listening a stock-market talk (Dutch East India Company); speaking describing a process; project a NEPSE one-day stock report.
- Unit 17 — War and Peace (pp. 140–149): Reading an excerpt from Khushwant Singh's Train to Pakistan — Iqbal's overcrowded train journey to Mano Majra; Meet Singh reports Jugga's dacoity and murder; the village code of loyalty against Iqbal's "criminals are not born, they are made"; Hindi loanwords in English; suffixes with e-retention; the vowel chart (monophthongs/diphthongs); grammar past tenses (simple, continuous, perfect, perfect continuous); writing a descriptive paragraph and a letter about an unforgettable journey; listening a Partition oral-history interview; speaking making announcements; project a report on Nepal's role in world peace-building.
- Unit 18 — Music and Creation (pp. 150–159): Reading Ryan J. Huxtable's "A Life of Sound and Silence", a medical-humanities biography of Beethoven's deafness (tinnitus, hyperacusis, the Heiligenstadt Testament, ear trumpets, conversation books; died 26 March 1827); musical-instrument categories; word stress; grammar prepositions again (cf. Unit 13); writing a ~300-word biography of a favourite Nepali musician; listening a Michael Jackson biography; speaking predicting (a Will Smith press-interview role play); project a group oral presentation and a biography of a favourite singer.
- Unit 19 — Migration and Diaspora (pp. 160–169): Reading Michel S. Laguerre's "Dediasporization: Homeland and Hostland" — an academic essay on the return and reincorporation of diasporas (roles of the individual, the state, society; Israel, Haiti, Mexico, Russian-German and Caribbean-Chinese cases); the consonant-sounds chart, syllables and stress; grammar would / used to; writing interpreting charts and graphs (including Nepali student migration); listening a history of US immigration policy (1882 Chinese Exclusion to 1990 H-1B); speaking narrating past events; project interview a returnee from abroad.
- Unit 20 — Power and Politics (pp. 170–179): Reading Audre Lorde's "An Open Letter to Mary Daly" (1979) — charging that Gyn/Ecology uses Black women only as victims and quotes Lorde only to head the genital-mutilation chapter; a word-class chart; consonant clusters; grammar adjective order; writing a short account of discrimination witnessed and an article, "The Status of Women in Nepali Society"; listening Lee Kuan Yew's National Day Rally speech; speaking denying (polite refusals); project a debate competition.

Section Two — Literature. Every text carries Before reading, an author introduction, the text, a Glossary, Understanding the text, Reference to the context and Reference beyond the text.
Short Stories:
- "Neighbours" — Tim Winton (pp. 180–183): a young Australian couple in a multicultural migrant suburb; suspicion melts through garden help, a rebuilt henhouse and shared duck-slaughtering; the whole street cheers their baby's home birth — "The twentieth century novel had not prepared him for this."
- "A Respectable Woman" — Kate Chopin (pp. 184–188): Mrs. Baroda, unsettled by her attraction to her husband Gaston's quiet friend Gouvernail, flees to the city until he leaves; a year later she herself proposes inviting him again — "I have overcome everything!"
- "A Devoted Son" — Anita Desai (pp. 189–198): Rakesh tops the medical lists, returns from the USA and becomes the town's richest doctor, and rules his old father Varma's diet so strictly — weighed cereal, no fried food, endless pills — that the father begs to be let die and smashes the tonic bottle.
- "The Treasure in the Forest" — H. G. Wells (pp. 199–207): Evans and Hooker follow Chang-hi's map to buried Spanish gold, find a dead Chinaman beside the hole, and are themselves killed by the poisoned thorns planted among the ingots.
- "My Old Home" — Lu Xun (pp. 208–217): the narrator returns after more than twenty years to sell the family compound; his childhood friend Runtu, once a vivid boy with a silver neck ring, is now a crushed, formal peasant who calls him "Master"; he leaves hoping the next generation will not be walled off — hope is like a path made by walking.
- "The Half-closed Eyes of the Buddha and the Slowly Sinking Sun" — Shankar Lamichhane (pp. 218–223): a stream-of-consciousness double monologue — a Western tourist rhapsodises over Nepal's eyes, smiles and spirituality; the guide answers by showing him the eyes of a paralysed village child who can neither give nor take, the real Nepal behind the romance.
- "A Very Old Man with Enormous Wings" — Gabriel García Márquez (pp. 224–231): Pelayo and Elisenda keep the fallen "angel" in a chicken coop, charge five cents admission and grow rich; a spider-woman show steals the crowd; years later he grows new feathers and flies away while Elisenda watches, relieved.
Poems:
- "A Day" — Emily Dickinson (pp. 232–233): sunrise to sunset figured as a child's day, ending with a grey dominie leading the flock away.
- "Every Morning I Wake" — Dylan Thomas (pp. 234–235): a prayer from Under Milk Wood for the flawed inhabitants "born to die".
- "I Was My Own Route" — Julia de Burgos (pp. 236–238): the speaker refuses to walk backwards to the life men scripted for her and takes her own forward route.
- "The Awakening Age" — Ben Okri (pp. 239–240): a sonnet-like call for solidarity among the world's peoples, "richly linked in hope" like a mountain rope.
- "Soft Storm" — Abhi Subedi (pp. 241–245): the speaker "became soft" again and again before Kathmandu's tumult — homeless children in Thamel, a beaten man, a lost child in the corridors of violent history.
Essays:
- "On Libraries" — Oliver Sacks (pp. 247–251): a memoir in praise of libraries — his father's oak-panelled library, Willesden Public Library, the Bodleian and the Queen's College vaults, and grief at libraries discarding physical books in the 1990s.
- "Marriage as a Social Institution" — Stephen L. Nock (pp. 252–256): marriage as an institution with soft boundaries and a template that cohabiting couples lack; Durkheim on marriage restraining men's desires; marriage as social capital (Coleman); the six dimensions of normative marriage in America.
- "Knowledge and Wisdom" — Bertrand Russell (pp. 257–260): wisdom as a sense of proportion, comprehensiveness, awareness of the ends of life, and emancipation from the tyranny of the here and now; knowledge without wisdom is dangerous; wisdom can and should be taught.
- "Humility" — Yuval Noah Harari (pp. 261–264): "You are not the centre of the world" — every nation claims history began with it (Greeks, Chinese, Hindu nativists, Muslims, Jews); morality, art, spirituality and creativity are universal Stone Age African inheritances; he punctures his own people's egotism by example.
- "Human Rights and the Age of Inequality" — Samuel Moyn (pp. 265–271): the Croesus parable — human rights secure a floor of protection but no ceiling on inequality; the national-welfare era paired floor and ceiling; today's movement cannot stigmatise inequality; "Croesus's world is our common fate."
One-act Plays:
- "A Matter of Husbands" — Ferenc Molnar, translated by Benjamin Glazer (pp. 272–278): an Earnest Young Woman begs the Famous Actress to give back her husband Alfred, the actress's manager's lawyer; the Actress convinces her the affair was Alfred's invention to make her jealous; the wife leaves happy — and the Actress calls Alfred out of her boudoir, where he has been hiding: they are lovers. (Title as printed in the play's own header; the contents and the introduction prose both call it "A Matter for Husband" / "A Matter of Husband".)
- "Facing Death" — August Strindberg (pp. 279–292): bankrupt pension-owner Monsieur Durand, despised by his three daughters — poisoned against him by their dead mother's lies — has set a timed candle to burn the pension so the girls will get the fire-insurance money, and poisons himself; he dies as the fire starts, having told Adèle the truth (he never deserted; his wife ruined both inheritances) and charged her to care for her sisters.
- "The Bull" — Bhimnidhi Tiwari (pp. 293–300): bull-doctor Laxminarayan, threatened with having his head shaved if the king's favourite bull dies, hides the death from King Ranabahadur and stages the corpse at Thulo Gauchar as merely ill; the cowherds Jitman and Gore perform grief so well that the king, once he sees the bull is dead, gives them tips of 400 and 500 rupees instead of beheading them — a satire on the feudal practice of chakari.

### Grammar spine
Question tags → modal verbs → determiners and quantifiers → subject-verb agreement → connectives (although / despite) → questions (types of questions) → adverbs (frequency adverbs) → adjectives and adverbs (comparison) → reported speech → conditional sentences → passive voice → passive voice (repeated, with new practice) → prepositions and future tenses → connectives (when / while; reason connectives because / so / since / due to) → miscellaneous agreements → relative clauses → past tenses (simple, continuous, perfect, perfect continuous) → prepositions (repeated) → would / used to → adjective order. Two points are taught twice rather than once: passive voice in Units 11 and 12, and prepositions in Units 13 and 18.

### Honest negatives
- Examination preparation: NONE. The whole book was checked: there are no SEE or NEB past papers, no model question sets, no exam strategy, tips or marking schemes. Examinations appear only incidentally — "You must read …of these books for the exam" inside a determiners exercise (Unit 3); "He could not score goods grades in the SEE exams in spite of…" as a connectives completion item (p. 42); "Unless you follow the instructions, you…pass the exams" in the conditionals unit (Unit 10); the school exam results reported in Kalam's childhood narrative (Unit 5 reading — story, not preparation); Unit 10's project of interviewing a Public Service Commission / Teacher Service Commission passer, which is job-entry career guidance, not board-exam preparation; "A student can't do well in English exam…" as a speaking situation (Unit 15); and a model narration about a farewell for students "who had just completed SEE" (Unit 19).
- No answer keys for any exercise; the questions in the literature apparatus are set, not answered.
- No teacher's guide for Grade 12 is held in the record, and none is referred to in the book.
- No ISBN is printed; the imprint's price line is blank.
- Despite the record's "(Compulsory)" wording, the book nowhere styles itself "Compulsory English" or similar.
- No whole-book glossary or index: vocabulary support is per-unit word work and per-text glossaries in Section Two only.

### Slips and errors as printed
- Imprint (unnumbered): "without the written permission of the pubisher" for publisher; "not-profit making purposes" for non-profit.
- Unit 5: the friend's name is spelled "Ramanadha Sastry" and "Ramanadhan" inconsistently (pp. 37–38); comprehension ordering item "his Bramhin friend" for Brahmin (p. 40); grammar example "In spite of her hard labout" for labour (p. 41); "He could not score goods grades" for good (p. 42); speaking rubric "Have conversions in the following situations" for conversations (p. 43); model dialogue "We've meeting tomorrow at 9:00 am" (p. 43).
- Unit 8: exercise sentence "…grows especially in California and Oregan" for Oregon (p. 72); the listening true/false statement prints "the trash tanks in Helsinki are special because they use censors" for sensors (p. 67).
- Unit 12: Lewis Thomas's text prints "this kind of nude branch" for nudibranch (p. 99) and "a yeast cell, say, say, and a chicken erythrocyte" with "say" duplicated (p. 98).
- Unit 13: grammar example "Fort Green Park" for Fort Greene (p. 108); "Franks' office" (p. 105).
- Unit 15: the listening true/false items skip the letter h (g is followed by i) (p. 128).
- Unit 16: the printed model summary says "people have started to migrate to Golf countries" for Gulf (p. 136); a relative-clause study sentence quotes the reading as "I snatched a minute when things had slackened a minute" where the text reads "slackened up a bit" (p. 137).
- Unit 18: the reading opens "Over twenty-three million Americans suffer from deafness" but the grammar section quotes the same sentence as "Over seventy-three million Americans" (p. 156) — the two figures contradict each other; "comnmented" for commented (p. 152); "Johann NepomukMälzel" and "MissaSolemnis" run together (p. 153); comprehension set B begins at item "b." with no item a (p. 155); "Hellen Keller" for Helen (p. 155); "Stephen von Breeuning" for Breuning (p. 155).
- Unit 19: a listening true/false item dates the end of nation-based quotas to 1960, while the same unit's matching exercise dates the Immigration and Nationality Act to 1965; the Cuban item likewise says 1960 against the 1966 Cuban Adjustment Act (p. 168).
- Stories: Chopin introduction "Kate Chopin (18511904)" — the dash is missing (p. 184); the same introduction prints "'Disiree's Baby'" for Désirée's Baby (p. 184); glossary "mabelle (adj.)" for "ma belle" (p. 187); Desai introduction "She received Shahitya Academy Award" for Sahitya (p. 189) and lists "Feasting, Feasting (1999)" for Fasting, Feasting (p. 189); "My Old Home" glossary labels "flabbergasted (ad.)" (p. 216) and the text runs "missShuisheng" together (p. 215); Lamichhane's text prints "discuss Bhrikutl's dowry" for Bhrikuti (p. 220); the Márquez exercises repeatedly spell "Palayo" for Pelayo (p. 230), while the story text uses Pelayo; story text "the pentinents brought him" for penitents (p. 226), "rent the sky in tow" for in two (p. 227), "have up his job as a bailiff" for gave up (p. 228).
- Poems: Dylan Thomas introduction "(19141953)" — dash missing — and "Englnad" for England (p. 234); the play title is printed "Under Milk Wood" in the introduction but "Under the Milk Wood" in the glossary (p. 235) — the correct title is Under Milk Wood; the Ben Okri introduction gives his birth year as 1949 — he was born in 1959 (p. 239); Subedi questions print "children potrayed" for portrayed (p. 245) and "anomolous expressions" for anomalous (p. 246).
- Essays: Sacks glossary "helt (v.): to lift or carry" for heft (p. 250); "Gesner's HistoriaeAnimalium" run together (p. 249); the Reference-to-the-context question misquotes Sacks as "I was not a good pupil, but I was a good listener" — the essay says "good learner" (essay p. 248; question p. 251); Nock glossary defines "bolster (n.): a thick pillow shaped like a long tube", although the text uses the verb ("Laws, religion, and customs bolster this contract") (p. 255); Russell's text prints "emancipation, as fat as possible" for far (p. 259) and the questions spell his name "Russel" (p. 260); Harari's Before reading questions run a. then c. — there is no b. (p. 261); Moyn glossary "enumenical" for ecumenical (p. 270) and labels "egalitarianism (adj.)" though it is a noun (p. 270).
- Plays: the Molnar introduction prints "Lilion (1909)" for Liliom (p. 272) and a stray "Unit 4" header stands above the play (p. 272); the play's title is printed three ways — contents "A Matter for Husband", introduction prose "A Matter of Husband", play header "A Matter of Husbands"; the Molnar glossary mislabels word classes: "appraisingly (n.)", "contritely (adj.)", "impetuously (adj.)" (p. 277); Strindberg's Before reading asks "Have you ever observe your parents" for observed (p. 279); the Facing Death character list gives both Annette and Thérèse as "twenty-four" (p. 279); the Facing Death questions spell "Monsieur Duran" (missing d) and "Theresa" throughout (pp. 291–292); its glossary's account of the sou is muddled ("100 sou coin is equal to five Swiss franc coin") (p. 290); in The Bull, stage directions misspell the king's name as "Ranabahdur" and "Raabahadur" (pp. 295–297) and a cowherd as "Jitmman" (p. 297); The Bull glossary: "ana (n.): twenty five paisa, one fourth of a rupee" — an anna is one-sixteenth of a rupee, not one fourth (p. 299); "equal to3.2 kg" with the space missing (p. 299).

### Copy completeness and cross-check
- Folio run: printed pp. 1–300 continuous; no missing or duplicated leaves observed in this copy.
- Contents vs actual: the Section Two contents (PDF p. 8) is clean and matches the literature as printed. The Section One contents (PDF pp. 5–7) is a graphical, jumbled table and does NOT reliably match the units: it places "A Story of My Childhood" under Human Culture and "QR Code" under Education, whereas the Kalam childhood story is actually the reading of Unit 5 (Education) and the QR Code text is the reading of Unit 6 (Money and Economy); and its theme order from about Unit 14 onward is scrambled against the actual units (contents: Unit 14 Fantasy / "Train to Pakistan", Unit 15 War and Peace / "Romance of a Busy Broker", Unit 16 Human Rights / "I am Sorry", Unit 18 Power and Politics, Unit 20 Music and Creation; actual: Unit 14 Human Rights, Unit 15 Leisure and Entertainment, Unit 16 Fantasy, Unit 18 Music and Creation, Unit 20 Power and Politics). All summaries above follow the units as actually printed.
- Cross-check: no teacher's guide for Grade 12 exists in the record, so no companion cross-check is possible. The record's title wording "(Compulsory)" is not supported by the book itself (see Identity block).
